我看见他紧跟着走下月台——那是我见他们两人的最后一眼。"
可想而知，我们是怀着怎样惊惧的兴味听完这番陈述的，它又引出了多少千头万绪的猜测。
"要找旁的证人来指认此人，想来不难。"我喊道。
詹姆斯却使我们确信：为寻此人的踪迹，能想的法子都想尽了。合乎那副相貌的人，村里没有住户；附近也无人见过这样一个人。车站和近旁旅馆一带闲荡的人里，没有一个记得见过这陌生汉子下车；旅馆没有这样一个人投宿；连列车员也说不准有这么位乘客——尽管他依稀记得莫尔兰先生那节车里有个粗汉——没留神他在哪儿下的车——记得他的票是到奥尔巴尼的。
"但我们还没对证据死心。"阿盖尔先生说。
"纽约的警察在此地无事可办，都回去了。"詹姆斯接着说。"这样一个恶棍只要藏身纽约，早晚揪得出来。手上那道疤是认人的好记号——您说是不是，先生？"这话是对伯顿先生说的。
"唔——是啊！除非那疤是特意做出来的。兴许是拿赭石涂的，事后一洗了之。看那一刀的利落精准，这家伙若是个老手，这些花招他样样精通。他若真有疤，办这号差事时必戴手套。"
"您这么想？"詹姆斯长吸一口气，多半是被这番新论断泄了气。
"我想去车站和沿河码头走一个钟头。"伯顿先生续道，"眼下若没有别的急务的话。"
詹姆斯殷勤地执意同行。
"你干吗又弄一个这些暗探上来？"他逮着头一个机会，压低嗓音问我。"这号人我们领教够了——腻透了！这个巴罗斯还是伯顿，随他姓什么，是他们里头顶讨厌的一个。自负的家伙——天生就是我讨厌的那一路。"
"你看错他了。他有才学，是位绅士。"
"那你去享受同他做伴吧。"他讥诮地回了一句。
饶是这样，上午巡查，詹姆斯仍赏光作陪。

===Original===

One sole fact the detective ascertained in the course of his two hours’ work. A fisherman had lost a small-boat during the storm of Saturday night. He had left it, fastened to its accustomed moorings, and, in the morning, found that the chain, which was old and rusty, had parted one of its links, probably by the extreme violence with which the wind had dashed the boat about. Mr. Burton had asked to see the remnant of the chain. It was still attached to the post around which it had been locked. An examination of the broken link showed that it was partly rusted away; but there were also marks upon it, as if a knife or chisel might have been used.
“I see my boy, Billy, a-tinkerin’ with it,” said the fisherman. “Like as not he’s been a-usin’ of it to whittle on. That boy breaks more knives’n his neck’s wuth. He’s goin’ on nine, now, and he’s had six jackknives in as many months.”
Mr. Burton stood, holding the chain in his hand, and looking up and down the river. His face glowed with a light which shone through from some inward fire. I, who had begun to watch his varying expressions with keen interest, saw that he was again becoming excited; but not in the same way as on that first evening of our meeting, when he grew so leonine.
He looked at the water and the sky, the fair shores and the dull dock, as if these mute witnesses were telling to him a tale which he read like a printed book. A few moments he stood thus in silence, his countenance illuminated by that wonderful intelligence. Then, saying that his researches were through with in this part of the village, we returned, almost in silence, to the office; for when this man was pondering the enigmas whose solution he was so certain to announce, sooner or later, he grew absorbed and taciturn.
Mr. Argyll made us go home with him to dinner. I knew that I should not see Eleanor; yet, even to be under the same roof with her, made me tremble. Mary, who was constantly in attendance upon her sister, would not appear at the table. She came down, for a moment, to greet me, and to thank me for my poor efforts. The dear child had changed some, like the rest of us. She could not look like anything but the rosebud which she was﻿—a fresh and pure young creature of sixteen summers﻿—a rosebud drenched in dew﻿—a little pale, with a quiver in her smile, and bright tears beading her eyelashes, ready, at any moment, to drop.

===Chinese===

侦探两个钟头的巡查，只查明了一件事实。有个渔夫，星期六夜里的风暴里丢了一条小船。他原先把船拴在惯常的泊位上，早晨一看，那条又老又锈的链子断开了一环——多半是风把船摔打得过分，才崩断的。伯顿先生要来链子的残段看了看。残段还锁在桩上。细验断口那一环，见它半是锈蚀断了；上面却另有几道痕迹，像动过刀子或凿子。
"我瞧见我家小子比利拿它鼓捣来着。"渔夫说。"八成是他削东西使的。这小子折的刀比他的命都多。眼瞅着九岁了，六个月里换了六把折刀。"
伯顿先生站着，手里握着链子，望望河的上游下游。他脸上透出一层光，像内里有火照出来。我已开始极有兴味地留意他变换的神色，看得出他又激动起来；但同我们初见那晚的激动不同，那一回他发的是狮子般的威。
他望着水和天，望着明媚的两岸和灰暗的码头，仿佛这些无言的见证正在对他讲一个故事，而他读它如读一册印好的书。他这样默默立了片刻，满面焕发着那种异样的灵慧。随后他说，村子这一片的查勘可以收了；我们几乎一路无言地回到事务所；因为这个人一旦沉入那些谜团——那些他笃信迟早要亲手揭晓的谜团——便变得浑然忘我，缄默不语。
阿盖尔先生拉我们回家吃饭。我自知见不着埃莉诺；可哪怕同她共一处屋檐，也叫我发颤。玛丽寸步不离地守着姐姐，不肯上桌。她下来了一小会儿，同我见礼，谢我那点微不足道的奔走。亲爱的孩子同我们大家一样，也变了些。她怎么看都还是她本来的那朵玫瑰花苞——十六个夏天、清新纯洁的小人儿——一苞浸透露水的玫瑰——只是有点苍白，笑里带一丝颤，亮亮的泪珠挂在睫毛上，随时要落下来。

===Original===

It was touching to see one naturally so joyous, subdued by the shadow which had fallen over the house. Neither of us could say much; our lips trembled when we spoke *her* name; so, after a moment’s holding my hand, while the tears began to flow fast, Mary unclasped my fingers, and went upstairs. I saw Mr. Burton hide those blue-gray eyes of his in his handkerchief; my respect for him deepened as I felt that those eyes, sharp and penetrating as they were, were not too cold to warm with a sudden mist at the vision he had beheld.
“Ah!” murmured I to myself, “if he could see Eleanor!”
When dinner was over, Mr. Argyll went up to see his children, giving me permission to show the house and grounds to the detective. James went on the portico to smoke a cigar. Mr. Burton sat a short time in the library, taking an impression of it on his mind, examined the lock of the desk, and noticed the arrangement of the one window, which was a large bay-window opening to the floor and projecting over the flower-garden which lay behind the house and bordered the lawn to the right. It was about three feet to the ground, and although quite accessible, as a mode of entrance, to anyone compelled to that resource, the window was not ordinarily used as a mode of ingress or egress. I had sometimes chased Mary, when she was not so old as now, and sent her flying through the open casement into the mignonette and violets beneath, and I after; but since we had both grown more sedate, such pranks were rare.
We then went out upon the lawn. I took my companion to the tree beneath which I had stood, when that dark figure had approached, and passed me, to crouch beneath the window from which the death-candles shone. From this spot, the bay-window was not visible, that being at the back of the house and this on the side. Mr. Burton looked carefully about him, walking all over the lawn, going up under the parlor windows, and thence pursuing his way into the garden and around to the bay-window. It was quite natural to search closely in this precinct for some mark or footsteps, some crushed flowers, or broken branches, or scratches upon the wall, left by the thief, if he or she had made his or her entrance at this spot. Going over the ground thus, inch by inch, I observed a bit of white lawn, soiled and weather-beaten, lying under a rosebush a few feet from the window. I picked it up.

===Chinese===

眼见一个天性如此欢快的人，被笼罩全家的阴影压得这样安静，真叫人心酸。我们俩都说不出多少话；一提到*她*的名字，唇就发颤；于是玛丽握了我的手握了片刻，泪珠开始急急滚落，她掰开我的手指，上楼去了。我看见伯顿先生把那双蓝灰色的眼睛埋进手绢；我对他的敬意又深了一层——那双眼睛那样锋利、那样洞穿，却并不因之冷到不能为他目睹的这一幕温起一层薄雾。
"啊！"我暗自喃喃，"他若能见到埃莉诺该多好！"
饭罢，阿盖尔先生上楼看孩子们去了，吩咐我领侦探看看宅子和园子。詹姆斯到门廊上抽雪茄去了。伯顿先生在书房坐了一小会儿，把整间屋印进脑海，验看了书桌的锁，又察看了那扇独窗的格局——那是一扇落地的大凸窗，探出在宅后的花园上，园子右边连着草坪。窗离地约三英尺，对被迫出此一途的人来说，倒也不难翻越；但这窗平日并不作出入的道。玛丽年幼时，我追过她几回，把她从敞开的窗扇里赶进窗下的木樨草和紫罗兰里，我跟在后头；如今两人都稳重了，这种顽皮少有了。
随后我们走到草坪上。我把同伴领到那棵树下——那夜我就站在树下，一个黑影趋近我、掠过我，去蹲伏在守灵烛光透出的窗下。从这个位置望不见那扇凸窗：窗在宅后，这里在宅侧。伯顿先生细细环顾，走遍草坪，踱到客厅窗下，又循路进花园，绕到凸窗跟前。在这一带仔细搜寻窃贼出入留下的痕迹——脚印、压折的花、断枝或墙上的划痕——原是极自然的事。就这样一寸一寸搜过地面，我瞥见窗边几英尺外一丛玫瑰底下，躺着一块脏污的、经了风霜的白细麻布。我拾了起来。

===Original===

It was a woman’s handkerchief, of fine lawn, embroidered along the edge with a delicate running vine, and a spray of flowers at the corner.
“One of the young ladies has dropped it, some time ago,” I said, “or it has blown across from the kitchen grass-plot, where the linen is put out to dry.”
Then I examined the discolored article more closely, and, involved in the graceful twinings of the spray of flowers, I saw worked the initials﻿—“L. S.”
“Leesy Sullivan,” said my companion, taking it from my hand.
“It seems too dainty an article for her ownership,” I said, at last, for, at first, I had been quite stupefied.
“A woman’s vanity will compass many things beyond her means. This thing she has embroidered with her own needle﻿—you remember, she is a proficient in the art.”
“Yes, I remember. She may have lost it Sunday night, during that visit which I observed; and the wind has blown it over into this spot.”
“You forget that there has been no rain since that night. This handkerchief has been beaten into the grass and earth by a violent rain. A thorn upon this bush has pulled it from her pocket as she passed, and the rain has set its mark upon it, to be used as a testimony against her.”
“The evidence seems to conflict. She can not be a man and woman both.”
“Why not?” was the quiet reply. “There may be a principal and an accomplice. A woman is a safer accomplice for a man than one of his own sex﻿—and vice versa.”
The face which I had seen, in its despair, the face of Leesy Sullivan, rose in my memory, full of passion, marked in every soft yet impressive lineament with slumbering power﻿—“such a nature,” I thought, “can be maddened into crime, but it will not consort with villainy.”
Mr. Burton put the handkerchief in the inside pocket of his coat, and we returned into the house. He inquired the names of the servants, none of whose initials corresponded with those we had found, nor could I recall any lady visitors of the family to whom the handkerchief might belong by virtue of its inscription. There was not the shadow of a doubt but that it had been the property of the sewing-girl. Some errand, secret and unlawful, had brought her to these grounds, and under this window. We now considered it proper to show the handkerchief to Mr. Argyll, and relate to him our grounds of suspicion against the girl. Mary and James were admitted to the council.

===Chinese===

那是一方女人的手绢，细麻纱质地，边缘绣着一道纤秀的蔓藤，一角还有一簇花。
"是哪位小姐从前掉的吧，"我说，"要么就是从厨房外晾衣的草地上刮过来的。"
随即我把这件变了色的东西细看，只见在那簇花优雅的缠枝之间，绣着两个起首字母——"L. S."
"丽西·沙利文。"我同伴说着，从我手里接了过去。
"这么件讲究东西，似乎不该是她的。"我终于说；起初我实在怔住了。
"女人的虚荣心，够得着许多她力所不及的东西。这一件是她亲手绣的——您记得吧，她是此道高手。"
"对，我记得。许是礼拜天夜里、我窥见她来访的那一趟丢的；风又把它刮到了这里。"
"您忘了，那夜之后没下过雨。这方手绢是被一场急雨打进草里、打进土里的。是这丛树上的刺，在她经过时从她口袋里勾下来的；雨又在上面落下了记号，好留作指证她的凭据。"
"证据似乎自相矛盾。她总不能既是男人又是女人。"
"为什么不能？"回答安安静静。"可以有正凶，也可以有帮凶。对一个男人来说，女帮凶比同性帮凶更稳妥——反过来也一样。"
我记忆里浮起那张绝望中的脸——丽西·沙利文的脸，满是激情，柔和而深刻的每一根线条都印着蛰伏的力量——"这样的天性，"我想，"可以被逼疯成罪，却不会同恶棍沆瀣一气。"
伯顿先生把手绢收进上衣内袋，我们回到宅内。他问了佣人们的姓名，没有一个的起首字母同手绢上的对得上；家里过往的女客，我也想不出哪一位凭这两个字母认领得了。这手绢曾属缝衣女所有，已无一丝疑义。必是某桩隐秘而不法的勾当，把她引到这园子里、引到这扇窗下。此刻我们认为，该把手绢拿给阿盖尔先生看，并把我们对那姑娘的疑点向他摊明。玛丽和詹姆斯也准与闻。

===Original===

The former said that she remembered Miss Sullivan; that she had been employed in the family, for a few days at a time, on several different occasions, but none of them recent. “We liked her sewing very much, and wanted to engage her for the next six weeks,” she added, with a sigh, “but on inquiring for her, learned that she was now employed in New York.”
“She must, then, have been perfectly familiar with the arrangement of the house, and with the habits of the family; as for instance, at what hour you dined. She might enter while the family were at table, since, had she been surprised by the entrance of a servant, or other person, she could affect to have called on an errand, and to be waiting for the young ladies,” remarked Mr. Burton.
The servants were then summoned, one at a time, and questioned as to whether they had observed any suspicious persons whatever about the house or grounds within a week. They were, of course, in a national state of high excitement, and immediately upon a question being put to them, answered every other imaginary case in the world but that, blessed themselves, called on the Virgin Mary, gave an account of all the beggars as called at the kitchen last year and the year afore, cried abundantly, and gave no coherent information.
“Ah, sure!” said Norah, the cook, “there was the blackin’-and-bluin’ man come around last Wednesday, and I tuk a bottle of the blue for the clothes. It’s a poor mimiry I have, sure, since I came across the say. Afore that I could recollect beyond anything, and the praste used to praise my rading. I think it was the tossin’ an’ rollin’ ov the ship upsot my brain.

===Chinese===

玛丽说，她记得沙利文小姐；家里先后雇过她几回，每回做几天活，都不算近。"我们很喜欢她的针线，原想下个六礼拜也雇她。"她叹口气补道，"可一打听，说她如今在纽约做事了。"
"这么说，宅子的格局、全家的习惯——比如几时用饭——她必是熟透了。她可以趁全家用饭时进来；万一撞上佣人或旁人进屋，她还可以装作有事来访、正在等小姐们。"伯顿先生说。
随后佣人们被逐个唤来，问的是一周之内可曾见宅内宅园周围有什么形迹可疑的人。她们个个自然都处在举国高亢的激动里，问题刚一出口，便把天底下别的想得出来的情形全答了一遍，又忙不迭自求主保佑，呼圣母之名，把去年前年到厨房来过的叫花子挨个数了一遍，痛哭一场，终究没说出一句囫囵话。
"啊，对了！"厨娘诺拉说，"上礼拜三来过个卖黑鞋油蓝漂粉的，我买了一瓶漂衣裳的蓝。我这记性真是差了，打渡过那片海就这样。从前我什么都记得住，神父还夸过我念书好呢。我看是船上颠得把脑子颠坏了。

===Original===

It was Saturday, it wur, and oh, Lordy, it is setting me all of a trimble a-thinkin’ of that day, and I see a little yeller dog a-stickin’ his nose into the kitching door, which was open about half, and says I, there’s vagabonds around sure, now, I knew by the dog, and I wint and looked out, and sure as me name’s Norah, there was an old lame man wid a stick a-pretinding to look for rags an’ bones in the alley to the stable, which I niver allows such about, as it’s against the master’s orthers, and I druv him off immajetly﻿—and that, I think, was Saturday two weeks now, but I won’t be sure; and I don’t mind nobody else but the chany-woman, wid her basket, which I don’t think it could have been her as done any thin’ bad, for she’s been round rig’ler, for a good while, and is a dacent-spoken body that I’ve had some dalin’s wid myself. I sowld her my old plaid gown for the matchbox of ebony that sits on the kitching-mantel now, and oh dear! but my heart’s dead broke, sure! Margaret and I daren’t set in the kitching of nights no more, unless Jim’s there, an’ I’ve woke up scr’aming two nights now﻿—och hone! and if I’d seed anything, I’d a told it long afore, which I wish I had, seein’ you’ve axed me, sir. It don’t do no good a-cooking delicacies which nobody eats no longer﻿—I wish I had never come to Amyriky, to see poor Miss Eleanor so tuk down!” and having relieved herself of the sympathy which she had been aching to express, without the opportunity, she threw her apron over her head, and sobbed after the manner of her people.
Margaret’s testimony was no more to the point than Norah’s. Mr. Burton let each one go on after her own heart, putting up with the tedious circumlocution, in the hope of some kernel of wheat in the bushel of chaff.
After a deluge of tears and interjections, Maggie did finally come out with a statement which arrested the attention of her listeners.
“I’ve never seen none gawking about as didn’t belong here﻿—not a living sowl. The howly Virgin prevint that iver I should see what Jim did﻿—it wasn’t a human being at all, but a wraith, and he seen it that very night. He niver told us of it, till the Tuesday night, as we sot talking about the funeral, and it frightened us so, we niver slept a wink till morning.

===Chinese===

那天是礼拜六，没错；哦，主啊，一想起那天，我这浑身就打颤。我看见一条小黄狗把鼻子拱进厨房门缝——门半开着——我就说，准是外头有游荡的了，我一看这狗就知道；我出去一瞧，我拿性命担保，我的名字叫诺拉，果然有个瘸腿的老头拄着棍，在通马厩的夹道里假装寻破烂收骨头。这种人我从来是不许的，违犯了主人的规矩，我立刻把他轰走了——算算，那是两个礼拜前的礼拜六吧，我也拿不准；除了那回收瓷器的女人挎着篮子来过，我不记得还有谁；她总来，来了好一阵了，说话斯文，是个体面人，我自家也同她打过几回交道，不像是她能干出什么坏事。我把我的旧格子裙卖给了她，换了她那盒乌木火柴盒，如今就搁在厨房壁炉台上；哦天哪！我这心真是碎透了！一到夜里，玛格丽特同我再不敢一个人坐厨房，除非吉姆在；有两夜我都是叫喊着吓醒的——苦哇！我要真瞧见了什么，早说了，断不会等到先生您来问；早知如此，倒盼着真让我瞧见点什么好。烹些个细巧吃食也没人动一筷子——真后悔来这亚美利加，眼看可怜的埃莉诺小姐遭这样的难！"她把这份憋了许久没处倾诉的同情一气倒完，便把围裙蒙上头，按她族人的规矩呜呜咽咽哭起来。
玛格丽特的证词也不比诺拉的更贴题。伯顿先生由着各人絮絮地讲，耐着性子听那些绕不完的弯子，指望一蒲式耳的秕糠里能筛出一粒麦子。
泪水与惊叹的洪流过后，玛吉总算倒出一句叫听的人都竖起耳朵的话。
"东张西望的外人，我一个也没见过——一个活人也没见过。圣洁的圣母保佑，千万别让我撞见吉姆撞见的东西——那根本不是人，是个魂儿，就是那天夜里他撞见的。他一直没吭声，到礼拜二夜里，我们坐着说葬礼的事，他才说出来，吓得我们到天亮都没合一下眼。

===Original===

Poor Jim’s worried with it, too; he pretinds he isn’t afraid of the livin’ nor dead, but it’s no shame to the best to stand in awe of the sperits, and I see he’s backward about going about the place, alone, after dark, and no wonder! Sure, he saw a ghost!”
“What was it like?”
“Sure, you’d best call him, and let him describe it for hisself﻿—it’ll make your blood run cold to think of sich things in a Christian family.”
Jim was summoned. His story, weeded out, was this: On Saturday evening, after tea, his mistress, Miss Eleanor, had asked him to go to the post-office for the evening mail. It was very dark and rainy. He lighted the lantern. As he went out the back gate, he stopped a minute and lifted his lantern to take a look about the premises, to see if there was anything left out which ought to be taken in from the storm. As he waved the light about, he saw something in the flower-garden, about six feet from the bay-window. It had the appearance of a woman; its face was white, its hair hung down on its shoulders; it stood quite still in the rain, just as if the water was not coming down by bucketfuls. It had very large, bright eyes, which shone when the candle threw the light on them, as if they had been made of fire. He was so frightened that he let his lantern fall, which did not happen to extinguish the candle, but when he lifted it up again, the wraith had vanished. He felt very queer about it, at the time; and next day, when the bad news came, he knew it was a warning. They often had such in the old country.
We did not undeceive Jim as to the character of the phantom. With the assurance that it probably would not come again, since its mission had been accomplished, and a caution not to make the girls in the kitchen too nervous about it, we dismissed him.

===Chinese===

可怜的吉姆也叫这事缠上了；他嘴上说活人死人他一概不怕，可敬畏鬼神，圣贤也在所难免；我看他天一黑就不肯独自在宅子四下走动，也难怪！他可是亲眼见了鬼！"
"那鬼什么模样？"
"您最好把他叫来，让他自己说——想想基督徒家里出这样的事，保叫您血都凉了。"
吉姆被唤了进来。去掉枝蔓，他的故事是这样的：礼拜六傍晚茶后，女主人埃莉诺小姐差他去邮局取晚班邮件。天黑，雨大。他点了灯笼。出后门时，他停了停，举灯笼四下照看，瞧瞧有没有该从风雨里收进来的东西没收拾。灯光一晃，他看见花园里、离凸窗约莫六英尺的地方，有个东西。像个女人；脸是白的，头发披在肩上；在大雨里一动不动地立着，仿佛那水不是一桶一桶往下泼。一双眼睛极大、极亮，烛光一照，闪闪的像两团火。他吓得灯笼脱了手——巧的是烛没灭；等他把灯笼再提起来，那魂儿没了。他当时就觉着邪性；第二天噩耗传来，他知道那是个预兆。老家那边，这样的事常有。
我们没有向吉姆点破那幻影的底细。只安慰他说，它使命已完，多半不会再来了；又叮嘱他别把厨房里的姑娘们吓得太过；随后便打发他去了。

## VII Eleanor / 七 埃莉诺
===Original===

One week, another﻿—a third﻿—a fourth, passed by. Our village was as if it had never been shaken by a fierce agitation. Already the tragedy was as if it had not been, except to the household whose fairest flower it had blighted. People no longer looked over their shoulders as they walked; the story now only served to enliven the history of the little place, when it was told to a stranger.
Everything that human energy could accomplish had been done to track the murder to its origin; yet not one step had been gained since we sat, that Wednesday afternoon, in the parlor, holding a council over the handkerchief. Young and healthful as I was, I felt my spirits breaking down under my constant, unavailing exertions. The time for my examination came, which could not be unsuccessful, I had so long been thoroughly prepared, but I had lost my keen interest in this era of my life, while my ambition grew torpid. To excel in my profession had become, for the time, quite the secondary object of my life; my brain grew feverish with the harassment of restless projects﻿—the recoil of thwarted ideas. There was not one in the family group (always excepting that unseen and cloistered sufferer) who betrayed the wear-and-tear of our trouble so much as I. James remarked once that I was improved by losing some of my boyish ruddiness﻿—I was “toning down,” he said. On another occasion, with that Mephistophiles smile of his, he observed that it must be that I was after the handsome rewards﻿—the sum-total would make a comfortable setting-out for a person just starting in the world.
I do not think he wished to quarrel with me; he was always doubly pleasant after any such waspish sting; he was naturally satirical, and he could not always curb his inclination to be so at my expense.
In the meantime an impression grew upon me that he was watching me﻿—with what intent I had not yet decided.
In all this time I had not seen Eleanor. She had recovered from her illness, so as to be about her room, but had not yet joined the family at meals.

===Chinese===

一周过去了，又是一周——第三周——第四周。我们村子仿佛从来不曾被一阵狂澜摇撼过。这桩惨事仿佛从来不曾有过——只除了那一户人家，它最娇艳的花是在那里被摧折的。人们走路不再回头张望了；这故事如今只剩一个用处：讲给外来人听时，给小村的历史添几分活气。
人力所能做的，都已做尽，要追到凶案的源头；然而自那个星期三下午、我们坐在客厅里就着一方手绢计议以来，寸步未进。我虽年轻健旺，连日无果的奔忙也觉出精神要垮。应考的日子到了；我准备得那样充分，断无落第之理，可我对人生这一段的兴味已失，壮志也发了钝。一时之间，在本业里出类拔萃竟成了我次一等的心事；我的头脑被那些焦灼不休的盘算烧得发热——那是受挫的念头的反激。全家上下（那位深锁幽室、无人得见的受难者除外），没有一个把这场变故的磨损写在身上如我之甚。詹姆斯有一回说，褪了些少年红润，我倒添了成色——说我"收敛"了。又有一回，他带着他那副靡非斯特的笑，说我必定是奔着那几笔可观的赏格去的——加起来，够一个刚出山的人体面起家了。
我不以为他要同我吵；每扎出这样一句黄蜂话，他过后必加倍地和气；他天性刻薄，忍不住要拿我垫刀口时，也从不多加收敛。
与此同时，我心里渐渐生出一个感觉：他在盯我——存着什么心，我还没断定。
这些时日里，我始终没见到埃莉诺。她病体已复，能在房里走动了，却还没下楼同全家同桌用饭。

===Original===

I went frequently to the house; it had been a second home to me ever since I left the haunts of my boyhood and the old redbrick mansion, with the Grecian portico, whose massive pillars were almost reflected in the waters of Seneca lake, so close to the shore did it stand﻿—and where my mother still resided, amidst the friends who had known her in the days of her happiness﻿—that is, of my father’s life.
With the same freedom as of old, I went and came to and from Mr. Argyll’s. I was not apprehensive of intruding upon Eleanor, because she never left her apartments; while Mary, gay young creature, troubled and grieved as she was, could not stay always in the shadow. At her age, the budding blooms of womanhood require sunshine. She was lonely, and when she left her sister to the solitude which Eleanor preferred, she wanted company, she said. James was gloomy, and would not try to amuse her﻿—not that she wanted to be amused, but everything was so sad, and she felt so timid, it was a relief to have anyone to talk to, or even to look at. I felt very sorry for her. It became a part of my duty to bring her books, and sometimes to read them aloud, through the lengthening evenings; at others to while away the time with a game of chess. The piano was abandoned out of respect for the mourner in the chamber above. Carols would rise to Mary’s lips, as they rise from a lark at sunrise, but she always broke them off, drowning them in sighs. Her elastic spirit constantly asserted itself, while the tender sympathy of a most warm, affectionate nature as constantly depressed it. She could not speak of Eleanor without tears; and for this my heart blessed her. She did not know of the choking in my own throat which often prevented me from speaking, when I ought, perhaps, to be uttering words of help or comfort.
James was always hovering about like a restless spirit. It had been one of his indolent habits to spend a great deal of time with the young ladies; and now he was forever in the house; but so uneasy, so irritable﻿—as Mary said﻿—he was not an agreeable companion. He would pick up a book in the library; in five minutes he would throw it down, and walk twice or thrice up and down the hall, out upon the piazza, back into the parlor, and stand looking out of the windows﻿—then to the library and take up another book. He had the air of one always listening﻿—always waiting.

===Chinese===

我常去那座宅子；自我离开童年旧地，它便是我第二个家——旧地有那座红砖老宅，带希腊式门廊，粗大的廊柱几乎映在塞纳卡湖水里，它离岸那样近——我母亲如今仍住在那里，住在她幸福岁月——也就是我父亲在世时——就相识的朋友中间。
我照旧无拘无束地出入阿盖尔家。我不愁撞见埃莉诺，她从不出她的闺房；玛丽呢，虽说愁苦，到底是欢快年纪的小人儿，总不能一直待在阴影里。在她这个年纪，含苞的少女是要阳光的。她闷；把姐姐撇给她自个儿偏爱的清静之后，她说，她要人作伴。詹姆斯阴沉沉的，不肯费心哄她——倒不是她要人哄，只是处处这样凄凉，她心里发怯，有个人说说话、哪怕只是看看，也是松快。我很怜惜她。给她带书成了我的功课之一；夜渐长，有时我便读书给她听；有时下一盘棋消磨时光。钢琴因为楼上居丧的人而歇了。颂歌会不由自主涌到玛丽唇边，像日出时云雀喉咙里的歌，可她总是一开口就掐断，叹一口气淹了它。她那活泼的天性时时要抬头，而那颗火热柔情的心肠里的体贴，又时时把它按下去。她一提埃莉诺必落泪；为这个，我的心为她祝福。她不知道我自己喉咙里也常常哽着，堵得我说不出话——在那些本该由我来说几句宽慰的时辰。
詹姆斯像一缕不安的魂，总在宅子里荡来荡去。同年轻小姐们消磨光阴，本就是他怠惰习性中的一桩；如今他索性长在宅里了；却那样坐立不安、那样躁——照玛丽的话说——实在不是个可亲的伴。他从书房抓起一本书；五分钟便撂下，在门厅里来回走上两三趟，出到游廊，又折回客厅，立在窗前望外面——再回书房另抓一本。他那副神气，像一直在听——一直在等。

===Original===

He had, too, a kind of haunted look, if my reader can imagine what that is. I guessed that he was listening and waiting for Eleanor﻿—whom, like myself, he had not seen since the Sunday so memorable; but the other look I did not seek to explain.
There had been a light fall of snow. It seemed as if winter had come in November. But in a few hours this aspect vanished; the snow melted like a dream; the zenith was a deep, molten blue, transfused with the pale sunshine, which is only seen in Indian-summer; a tender mist circled the horizon with a zone of purple. I could not stay in the office that afternoon, so infinitely sad, so infinitely lovely. I put aside the law-papers which I had been arranging for a case in which I was first to appear before a jury and make my maiden argument. The air, soft as that of summer and scented with the indescribable perfume of perishing leaves, came to me through the open window, with a message calling me abroad; I took up my hat, stepped out upon the pavement, and wandering along the avenue in the direction of the house, went in upon the lawn. I had thought to go out into the open country for a long walk; but my heart drew me and held me here. The language of all beauty, and of infinity itself, is love. The divine melancholy of music, the deep tranquillity of summer noons, the softened splendor of autumn days, haunting one with ineffable joy and sadness﻿—what is the name of all this varying demonstration of beauty, but love?
I walked beneath the trees, slowly, my feet nestling among the thickly-strewn leaves, and pressing a faint aroma from the moist earth. To and fro for a long time I rambled, thinking no tangible thoughts, but my soul silently filling, all the time, like a fountain fed by secret springs. To the back of the lawn, extending around and behind the flower-garden, was a little ascent, covered by a grove of elms and maples, in the midst of which was a summerhouse which had been a favorite resort of Eleanor’s. Hither I finally bent my steps, and seating myself, looked musingly upon the lovely prospect around and beneath me. The rustic temple opened toward the river, which was visible from here, rolling in its blue splendor across the exquisite landscape.

===Chinese===

他脸上还有种中了邪似的神色——读者若能想象那是什么样子。我猜他在听、在等的，是埃莉诺——同我一样，那个难忘的礼拜天之后他也没见过她；可另外那种神色，我没去求解。
落了一场薄雪。冬天仿佛十一月里就到了。可几个时辰之间，这番景象便消散了；雪化得像一场梦；天顶是一片深深的、熔化的蓝，浸透了小阳春里才有的淡淡日光；一圈柔雾给地平线围上一道紫带。那天下午我在事务所里坐不住，那样的无边哀婉，又那样的无边可爱。我推开手头的法律文书——那是一桩案子的卷宗，我将在其中头一回站上陪审团前，做我的初辩。空气软得如同夏天，带着朽叶那说不出的芬芳，从敞开的窗口涌进来，捎着唤我出外的音信；我拿起帽子，踱到街上，沿着林荫道朝宅子的方向信步走去，进了草坪。本想出城到野地里走个痛快；可我的心把我拽住，把我留在原地。一切美的语言、无限自身的语言，都是爱。乐音里神圣的忧郁，夏日正午深沉的静谧，秋日柔化了的辉煌，教人萦怀于莫可名状的欣悦与哀愁——这一切美的千变万化的昭示，它的名字，除了爱，还能是什么？
我在树下缓缓走着，双脚踏进厚厚的落叶，从湿土里踩出一缕幽香。我来回踱了许久，想的都不是成形的心事，可我的灵魂一直在无声地满涨，像一眼受暗泉喂养的泉。草坪后面，绕着花园、花园再往后，是一道小坡，覆着一片榆树枫树的林子，林中有一座凉亭——那原是埃莉诺最爱盘桓的去处。我终于举步往那里去，坐下，出神地望着四围和脚下可爱的景色。这座粗木的小亭朝河开着；从这儿望得见大河，携着它蓝色的壮阔，滚过这一带明媚的原野。

===Original===

There is a fascination in water which will keep the eyes fixed upon it through hours of reverie; I sat there, mindful of the near mountains, the purple mist, the white ships, the busy village, but gazing only at the blue ripples forever slipping away from the point of my observation. My spirit exhaled like the mist and ascended in aspiration. My grief aspired, and arose in passionate prayers to the white throne of the eternal justice﻿—it arose in tears, etherealized and drawn up by the rays from the one great source and sun﻿—the spirit of Love. I prayed and wept for *her*. No thought of myself mingled with these emotions.
Suddenly a slight chill fell upon me. I started to perceive that the sun had set. A band of orange belted the west. As the sun dropped behind the hills the moon came up in the east. It seemed as if her silver light frosted what it touched; the air grew sharp; a thin, white cloud spread itself over the river. I had sat there long enough, and I was forcing myself to a consciousness of the fact, when I saw one coming through the flower-garden and approaching the summerhouse.
My blood paused in my veins when I saw that it was Eleanor. The sunset yet lingered, and the cold moonlight shone full on her face. I remembered how I had seen her, that last time but one, glowing and flushing in triumphant beauty, attired with the most skilled coquetry of a young, beloved woman, who is glad of her charms because another prizes them.
Now she came along the lonesome path, between the withered flowerbeds, clothed in deepest black, walking with a feeble step, one small white hand holding the sable shawl across her chest, a long crape veil thrown over her head, from which her face looked out, white and still.
A pang like that of death transfixed me, as I gazed at her. Not one rose left in the garden of her young life! The ruin through which she walked was not so complete﻿—but this garden would resurrect itself in the months of another spring﻿—while for her there was no spring on this side of the grave.
Slowly she threaded her way, with bent gaze, through the garden, out upon the hillside, and up to the little rustic temple in which she had spent so many happy hours with him. When she had reached the grassy platform in front of it, she raised her eyes and swept a glance around upon the familiar scene.

===Chinese===

水有一种魔力，教人凝眸几个钟头也不倦；我坐在那里，也记得近处的山、紫雾、白帆的船、忙碌的村庄，眼睛却只望着蓝色的涟漪，一波一波，从我视线尽头永远溜走。我的精神像雾气一样蒸发，化作祈愿上升。我的哀伤升上去，化作炽热的祷告，升向永恒正义的白色宝座——化作眼泪升上去，被那一轮伟大的源头与太阳——爱的灵——的光线所摄取、升华。我祈求着，为她流泪。这些情感里，不掺一丝为我自己的念头。
忽然一阵轻寒袭来。我惊觉日落了。西天束着一带橙色。太阳落进山后，月亮从东边升起来。她的银光仿佛落到哪里，哪里便结霜；空气利了；一层薄薄的白霭铺在河面上。我坐得够久了，正强使自己承认这一层，忽见有人穿过花园，朝凉亭走来。
看清那是埃莉诺，我血管里的血停了一停。落日余晖未尽，清冷的月光正照满她的脸。我记得上一次见她——倒数第二次——她浑身焕发，红光满面，是得胜的美，衣装透着一个被爱的年轻女子最精心的娇俏，她为自己的魅力欢喜，因为有个人珍重它。
此刻她沿那条寂寞的小径走来，行在凋残的花畦之间，一身重黑，步子虚浮，一只雪白的小手把黑貂披肩拢在胸前，头上罩着一幅长长的黑纱，纱后那张脸望出来，白而静止。
望着她，一阵死亡的痛楚把我钉住。她那年轻生命的花园里，一朵玫瑰也不剩了！她行走其间的那片荒芜还没这般彻底——可这园子来年春天还会重生——而她，坟墓这一边再没有春天了。
她垂着眼，缓缓穿过花园，走上山坡，一路来到这座粗木小亭——她曾同他在这里消磨过多少快乐的时辰。走到亭前草地上，她才抬起眼，把四下熟悉的景致扫了一眼。

===Original===

There were no tears in her blue eyes, and her lips did not quiver. It was not until she had encircled the horizon with that quiet, beamless look, that she perceived me. I rose to my feet, my expression only doing reverence to her sorrow, for I had no words.
She held out her hand, and as I took it, she said with gentleness﻿—as if her sweetness must excuse the absence of her former smiles,
“Are you well, Richard? You look thin. Be careful of yourself﻿—is it not too chilly for you to be sitting here at this hour?”
I pressed her hand, and turned away, vainly endeavoring to command my voice. *I* had changed!﻿—but it was like Eleanor to put herself aside and remember others.
“Nay, do not go,” she said, as she saw that I was leaving her out of fear of intruding upon her visit, “I shall remain here but a few moments, and I will lean upon your arm back to the house. I am not strong, and the walk up the hill has tired me. I wanted to see you, Richard. I thought some of coming downstairs a little while this evening. I want to thank you.”
The words were just whispered, and she turned immediately and looked away at the river. I understood her well. She wanted to thank me for the spirit which had prompted me in my earnest, though unsuccessful efforts. And coming down to the family-group a little while in the evening, that was for Mary’s sake, and her poor father’s. Her own light had expired, but she did not wish to darken the hearthstone any more than was unavoidable. She sunk down upon the seat I had vacated, remaining motionless, looking upon the river and the sky. After a time, with a long, tremulous sigh, she arose to go. A gleam from the west fell upon a single violet which, protected from the frost by the projecting roof, smiled up at us, near the door of the summerhouse. With a wild kind of passion breaking through her quiet, Eleanor stooped, gathered it, pressed it to her lips, and burst into tears﻿—it was her favorite flower﻿—Henry’s favorite.
It was agony to see her cry, yet better, perhaps, than such marble repose. She was too weak to bear this sudden shock alone; she leaned upon my shoulder, every sob which shook her frame echoed by me. Yes! I am not ashamed to confess it! When manhood is fresh and unsullied, its tears are not wrung out in those single drops of mortal anguish which the rock gives forth when time and the foot of the world have hardened it.

===Chinese===

她蓝眼睛里没有泪，唇也不颤。她用那种安静的、不发光的目光绕地平线兜了一圈，才看见我。我起身站定，神情只向她的悲恸致敬——我没有话。
她伸出手来；我握住时，她温存地说——仿佛她的温柔必须替她补上旧日笑靥的缺席：
"你好吗，理查德？你瘦了。当心自己——这个时辰坐在这儿，不嫌太凉么？"
我紧一紧她的手，别过脸去，徒然想管住自己的嗓音。*我*变了！——可把自己撇在一边、只记得旁人，正是埃莉诺的为人。
"不，别走。"她说，见我因为怕搅扰她这番凭吊而要退开，"我待一会儿就走，回头我扶着你的胳膊回宅子。我身子弱，上这道坡乏了。我想见见你，理查德。今晚我想着下楼来一小会儿。我要谢谢你。"
这几个字轻得像耳语，说完她立即转过脸去望河。我懂她的意思。她要谢的，是促成我那番恳切努力——虽徒劳——的那份心。晚间下楼到家人中间坐一小会儿，那是为玛丽，为她可怜的父亲。她自己的灯灭了，可她不愿把家的炉石压得更暗，除非避不开。她在我让出的座位上坐下，一动不动，望着河水与天空。过了一阵，长长一声颤抖的叹息，她起身要走。一线西天的余光落在一朵紫罗兰上——它受亭檐荫庇，躲过了霜，正在凉亭门口仰脸向我们微笑。埃莉诺俯身摘下它，一种狂野的激情冲破了她的安静，她把它按上唇，泪如泉涌——那是她最爱的花——也是亨利最爱的。
看她哭是酷刑，可或许好过那种大理石般的沉静。她太弱了，独力受不住这骤然一击；她伏在我肩上，她每一次撼动身躯的抽噎，都在我身上起了回响。是的！我不耻于承认！当男子汉还新鲜无瑕，他的眼泪不会凝成那种单独的、垂死苦痛的点滴——那要等岁月和世人的脚跟把磐石磨硬了，它才渗得出。

===Original===

I could still remember when I had kissed my mother, and wept my boyish troubles well upon her breast. I should have been harder than the nether millstone, had I not wept tears with Eleanor then.
I mastered myself in order to assist her to regain composure, for I was alarmed lest the violence of her emotion should break down the remnant of her frail strength. She, too, struggled against the storm, soon growing outwardly calm, and with the violet pressed to her bosom with one hand, with the other she clung to my arm, and we returned to the house, where they were already looking for Eleanor.
Under the full light of the hall-lamp we encountered James. It was his first meeting with his cousin as well as mine. He gave her a quick, penetrating look, held out his hand, his lips moved as if striving to form a greeting. It was evident that the change was greater than he expected; he dropped his hand, before her fingers had touched it, and rushing past us through the open door, he closed it behind him, remaining out until long after tea.
When he came in, Eleanor had retired to her chamber, and Mary brought him the cup of tea which she had kept hot for him.
“You are a good girl, Mary,” he said, drinking it hastily, as if to get rid of it. “I hope nobody will ever make you look like *that*! I thought broken hearts were easily mended﻿—that girls usually had theirs broken three or four times, and patched them up again﻿—but I have changed my mind.”
That gloomy look, which Mary declared she dreaded, clouded his face again. His countenance was most variable; nothing could excel it in glitter and brilliant color when he was in his pleasing mood, but when sullen or sad, it was sallow and lusterless. Thus it looked that evening. But I must close this chapter now and here﻿—it is consecrated to that meeting with the object of my sorrow and adoration, and I will not prolong it with the details of other events.

===Chinese===

我还记得从前吻别母亲、把少年的烦恼在她怀里哭个痛快的日子。那时不同埃莉诺一起落泪，我岂不比磨盘底下的磨石还硬。
我强自镇定，好扶她恢复平静——她情绪这样凶猛，我怕震碎她那点残存的弱力，实在惶恐。她也在同风暴搏斗，很快便外表平静下来；一手把紫罗兰按在胸口，一手挽住我的手臂，我们回到宅中——家里人正已经在寻埃莉诺了。
门厅灯的满光下，我们撞见詹姆斯。这也是他同堂妹的重逢，同我一样。他飞快地、透骨地看了她一眼，伸出手，嘴唇翕动，像要拼出一句问安。变化显然远出他意料；不等她的指尖碰到，他先自垂下手，从我们身边抢过敞开的门冲出去，随手带上门，直到吃茶后许久才回来。
他进屋时，埃莉诺已经回了房，玛丽端给他那杯一直温着的茶。
"你是个好姑娘，玛丽。"他说，三口两口把茶灌下去，像只求脱手。"但愿永远别有人把你弄成*那副*样子！我原以为心碎是容易补的——姑娘们的心总要碎个三四回，再自己缝上——我可改了主意。"
那副玛丽说她见了发怵的阴沉神色，又罩上他的脸。他的脸色最是多变：得意时，光彩色泽，鲜亮无伦；一旦乖戾或颓丧，便灰黄暗哑。那晚便是这样。可本章必须到此为止、就此打住——这一章是献给同我悲恸与倾慕之人的那番相见的，我不愿拿旁的事故细节来把它拖长。

## VIII The Haunted Grave / 八 闹鬼的坟墓
===Original===

When I returned to my boardinghouse that same evening, I found a telegram awaiting me from Mr. Burton, asking me to come down to the city in the morning. I went down by the earliest train, and, soon after, ringing the bell at the door of his private residence in Twenty-third street, a servant ushered me into the library, where I found the master of the house so absorbed in thought, as he sat before the grate with his eyes bent upon the glowing coals, that he did not observe my entrance until I spoke his name. Springing to his feet, he shook me heartily by the hand; we had already become warm personal friends.
“You are early,” he said, “but so much the better. We will have the more time for business.”
“Have you heard anything?” was my first question.
“Well, no. Don’t hope that I have called you here to satisfy you with any positive discoveries. The work goes on slowly. I was never so baffled but once before; and then, as now, there was a woman in the case. A cunning woman will elude the very Prince of Lies, himself, to say nothing of honest men like us. She has been after the child.”
“She has?”
“Yes. And has taken it away with her. And now I know no more of her whereabouts than I did before. There! You must certainly feel like trusting your case to some sharper person to work up”﻿—he looked mortified as he said it.
Before I go further I must explain to my reader just how far the investigation into the acts and hiding-place of Leesy Sullivan had proceeded. Of course we had called upon her aunt in Blankville, and approached the question of the child with all due caution. She had answered us frankly enough, at first﻿—that Leesy had a cousin who lived in New York, whom she was much attached to, and who was dead, poor thing! But the moment we intruded the infant into the conversation, she flew into a rage, asked if “we’d come there to insult a respectable widdy, as wasn’t responsible for what others did?” and wouldn’t be coaxed or threatened into any further speech on the subject, fairly driving us out of the room and (I regret to add) down the stairs with the broomstick. As we could not summon her into court and compel her to answer, at that time, we were compelled to “let her alone.” One thing, however, became apparent at the interview﻿—that there was shame or blame, or at least a family quarrel, connected with the child.
After that, in New York, Mr.

===Chinese===

当天晚上回到寓所，我发现伯顿先生来电报等我，约我明早进城。我搭最早一班车下去，不久便在二十三街他私宅门前摇铃；仆人引我进书房，只见主人坐在炉栅前，目光沉沉落在烧红的煤上，出神到全然不觉我进来，直到我叫出他的名字。他一跃而起，热烈同我握手；我们早已成了知交。
"你来得早。"他说，"那更好。办正事的工夫就更多了。"
"有消息？"这是我头一句。
"唔，没有。别指望我叫你来，是为了拿什么确凿的发现让你高兴。事情进展得慢。我从没这样被难住过——只除了从前一回；而那一回，同这一回一样，案子里有个女人。一个狡黠的女人，连谎言王子本尊都能甩脱，更别说咱们这样的老实人。她去找那孩子了。"
"是吗？"
"是。把孩子一并带走了。这一来她的下落，我又同先前一样一无所知了。瞧！你八成觉得该把这案子交给哪个更厉害的人去办吧。"——说这话时，他一脸懊丧。
往下讲之前，我须向读者交代清楚：对丽西·沙利文行踪下落的查究，究竟到了哪一步。我们自然拜访过她在布兰克维尔的姑妈，就孩子的话题处处掂着分寸。起初她答得倒还坦率——说丽西有个堂姐住在纽约，两人极亲，人没了，可怜的人！可我们刚把婴孩递进话头，她登时火冒三丈，问我们"是不是上门来作弄一个体面寡妇的，旁人干的事凭什么叫她担待？"随即好说不听、恫吓无用，这个题目再吐不出半个字，索性拿扫帚把我们轰出房门——（我还得补一句）轰下楼梯。那时既不能传她到庭强令作答，我们只得"由她去"。但那一见倒显明了一件事：这孩子身上，连着耻辱或罪责，至少也是一场家庭纠纷。
此后在纽约，伯

===Original===

Burton ascertained that there had been a cousin, who had died, but whether she had been married, and left a babe, or not, was still a matter of some doubt.
He had spent over a week searching for Leesy Sullivan, in the vicinity of Blankville, at every intermediate station between that and New York, and throughout the city itself, assisted by scores of detectives, who all of them had her photograph, taken from a likeness which Mr. Burton had found in her deserted room at her boarding-place. This picture must have been taken more than a year previous, as it looked younger and happier; the face was soft and round, the eyes melting with warmth and light, and the rich, dark hair dressed with evident care. Still, Leesy bore resemblance enough to her former self, to make her photograph an efficient aid. Yet not one trace of her had been chanced upon since I, myself, had seen her fly away at the mention of the word which I had purposely uttered, and disappear over the wooded hill. We had nearly made up our minds that she had committed suicide; we had searched the shore for miles in the vicinity of Moreland villa, and had fired guns over the water; but if she had hidden herself in those cold depths, she had done it most effectually.
The gardener’s wife, at the villa, had kept vigilant watch, as I had requested, but she had never anything to report﻿—the sewing-girl came no more to haunt the piazza or the summerhouse. Finally, Mr. Burton had given over active measures, relying simply upon the presence of the child in New York, to bring back the protectress into his nets, if indeed she was still upon earth. He said rightly, that if she were concealed and had any knowledge of the efforts made to discover her, the surest means of hastening her reappearance would be to apparently relinquish all pursuit. He had a person hired to watch the premises of the nurse constantly; a person who took a room next to hers in the tenement-house where she resided, apparently employed in knitting children’s fancy woolen garments, but really for the purpose of giving immediate notification should the guardian of the infant appear upon the scene. In the meantime he was kept informed of the sentiments of the nurse, who had avowed her intention of throwing the babe upon the authorities, if its board was not paid at the end of the month.

===Chinese===

顿先生查明：确曾有过一位堂姐，死了；可她婚否、有否遗下一个婴孩，仍是疑案。
他花了不止一个礼拜搜寻丽西·沙利文——布兰克维尔四乡、它同纽约之间的每一个中间站、以及纽约全城——手下几十名侦探相助，人人手里有她的照片，是从她弃置的寓所房间里找到的一幅画像翻拍的。照片想必摄于一年多以前，人显得更年轻、更快活；脸柔而圆，眼睛含着暖光，一头浓密的黑发梳得显然用心。尽管如此，同如今的丽西仍相像得足以让照片成为得力的帮手。然而，自我亲见她在我有意吐出的那个字眼上惊飞、消失在林山背后以来，再无人撞见她半点踪迹。我们几乎认定她自尽了；我们在莫尔兰别墅四周沿河搜寻数英里，又朝水面鸣枪；可她若真把自己藏进了那片寒冷的深处，那也藏得干净利落。
别墅的园丁妻子遵我嘱托，一直留意守望，却从无可报——缝衣女再没来缠磨那门廊和凉亭。末了，伯顿先生停止了主动出击，单凭着纽约城里那孩子在，等那位保护人自投罗网——倘若她还在人世。他说得对：她若藏匿着，又多少晓得外间的搜寻，那么要催她露面，最稳的法子，便是摆出尽数罢手的样子。他雇了一个人常年盯住奶妈的住处；那人在奶妈所住的出租大院里租下隔壁一间屋，表面靠织儿童花色绒线衣过活，实则为的是婴儿的保护人一露面，即刻报信。与此同时，奶妈的口风他也随时听得到——她已放话：月底再不见伙食钱，就把孩子推给官府。

===Original===

“Hard enough,” she avowed it was, “to get the praties for the mouths of her own chilther; and the little girl was growing large now. The milk wouldn’t do at all, at all, but she must have her praties and her bit bread wid the rest.”
In answer to these complaints, the wool-knitter had professed such an interest in the innocent little thing, that, sooner than allow it to go to the alms-house, or to the orphan-asylum, or any other such place, she would take it to her own room, and share her portion with it, when the nurse’s month was up, until it was certain that the aunt was not coming to see after it, she said.
With this understanding between them, the two women got along finely together; little Nora, just toddling about, was a pretty child, and her aunt had not spared stitches in making up her clothes, which were of good material, and ornamented with lavish tucks and embroidery. She was often, for half a day at a time, in the room with the new tenant, when her nurse was out upon errands, or at work; and the former sometimes took her out in her arms for a breath of air upon the better streets. Mr. Burton had seen little Nora several times; he thought she resembled Miss Sullivan, though not strikingly. She had the same eyes, dark and bright.
Two days before Mr. Burton telegraphed for me to come down to New York, Mrs. Barber, the knitting detective, was playing with the child in her own room. It was growing toward night, and the nurse was out getting her Saturday afternoon supplies at Washington Market; she did not expect her back for at least an hour. Little Nora was in fine spirits, being delighted with a blue-and-white hood which her friend had manufactured for her curly head. As they frolicked together, the door opened, a young woman came in, caught the child to her breast, kissed it, and cried. “An‑nee﻿—an‑nee,” lisped the baby﻿—and Mrs. Barber, slipping out, with the excuse that she would go for the nurse, who was at a neighbor’s, jumped into a car, and rode up to Twenty-third street. In half an hour Mr. Burton was at the tenement-house; the nurse had not yet returned from market, and the bird had flown, carrying the baby with her. He was sufficiently annoyed at this denouement. In the arrangements made, the fact of the nurse being away had not been contemplated; there was no one to keep on the track of the fugitive while the officer was notified.

===Chinese===

"日子难哪。"她白己也这么说，"自家几张嘴的土豆都愁不出来；小姑娘又一天天大了。光喝奶怎么行，怎么也不行，她得跟上头一块儿吃土豆、啃口面包。"
对这些抱怨，织绒线的侦探娘子表示出对那无辜小东西深切的关怀，说到时候若奶妈的月头一满，她宁可把孩子抱到自己屋里，同自己一份吃食匀着吃，也断不容她进贫民院、孤儿院那等地方——只要还没断定她姑妈真不来照看了，她就这样养着——她是这么说的。
有这一层默契，两个女人处得极好；小诺拉刚会蹒跚走路，是个标致娃儿，她姑妈给她裁衣裳针脚从不吝惜，料子又好，细裥和绣花堆得满满的。奶妈出门跑差或上工时，她常整半天待在新房客屋里；新房客有时也抱她到体面街上透气。伯顿先生见过小诺拉几面；他觉得她像沙利文小姐，虽说不十分像。一双眼睛是一样的，又黑又亮。
伯顿先生打电报叫我下来之前两天，织绒线的侦探巴巴太太正在自己屋里陪孩子玩。天色渐晚，奶妈出门到华盛顿市场办礼拜六下午的吃食去了；一时半会儿回不来。小诺拉兴致极高，正为朋友给她鬈发脑袋织的蓝白小兜帽乐得不行。两人正闹着，门开了，一个年轻女子进来，把孩子搂到怀里，亲她，哭。"安——妮——安妮。"娃娃咿呀着——巴巴太太抽身出门，借口去邻家叫奶妈，跳上一辆车，直奔二十三街。不出半个钟头，伯顿先生已到那座出租大院；奶妈还没从市场回来，鸟儿却飞了，连孩子一起带走了。这个结局叫他懊恼得很。原先的布置里，没料到奶妈恰好外出；去报信通知警官的当口，竟无一人接手盯住逃人。

===Original===

One of the children said that the lady had left some money for mother; there was, lying on the table, a sum which more than covered the arrears due, and a note of thanks. But the baby, with its little cloak and its new blue hood, had vanished. Word was dispatched to the various offices, and the night spent in looking for the two; but there is no place like a great city for eluding pursuit; and up to the hour of my arrival at Mr. Burton’s he had learned nothing.
All this had fretted the detective; I could see it, although he did not say as much. He who had brought hundreds of accomplished rogues to justice did not like to be foiled by a woman. Talking on the subject with me, as we sat before the fire in his library, with closed doors, he said the most terrible antagonist he had yet encountered had been a woman﻿—that her will was a match for his own, yet he had broken with ease the spirits of the boldest men.
“However,” he added, “Miss Sullivan is not a woman of that stamp. If *she* has committed a crime, she has done it in a moment of passion, and remorse will kill her, though the vengeance of the law should never overtake her. But she is subtle and elusive. It is not reason that makes her cunning, but feeling. With man it would be reason; and as I could follow the course of his argument, whichever path it took, I should soon overtake it. But a woman, working from a passion, either of hate or love, will sometimes come to such novel conclusions as to defy the sharpest guesses of the intellect. I should like, above all things, a quiet conversation with that girl. And I will have it, some day.”
The determination with which he avowed himself, showed that he had no idea of giving up the case. A few other of his observations I will repeat:
He said that the blow which killed Henry Moreland was given by a professional murderer, a man, without conscience or remorse, probably a hireling. A woman may have tempted, persuaded, or paid him to do the deed; if so, the guilt rested upon her in its awful weight; but no woman’s hand, quivering with passion, had driven that steady and relentless blow. It was not given by the hand of jealousy﻿—it was too coldly calculated, too firmly executed﻿—no passion, no thrill of feeling about it.

===Chinese===

奶妈家一个孩子说，那位太太给妈妈留下了钱；桌上果然放着一笔，抵过欠账还有余，另附一纸谢条。可孩子，连同她的小斗篷和新蓝兜帽，一并无影无踪。消息发到各处所点，一夜都在寻找这母女俩；可要甩脱追踪，再没有比大都会更好的地方了；直到我踏进伯顿先生家门那一刻，他仍旧一无所获。
这一切搅得侦探心浮；我看得出来，虽则他没明说。把成百个老练惯贼送上公堂的人，不甘心栽在一个女人手里。书房里门关着，我们坐在炉前谈这话题，他说，他生平遇到的最可怕的对手是个女人——那意志同他旗鼓相当；而最胆大的汉子的精神，他折断起来易如反掌。
"不过，"他补道，"沙利文小姐不是那种路数。倘若*她*犯了罪，那也干在一时激情之下；就算法律的报复永远追不上她，悔恨也会处死她。可她机敏，难捉。使她狡黠的不是理性，是情感。换了男人，便是理性；他的论证走哪条路，我便能追哪条路，很快就能撵上。可一个凭激情行事的女人——恨也罢，爱也罢——有时会得出那样新奇的结论，任凭最锐利的智力去猜也要落空。我最想要的，是同那姑娘静静谈一次。我总会谈上的，早晚。"
他表白这决心时的口气，表明他绝无撒手的念头。他的另几句话，我也记在下面：
他说，杀死亨利·莫尔兰的那一刀，出自一个职业凶手之手——一个男人，没有良心，没有悔恨，多半是拿钱办事的。可能是某个女人诱惑、怂恿或雇他行凶；若果真如此，罪孽的全部重量便压在她身上；可那样又稳又狠的一击，绝不是女人激颤的手打出来的。那不是妒忌的手笔——算计得太冷，执行得太定——里头没有激情，没有一丝感情的悸动。

===Original===

“Then you think,” said I, “that Leesy Sullivan robbed the family whose happiness she was about to destroy, to pay some villain to commit the murder?”
“It looks like it,” he answered, his eye dropping evasively.
I felt that I was not fully in the detective’s confidence; there was something working powerfully in his mind, to which he gave me no clue; but I had so much faith in him that I was not offended by his reticence. Anxious as I was, eager, curious﻿—if it suits to call such a devouring fire of longing as I felt, curiosity﻿—he must have known that I perceived his reservations; if so, he had his own way of conducting matters, from which he could not diverge for my passing benefit. Twelve o’clock came, as we sat talking before the fire, which gave a genial air to the room, though almost unnecessary, the “squaw winter” of the previous morning being followed by another balmy and sunlit day. Mr. Burton rung for lunch to be brought in where we were; and while we sipped the strong coffee, and helped ourselves to the contents of the tray, the servant being dismissed, my host made a proposition which had evidently been on his mind all the morning.
I was already so familiar with his personal surroundings, as to know that he was a widower, with two children; the eldest, a boy of fifteen, away at school; the second, a girl of eleven, of delicate health, and educated at home, so far as she studied at all, by a day-governess. I had never seen this daughter﻿—Lenore, he called her﻿—but I could guess, without particular shrewdness, that his heart was wrapped up in her. He could not mention her name without a glow coming into his face; her frail health appeared to be the anxiety of his life. I could hear her, now, taking a singing-lesson in a distant apartment, and as her pure voice rose clear and high, mounting and mounting with airy steps the difficult scale, I listened delightedly, making a picture in my mind of the graceful little creature such a voice should belong to.
Her father was listening, too, with a smile in his eye, half forgetful of his coffee. Presently he said, in a low voice, speaking at first with some reluctance,
“I sent for you today, more particularly to make you the confidential witness of an experiment than anything else. You hear my Lenore singing now﻿—has she not a sweet voice? I have told you how delicate her health is.

===Chinese===

"那么你认为，"我说，"是丽西·沙利文偷了她将要毁掉的那户人家的钱，拿去雇哪个恶棍行凶？"
"看样子是。"他答，眼睛躲闪地垂下去。
我觉出自己并未尽得侦探的信任；他心里正翻腾着什么了不得的东西，却不给我半点线头；可我对他信得那样深，连他的沉默也不见怪。我何等焦急、急切、好奇——若肯把我心头那团吞人的渴念之火称作好奇的话——他必定知道我察觉了他的保留；既知道，他自有他行事的路数，不能为我一时的好处而改道。我们坐在炉前谈着，炉火给书房添了一团融融暖气——其实几乎用不上：前日清晨那场"印第安婆娘冬"过后，又是一个和煦的丽日。十二点到了，伯顿先生摇铃，叫把午饭送到我们跟前；我们呷着浓咖啡、随手取用托盘里的吃食，仆人退下后，主人提出一件显然在心头盘桓了一上午的事。
对他的家常我已相当熟：他是鳏夫，带着两个孩子；大的男孩十五岁，在外念书；二的女孩十一岁，体质纤弱，若论功课，是在家由一位日课女教师带着读的。这女儿我从未见过——他叫她莉诺——可不必怎样精明也猜得出，他的心拴在她身上。一提她的名字，他脸上就放光；她纤弱的健康是他此生的悬心。这会儿我还听得见她在远处一间屋里上唱歌课，那纯净的嗓音清亮高扬，踏着空灵的步子一级级攀上艰难的音阶；我听得心醉，在脑中描画这嗓音该属的那位灵秀小人儿。
她父亲也在听，眼里带笑，险些忘了咖啡。半晌，他低声开口，起先还带着几分迟疑：
"今天叫你来，与其说为别的，不如说要请你做一场实验的心腹见证。你听见我的莉诺在唱歌了——嗓音甜不甜？她身子怎样娇弱，我对你说过的。

===Original===

I discovered, by chance, some two or three years since, that she had peculiar attributes. She is an excellent clairvoyant. When I first discovered it, I made use of her rare faculty to assist me in my more important labors; but I soon discovered that it told fearfully upon her health. It seemed to drain the slender stream of vitality nearly dry. Our physician told me that I must desist, entirely, all experiments of the kind with her. He was peremptory about it, but he had only need to caution me. I would sooner drop a year out of my shortening future than to take one grain from that increasing strength which I watch from day to day with deep solicitude. She is my only girl, Mr. Redfield, and the image of her departed mother. You must not wonder if I am foolish about my Lenore. For eighteen months I have not exercised my power over her to place her in the trance state, or whatever it is, in which, with the clue in her hand, she will unwind the path to more perplexed labyrinths than those of the fair one’s bower. And I tell you, solemnly, that if, by so doing, she could point out pots of gold, or the secrets of diamond mines, I would not risk her slightest welfare, by again exhausting her recruiting energies. Nevertheless, so deeply am I interested in the tragedy to which you have called my attention﻿—so certain am I that I am on the eve of the solution of the mystery﻿—and such an act of justice and righteousness do I deem it that it should be exposed in its naked truth before those who have suffered from the crime﻿—that I have resolved to place Lenore once more in the clairvoyant state, for the purpose of ascertaining the hiding-place of Leesy Sullivan, and I have sent for you to witness the result.”
This announcement took away the remnant of my appetite. Mr. Burton rung to have the tray removed, and to bid the servant tell Miss Lenore, as soon as she had lunched, to come to the library. We had but a few minutes to wait. Presently we heard a light step; her father cried, “Come in!” in answer to her knock, and a lovely child entered, greeting me with a mingled air of grace and timidity﻿—a vision of sweetness and beauty more perfect than I could have anticipated. Her golden hair waved about her slender throat, in glistening tendrils.

===Chinese===

两三年前，我偶然发现她有异禀。她是位顶好的千里眼。初发现时，我借重她这军见的天赋，助我办几件要紧的案子；可不久便看出，这于她的健康戕害极大。那仿佛把生命力的细流几乎抽干。我们的医生告诉我，这类实验必须全停下来。他说得斩钉截铁，其实只消提醒我一句就够。我宁可从我这越来越短的未来里减去一年，也不愿从我日日悬心注视着的那点日渐充盈的元气里克扣一星。她是我唯一的女儿，雷德菲尔德先生，是她亡母的影子。我为我这莉诺糊涂，你不必奇怪。十八个月来，我没有行使过我的这种权柄，把她导入通灵恍惚之态——随它叫什么态吧——在那态里，她手中执一条线头，能一路解开比美人闺阁更迷乱的迷宫。我郑重告诉你：倘若这样做了，她能指给我一坛坛的金子、钻石矿的秘密，我也绝不肯再耗损她那点正在积蓄的元气，去冒她毫发之险。然而——你引我注意的这桩惨案，我是这样关切；这谜局的揭晓，我自信已在门槛上；而我认定，把它的赤裸真相摆到那些受这桩罪戕害的人面前，是一桩义举——因此我决意让莉诺再入一次通灵之态，去查明丽西·沙利文的藏身之处；叫你来，就是要你见证结果。"
这一宣布，把我剩下的一点胃口也收走了。伯顿先生摇铃撤了托盘，又嘱仆人告诉莉诺小姐，一用完午饭就到书房来。我们只等了几分钟。不久，轻快的脚步到了；她敲门，她父亲应一声"进来！"，一个可爱的小人儿走进门来，见了我的神气半是优雅半是羞怯——那份甜美与秀美，比我预想的更完全。金色的头发泛着亮亮的小卷，披拂在她纤细的颈边。

===Original===

Seldom do we see such hair, except upon the heads of infants﻿—soft, lustrous, fine, floating at will, and curled at the end in little shining rings. Her eyes were a celestial blue﻿—celestial, not only because of the pure heavenliness of their color, but because you could not look into them without thinking of angels. Her complexion was the most exquisite possible, fair, with a flush as of sunset-light on the cheeks﻿—too transparent for perfect health, showing the wandering of the delicate veins in the temples. Her blue dress, with its fluttering sash, and the little jacket of white cashmere which shielded her neck and arms, were all dainty, and in keeping with the wearer. She did not have the serene air of a seraph, though she looked like one; nor the listless manner of an invalid. She gave her father a most winning, childish smile, looking full of joy to think he was at home, and had sent for her. She was so every way charming that I held out my arms to kiss her, and she, with the instinct of children, who perceive who their real lovers are, gave me a willing yet shy embrace. Mr. Burton looked pleased as he saw how satisfactory was the impression made by his Lenore.
Placing her in a chair before him, he put a photograph of Miss Sullivan in her hand.
“Father wants to put his little girl to sleep again,” he said, gently.
An expression of unwillingness just crossed her face; but she smiled, instantly, looking up at him with the faith of affection which would have placed her life in his keeping, and said, “Yes, papa,” in assent.
He made a few passes over her; when I saw their effect, I did not wonder that he shrunk from the experiment﻿—my surprise was rather that he could be induced to make it, under any circumstances. The lovely face became distorted as with pain; the little hands twitched﻿—so did the lips and eyelids. I turned away, not having fortitude to witness anything so jarring to my sensibilities. When I looked again, her countenance had recovered its tranquillity; the eyes were fast closed, but she appeared to ponder upon the picture which she held.
“Do you see the person now?”
“Yes, papa.”
“In what kind of a place is she?”
“She is in a small room; it has two windows. There is no carpet on the floor. There is a bed and a table, a stove and some chairs. It is in the upper story of a large brick house, I do not know in what place.”
“What is she doing?”

===Chinese===

这样的头发实在少见，只婴儿头上有——柔、亮、细，随意飘拂，发梢卷成一圈圈闪光的小环。她的眼睛是天蓝——说天蓝，不只因其色泽纯净如天，更因你一望进去，便不由得想起天使。肤色再精致不过，白皙，两颊透着一线落日似的红——透亮得过了健康的地步，看得见太阳穴上细血管的游走。蓝色的连衣裙，飘着腰带；白色开司米小外衣护着她的脖颈手臂；处处精巧，处处配她这个人。她虽像个六翼小天使，却没有天使那种安详；也没有病孩那种恹恹。她给父亲一个极动人的、孩子气的笑，想到他在家、又打发人来叫她，满脸是欢喜。她处处这样讨人喜欢，我忍不住张开双臂去亲她；她也怀着孩子们识得谁是真心爱他们的本能，又情愿又害羞地抱了我一下。伯顿先生看见他的莉诺给人的印象如此圆满，面有得色。
他把她安置在面前的椅子里，把沙利文小姐的照片放进她手中。
"爸爸要哄小闺女睡觉了。"他温存地说。
一丝不情愿刚掠过她的脸；她随即笑了，仰头望着他，那眼神里的信爱，是把性命也交他保管的信爱，应了一声："好，爸爸。"
他在她头上比划了几下；我一见那效力，便不奇怪他何以下不得手了——我奇怪的倒是，这种情形之下，怎么还劝得动他一试。那张可爱的脸痛得变了形；小手抽描——嘴唇和眼皮也是。我别过脸去，没有勇气看这样折磨我感官的景象。再看时，她的面容已复归宁静；眼睛闭得紧紧的，人却仿佛在端详手里那张照片。
"你现在看见这个人了？"
"看见了，爸爸。"
"她在一处什么样的地方？"
"她在一间小屋里；有两扇窗。地上没有地毯。有一张床、一张桌子、一个炉子和几把椅子。在一所大砖房的楼上，我不知道是什么地方。"
"她在做什么？

===Original===

“She is sitting near the back window; it looks out on the roofs of other houses; she is holding a pretty little child on her lap.”
“She must be in the city,” remarked Mr. Burton, aside; “the large house and the congregated roofs would imply it. Can you not tell me the name of the street?”
“No, I can not see it. I was never in this place before. I can see water, as I look out of the window. It appears like the bay; and I see plenty of ships, but there is some green land across the water, besides distant houses.”
“It must be somewhere in the suburbs, or in Brooklyn. Are there no signs on the shops, which you can read, as you look out?”
“No, papa.”
“Well, go down the stairs, and out upon the street, and tell me the number of the house.”
“It is No. —,” she said, after a few moments’ silence.
“Go along until you come to a corner, and read me the name of the street.”
“Court Street,” she answered, presently.
“It is in Brooklyn,” exclaimed the detective, triumphantly. “There is nothing now to prevent us going straight to the spot. Lenore, go back now, to the house; tell us on which floor is this room, and how situated.”
Again there was silence while she retraced her steps.
“It is on the fourth floor, the first door to the left, as you reach the landing.”
Lenore began to look weary and exhausted; the sweat broke out on her brow, and she panted as if fatigued with climbing flights of stairs. Her father, with a regretful air, wiped her forehead, kissing it tenderly as he did so. A few more of those cabalistic touches, followed by the same painful contortions of those beautiful features, and Lenore was herself again. But she was pale and languid; she drooped against her father’s breast, as he held her in his arms, the color faded from her cheeks, too listless to smile in answer to his caresses. Placing her on the sofa, he took from a nook in his secretary a bottle of old port, poured out a tiny glassful, and gave to her. The wine revived her almost instantly; the smiles and bloom came back, though she still seemed exceedingly weary.
“She will be like a person exhausted by a long journey, or great labor, for several days,” said Mr. Burton, as I watched the child. “It cost me a pang to make such a demand upon her; I hope it will be the last time﻿—at least until she is older and stronger than now.”

===Chinese===

"她坐在靠后窗的地方；窗外望见别人家的屋顶；她腿上抱着一个漂亮的小娃娃。"
"那她必是在城里。"伯顿先生侧头说；"大砖房，屋顶挤成片，都说明是城里。你能不能告诉我街名？"
"不能，我看不见。我从来没到过这个地方。往窗外望，我望得见水。像海湾；还有好多船；可水那边有一片绿地，还有远处的房子。"
"那必是城郊，或是在布鲁克林。往外看，可有店铺招牌，念得出字来？"
"没有，爸爸。"
"那么，下楼梯，到街上，告诉我门牌号。"
沉默片刻，她说："是某号。"（门牌号从略。）
"往前走到街角，把街名念给我。"
"法院街。"她随即答道。
"在布鲁克林！"侦探得胜般地扬声说。"这下再没有什么拦着我们直奔那地方了。莉诺，现在回到那所房子去；告诉我们这间屋在几楼，位置怎样。"
她循原路回去，又是一阵沉默。
"在四楼，上到楼梯口，左手第一扇门。"
莉诺显出倦色，憔悴起来；额上沁出汗珠，喘着气，像爬了几层楼爬乏了。她父亲满脸痛惜，替她拭了额，还轻轻吻了吻。又是几下那秘术般的抚触，又是那副美丽眉目一阵痛楚的抽搐，莉诺醒过来了。可她苍白无力；伏在父亲怀里，颊上的颜色褪尽了，懒懒得连回应他的爱抚都笑不动。他把她安放在沙发上，从书柜秘书匣的暗格里取出一瓶陈年波特酒，斟了顶小的一盏给她。酒几乎立刻唤回了她；笑靥和红晕回来了，虽说仍是一副倦极的样子。
"她要像一个长途跋涉或出过大力的人那样，蔫上好几天。"伯顿先生看着孩子，对我说。"这样支使她，我心口像割了一刀；但愿这是末一回——至少等她比现在大些、壮些再说。"
