When I went into Mr. Argyll’s office that morning, he greeted me with marked coldness. At last I could not conceal from myself that, not only had his manner changed, but that he wished me to feel that it had. He gave me, as I entered, a searching, suspicious glance, saying, “Good morning, Richard,” in the most formal tone. Nothing further. I took up a book, hiding my pain and embarrassment in an attempt to read; but my mind was not on the legal difficulties expounded therein; I was wondering at the causes of the situation in which I found myself. A hanger-on! yes, an unwelcome hanger-on in an office where I no longer had any conceded rights﻿—in a home where I was no longer trusted.
“Has Mr. Argyll placed a spy on my actions? Does he know already that I was out the entire night? and does he judge me before he has an explanation?” I asked myself, indignantly. “If he thinks I am forming bad habits, doing wrong in any respect, why does he not remonstrate with me﻿—give me a chance to defend myself?”
I had intended to take his advice in the matter of the haunted house; but now I sat, angry and silent, feeling, oh, so wounded and forlorn. I did not stay long in the office; going to my room, I wrote a long letter to my mother, telling her I should come soon to pay her the visit which should have been sooner made had I not been engrossed with the duty to which I had vowed myself.
Yes!

===Chinese===

恰恰是我这份沉默，使他们确信我见了什么解不开的东西；两人又是惊，又是得意。他们不敢多问——过于敬重我——我只向他们担保：我有理由同他们一样认为，别墅需要照看了。是谁在搅扰那地方，我还没能查明；但查不明白我誓不罢休。当夜我要回别墅睡；我要悄无声息地进去，多半趁天黑之前，免得惊动里头的住客——不论一位还是几位；我有把握，这样便能一把扑住那只鬼。斯科特太太望着我，佩服得五体投地，又怕得要命。
"所罗门王的金山银海全给她，她也断不肯去那宅子里独自睡一宿"；再说，我难道不该至少备一支手枪？
那天早晨我走进阿盖尔先生的事务所，他招呼我的语气冷得刺眼。到这一步，我再瞒不了自己：不仅他的态度变了，而且他存心要叫我觉出这变化。我一进门，他便投来一道审视的、狐疑的目光，用最公事公办的腔调说了句"早安，理查德"。再没有下文。我拿起一本书，把疼痛与窘迫藏进佯装的阅读里；可我的心根本不在书中铺陈的那些法律疑难上；我正在琢磨，自己怎么会落进眼前这般境地。一个依附者！是的，一个不再受欢迎的依附者——在一间我已不再有公认权利的事务所里，在一个不再信任我的家里。
"阿盖尔先生是不是派了人盯我的行止？他莫非已经知道我整夜在外？还没听我分说，就先判了我的罪？"我愤愤地自问，"他若疑我染了恶习、哪一头走了错路，为什么不同我直谈——给我一个自辩的机会？"
我本打算就闹鬼的宅子听听他的主意；可此刻我坐着，一腔怒火，一言不发，只觉得——哦，那样受伤，那样孤零。我在事务所待了不久便出来，回房给母亲写了一封长信，说我不日便去看她——要不是被我自己许下的那桩义务缠住，这趟探望本该早成行的。
是的！

===Original===

I had pledged my own heart to devote myself to the discovery of Henry Moreland’s murderer; and if Eleanor herself had put her foot on that heart, and crushed it yet more, I do not know that I should have held my vow absolved.
I should not have gone to the mansion that day, had not a message been sent, late in the afternoon, that Mr. Burton had arrived, and expected me to meet him at tea. I went; and had the pleasure of seeing little Lenore enthroned by the side of James, who attended upon her as if she were a princess, and of being treated with bare civility by all save Mr. Burton. Miss Argyll was ill, and did not come down.
I saw the observant eye of Mr. Burton watching the intimacy between his daughter and her new friend; whether he was pleased or not, I could not decide; the eye which read the secret thoughts of other men did not always betray its own impressions. I was certain, too, that he observed the change in the demeanor of the family toward me, and my own constrained manner.

===Chinese===

我已把自己的心许给一桩誓愿：查出杀害亨利·莫尔兰的凶手；哪怕踩着这颗心的是埃莉诺本人，把它又踩重了一层，我也不敢说这誓愿便可以勾销。
那天我本不会再去那座宅邸，若不是傍晚时分有人捎来口信：伯顿先生到了，盼我去共进晚茶。我去了；得见小莉诺高踞在詹姆斯身边——詹姆斯侍候她如同侍候一位公主——也领受了除伯顿先生之外、全家待我的那种仅存客套。阿盖尔小姐欠安，没有下楼。
我看见伯顿先生那双善察的眼睛，注视着他女儿同新朋友的亲昵；他乐意与否，我断不出；那双读得旁人心思的眼睛，并不总肯泄露自家的印象。我也确信，他察觉了全家人待我的变化，也察觉了我自己的拘谨。

## XII The Night in Moreland Villa / 十二 莫尔兰别墅之夜
===Original===

Mr. Burton’s arrival prevented my fulfilling the intention of sleeping at Moreland villa that night; I immediately resolved to defer my explorations until he could keep me company. The next day he came to my room, and we had, as usual when we met, a long talk over things past, present and to come. I did not introduce the subject of the mystery at the villa until we had discussed many other matters. My companion was preoccupied with important business of his own﻿—the same which had taken him to Boston; but his interest was pledged, almost as earnestly as mine, to unmask the criminal of the Blankville tragedy, and any reference to that sad subject was sure to secure his attention. Baffled we acknowledged ourselves, as we talked together that morning, but not discouraged. Mr. Burton told me that he was on the track of two five-hundred-dollar bills of the Park Bank, which had left the city the week after the murder, taking widely-different flights; there had one come back from St. Louis, whose course his agents were tracing. As for the sewing-girl, she had the power of vanishing utterly, like a light extinguished, leaving no trace behind, and her pursuers literally in the dark. This comparison of the detective reminded me of the curious light which had led me, like a Jack-o’-lantern, into a quagmire of uncertainty; I was about to begin my account of it, when he gave me one of those peculiar piercing looks of his, saying,
“You have not yet entered into the contemplated partnership?”
“No, Mr. Burton; and I hardly think now that I shall.”
There was some bitterness in my tone; he evinced no surprise, asking, simply,
“Why?”
“I think James has been chosen to fill the place.”
“But, he has not been admitted to the bar.”
“He is studying a little recently; probably in order to pass an examination.”
“The wind is changing,” said Mr. Burton, speaking like the old gentleman in Bleak House. “I see how the land lies. The goodly and noble Argyll ship is driving on to the rocks. Mark my words, she will go to pieces soon! you will see her ruins strewing the shore.”
“I pray heaven to avert your prophecy. I hope not to live to see any such sight.”
“How can it be otherwise?” he exclaimed, rising and pacing to and fro through my little room, like a caged elephant. “A spendthrift and a gambler﻿—a man like *that*﻿—about to have the helm put in his hands!

===Chinese===

伯顿先生来了，我在莫尔兰别墅过夜探察的打算便落了空；我当即决定，把探察推后，等他能陪我同去。第二天他到我房里来；我们照例一见面，就把过去、现在、将来长谈了一番。直到别的话说得差不多了，我才引到别墅那桩谜案上。我的同伴正被他自己一桩要紧事务缠着——也就是那趟去波士顿的事；可是对揭开布兰克维尔惨案凶手的面目，他的心几乎同我一样系得深；凡是提到那桩伤心事，准能赢得他的注意。那天上午我们交谈时，都承认自己是碰了壁，却并不气馁。伯顿先生告诉我，他正在追查公园银行的两张五百美元钞票；凶案发生后一礼拜，这两张票子便离开了本城，各奔东西；其中一张从圣路易斯流了回来，他的探员们正一路循迹追索。至于那个缝衣姑娘，她却有本事消失得干干净净，像一盏灯灭了一样，不留半点痕迹，把她那些追捕者实实在在地丢在黑地里。侦探这个比方，叫我想起那道引着我走的古怪光——像一盏引路的鬼火，把我引进了一片泥沼般的狐疑里。我正要开口讲这件事，他却给了我一个他那种锐利的特别眼色，说：
「你还没有入伙，进他们说定了的那家合伙事务所？」
「没有，伯顿先生；如今我简直不打算入了。」
我的话音里带着几分苦味；他并不显出惊讶，只简单地问：
「为什么？」
「我想，位置是留给詹姆斯的了。」
「可他还没有取得律师资格。」
「他近来稍稍用起功来；多半是为了应考。」
「风头转了，」伯顿先生说，口气活像《荒凉山庄》里的老绅士。「我看清了地势。阿盖尔那条堂堂大船，正对着礁石开过去。记住我的话，她不久就要撞得粉碎！你将看见她的残骸撒满海岸。」
「我求上天别叫你的预言应验。我不愿活着见到那样的光景。」
「怎么能不是这样？」他嚷起来，站起身，在我那间小屋里来回踱步，像一头笼中的象。「一个败家子，一个赌棍——*那样*一个人——舵要交到他手里了！」

===Original===

But it’s none of my business﻿—none of my business; nor much yours, either.”
“It is mine!” I cried; “I can not help but make it mine, as if these girls were my sisters, and Mr. Argyll my father. Yet, as you say﻿—it is, indeed, nothing to me. They will not allow it to be!”
I drooped my head on my arms; my own loss and disappointment were receding into the background before the idea of their possible discomfiture. I was startled by the detective bringing his clenched hand down upon the table with a blow which shook it; he was standing, looking not at me, but at the wall, as if he saw someone before him, invisible to me.
“James Argyll is a singular man﻿—a *singular* man! A person ought to be a panther in cunning and strength to cope with him. By George, if I don’t look out, he’ll overreach me yet﻿—with that will of his. I see everybody about me succumbing. He’s having the game all in his own hands. By the way, Redfield, I was a little surprised to see Lenore so fond of him.”
“Why so, Mr. Burton? James is an attractive, elegant young man; he has never had any lack of admirers. It would rather have been strange if your daughter had *not* fancied him. He has been very good to her.”
“He has, indeed; I’m sure I ought to be greatly obliged to all of you. Did I ever tell you that I place great confidence in Lenore’s intuitive perception of character? You know that I have a remarkable gift that way myself. When I meet people, I seem to see their minds, and not their bodies﻿—I can’t help it. Well, I’ve remarked the same thing in my child. She is so young and inexperienced that she can not explain her own impressions; she has her instantaneous partialities, and I have noticed that she leans toward true natures like a flower toward the light, and away from the false as if they were shadows. I hardly expected she would be so intimate with young Argyll.”
I remembered the curious effect his first address had made upon her; but I did not repeat it to her father. I was sensitive about appearing in any manner jealous of James; if he could win my friends from me, even that little girl whom I had loved for her pure sweetness, let them go! I was too proud to solicit them to reconsider their opinions.
“Do you know,” continued my companion, “he is performing a marvel with my little Lenore? He has gained a great ascendancy over her in these few days.

===Chinese===

「可是这不关我的事——不关我的事；也不大关你的事。」
「关我的事！」我叫道；「我没法不把它当自己的事，就好像这两个姑娘是我的妹妹，阿盖尔先生是我的父亲。可也正像你说的——它实在同我不相干。人家也不许它同我相干！」
我垂下头，伏在臂上；比起我自己可能遭的挫败，他们那家子可能的倾覆，把我自己的失意挤到了后头。侦探忽然搜起拳头，一拳捶在桌上，震得桌子直晃，吓了我一跳；他站着，眼睛却不望我，只望着墙，仿佛看见了我看不见的什么人。
「詹姆斯·阿盖尔是个不寻常的人——一个*不寻常*的人！要同他较量，人得有豹子的狡黠和力气才成。天哪，我要不当心，他连我也要算计过去——凭他那一股意志。我眼看身边的人一个个屈服。他把整盘棋都搜在自己手里了。对了，雷德菲尔德，看见莉诺这样喜欢他，我倒有点奇怪。」
「这有什么奇怪的，伯顿先生？詹姆斯是个招人喜欢的、风度翩翩的青年；他从来不缺爱慕的人。您女儿若*不*喜欢他，那反倒怪了。他待她一直很好。」
「的确很好；我自然该对你们大家感激不尽。我从前告诉过你没有？我对莉诺看人的直觉，是很信服的。你知道，我自己在这一门上有一种了不起的天赋。我见了人，仿佛看见的是他们的心思，而不是他们的身子——不由自主。后来我在我孩子身上也看出了同样的东西。她年岁小、历练浅，说不清自己的印象；她有一阵阵立时生出的亲疏好恶；我留意到，她亲近真实的天性，像花朝着光；躲避虚假的，像躲避影子。我原没有想到，她会同小阿盖尔这样亲热。」
我想起初见他时他那番话在她身上引起的古怪反应；可我没有对她父亲提起。我太敏感，不愿露出半点嫉妒詹姆斯的样子；倘若他能把我的朋友们从我身边夺去——连那个我因她纯净的甜美而疼爱的小姑娘——那就随他们去吧！我太骄傲，不肯去求他们重新掂量对我的看法。
「你知道吗，」我的同伴接着说，「他正在我那小莉诺身上行一件奇迹？几天工夫，他就取得了支配她的巨大威权。」

===Original===

This morning, for a purpose which you will realize I considered highly important, I endeavored, alone with her in my own apartment, to place her in the clairvoyant state. For the first time, I failed. Her mind is no longer a pellucid mirror, reflecting truths without color or refraction. She is under the influence of a counter-will, as strong as my own﻿—and mine moves mountains,” he added, with a laugh.
“I shouldn’t think you would like it.”
“I don’t; but she is going home tomorrow. I will tell you why I wished to procure Lenore’s aid again. I have succeeded in tracing Leesy Sullivan to this village. She came here the day after we frightened her from Brooklyn﻿—that is, she got off the cars at a little station about six miles from here, not daring to land at this depot, and, I have no doubt, started on foot for Blankville, coming here in the night.”
“That aunt of hers is in the work,” I exclaimed. “We are justified in taking any step to compel her to own up where she conceals that girl.”
“I am convinced that her aunt knows nothing whatever about her. Has Mrs. Scott kept a sharp lookout at the villa?”
“She has not seen her since that first day; and I believe it would be difficult for her to set her foot on the place without being discovered, for the woman has got it into her head that the place is haunted, and she is on guard night and day.”
“Haunted?”
Mr. Burton sat down and drew up his chair with an appearance of interest, which led me to recount our experiences at the villa, and my intention of completing my researches that night, in his company, if he had no objection. He said, “Of course; it would give him pleasure; he liked nothing better than an adventure of the kind.”
In fact, the idea evidently pleased him immensely; his face brightened, and after that, for the rest of the day, for the first time in our brief acquaintance, I saw him a little flurried and expectant. One of his mottoes was:

> “Learn to labor, and to *wait*.”

===Chinese===

「今天早晨，为了一个目的——你听下去便会明白，那目的在我看来极其要紧——我独自同她在自己房里，想法把她导入通灵恍惚之态。这可是头一回，我失手了。她的心思不再是那面澄澈的镜子，无色无折地映照真实。她受着一股逆反意志的影响，那意志同我的一样强——我的意志，可是移山的，」他笑着添了一句。
「我想您不会乐意这样。」
「我不乐意；可她明天就回家了。我告诉你我为什么想再借莉诺之力。我已经把丽西·沙利文追到这个村子了。我们把她从布鲁克林吓跑的第二天，她就到了这里——她是说，她在离这儿六英里的小站上下的车，不敢在本镇车站落脚；随后，我毫不怀疑，她连夜徒步，投奔布兰克维尔来了。」
「她那位姨妈也脱不了干系！」我叫道。「咱们满可以采取任何手段，逼她供出那姑娘藏在哪里。」
「我确信她姨妈对她的行踪一无所知。斯科特太太在别墅那边盯得紧吗？」
「打头一天起，她就再没见过她；而且我相信，那女人只要在宅子上露一露面，就瞒不过人：因为斯科特太太认准了那地方闹鬼，日夜都在提防。」
「闹鬼？」
伯顿先生坐下来，把椅子拉近，一脸兴致；我便把我们几番在别墅的经历讲了一遍，又讲了我想在当天夜里继续查探的打算——倘若他不嫌弃，就请他作伴。他说，当然愿意；这于他是桩乐事；再没有比这类冒险更合他胃口的了。
这话不假：这个念头显然叫他欢喜极了；他的脸亮了起来；从那时起，一整天里，在我们短短的交往中，我头一回看见他有些忙乱、有些翘盼。他的座右铭之一是：
「学会劳作，学会*等待*。」

===Original===

His was one of those minds which would have kept silence seven years, rather than speak a moment too soon; he was seldom in a hurry, no matter what was at stake; but the fancy for lying perdu in a haunted house, to “nab” a ghost, was a novelty in his detective experience, which inwardly amused him.
He smiled to himself more than once during the intervening hours. As soon as tea was over, we excused ourselves to the family, kissed Lenore, and, saying that Mr. Burton would stay with me all night, we took our departure. I left the conduct of the proceedings in his hands. When we reached the cottage, we found Mrs. Scott disposed to regard the non-fulfillment of my engagement on the previous night as proof that I was frightened from the pursuit; she accepted my excuse, however, and highly approved of my having a companion in the spiritual dangers which I was about to encounter. She made us, moreover, some of her excellent coffee, to aid us in keeping awake, and gave us her prayers for our protection along with the keys of the house.
“Treat a ghost as you would any other burglar,” said my companion, as we approached the villa, in the darkness, by the back entrance. “Steal a march on him if you can.”
It was a wild night for an enterprise like ours. It reminded me of that night upon which Henry Moreland was murdered. One of those sudden changes in the weather, common to our climate, had been transpiring through the day, and now the warm, wild wind which brings in the “January thaw,” was blowing about the place, making every loose board creak, and rubbing the bare branches of the trees against each other with a grating sound. Black clouds, with ragged edges, skurried along the air, with the large stars looking down between, with wide, bright eyes, as of fear. While we stood outside, the great drops began to patter down; and presently it was raining violently, as it rained *that* night. As gently as if he were a robber making a felonious entrance, Mr. Burton turned the key in the lock; we entered the thick darkness of the house, closed the door, and stole noiselessly, I taking the lead, along the stairs and corridors, until we came to Henry’s room. This we entered, and, finding chairs, sat down upon either side the little table in absolute silence.

===Chinese===

他是那样一种性子：宁可七年不开口，不肯早说半个时辰；无论押上什么，他都难得着急；可是蹲伏在一座闹鬼的宅子里、好捉住一个鬼，这念头在他侦探的生涯里是桩新鲜事，暗暗地叫他觉得有趣。
中间那几个钟头里，他不只一次独自微笑。晚茶一过，我们向一家人告了便，亲了亲莉诺，说伯顿先生要同我共宿一夜，便动身了。行动的指挥，我全交在他手里。到了那所小屋，斯科特太太见我头天夜里失了约，几乎认定我是叫这桩差事吓退了；不过她还是受了托辞，而且极赞成我在即将来临的灵界凶险里带个同伴。她又给我们煮了她那顶好的咖啡，好助我们熬夜，又把她的祈祷，连同宅子的钥匙，一并交给了我们。
「待鬼，就像待任何别的贼一样，」我们摸黑从后门走近别墅时，我的同伴说。「能偷袭他，就偷袭他。」
对我们这样一场营生，那是个狂暴之夜。它叫我想起亨利·莫尔兰遇害的那一夜。我们这里常见的天气骤变，闹了一整天；此刻，那带来「一月解冻」的暖热的野风，正绕着这宅子刮，刮得每一块松动的木板吱嘎作响，刮得光秃秃的树枝彼此摩擦，发出刺耳的声音。黑云带着破絮似的边，在天上疾奔；大颗的星星从云隙间往下望，睜着又大又亮的眼睛，像是害怕。我们站在外面的时候，大颗的雨点开始啪嗒啪嗒落下；不多时便下起了瓢泼大雨，同*那*一夜下得一样。伯顿先生转动钥匙开了锁，轻巧得像个撬门行窃的贼；我们走进宅子里浓重的黑暗，关上门，蹑手蹑脚地上楼、穿廊——我在前头引路——一直走到亨利的房间。我们进了屋，摸着椅子，在那张小桌两边坐下，一言不发。

===Original===

But we might safely have knocked over half the furniture without giving alarm to any inmate﻿—had there been an inmate of the room or villa﻿—such a tremendous uproar was now made by the elements. As the rain dashed fitfully against the windows, and the wind shook the solitary building, I was nearly overpowered with the memories which the place and the storm so vivified. I was in a fit mood to become a convert to a nocturnal specter﻿—in that hour of gloom and tempest, under the roof of the murdered, the material world seemed not so far removed from the awful and shadowy confines of the spiritual, as it appeared in the common routine of daylight life. As my heart thumped loudly with the agitation of feelings almost too powerful for mortal endurance, I was glad to consider that my companion was cool, calm and vigilant. He had no such memories of the wind and rain to overwhelm him as I had; this roof was not the roof of his friend﻿—he did not know Eleanor.
It was rather impressive to the dullest imagination to be sitting there at night, in that empty mansion, in the darkness, with the storm beating around it, waiting for﻿—we knew not what. To me, with my ardent temperament, and under the peculiar circumstances, it was exciting in the highest degree.
For a long time there was but one interruption to our silent watch. Mr. Burton leaned over the table, whispering,
“Did you hear someone singing?”
“I heard nothing but the wind, and the creaking of a tree against the side of the house, except the rain, that I would be sure of. Hark!”
I did think I heard a soft, angelic note of music swelling in the air above me, but at that moment the tempest redoubled its clamor, beating out all lesser sounds.
“Unless I am mistaken, there was a human voice,” he continued, in the same whisper.
“Or a heavenly one,” I murmured.
I believe Mr. Burton said “nonsense!” but I am not certain. Again there was a long interval of waiting; we both leaned over toward each other at the same instant, as the sound of something shoved overhead attracted our attentive ears.
“It is rats in the garret,” said I. “Mrs. Scott says they are in the house.”
“I hardly think it was rats; but we will wait a while.”
Mr. Burton had brought a lamp and matches, so that we could have a light when we wished it; if we heard anything more overhead, I knew he would examine the attic.

===Chinese===

其实，哪怕我们掀翻半屋子家具，也惊不着宅里任何人——倘若这房间、这别墅里还有什么人的话——因为此刻风雨大作，喧腾得了不得。雨一阵阵扑打在窗上，风摇撼着孤零零的宅子；此情此景，把往事唤活得那样鲜明，我几乎撑持不住。在那昏天黑地、风狂雨骤的时辰，置身于被害人的屋顶之下，物质世界同灵界那可怖而幽暗的疆界，仿佛不像白日寻常生涯里显得的那样遥远——我简直要皈依夜半的鬼魂了。我的感情激荡得几乎非血肉之躯所能承受，心口怦怦直撞；所幸我的同伴冷静、沉着、警醒。他没有我这样关于风雨的惨痛记忆；这屋顶不是他朋友的屋顶——他不认识埃莉诺。
夜里坐在那座空宅里，坐在黑暗中，风暴在四围抽打，等着一桩——我们也不知道是什么——的事：即便最迟钝的想象，也不能不为之动容。至于我，天生一副热烈性子，又处在这样特殊的境地里，更是兴奋到了极点。
好一阵子，我们默默的守望只有过一次打断。伯顿先生朝桌上俯过身来，低声道：
「你听见有人唱歌没有？」
「除了风声，除了一棵树蹭着屋子侧板的声音，我没听见什么拿得准的——雨声不算的话。听！」
那一霎我真觉得听见一个轻柔的、天使般的音符在头顶的空气里升起；可就在那时，暴风雨加倍喧嚣起来，把一切细小的声音都盖了过去。
「除非我弄错了，那是人的嗓音，」他仍旧低声说。
「要不，是天上的，」我喃喃道。
我记得伯顿先生像说了句「胡说！」可拿不准。又是一段长长的等待；头顶上有什么东西挪动的声响，同时引得我们两人侧过耳朵。
「是阁楼里的老鼠，」我说。「斯科特太太说宅子里有老鼠。」
「我不大信是老鼠；不过再等等看。」
伯顿先生带了一盏灯和一盒火柴，想用光的时候便有光；倘若头顶再有什么动静，我知道他会去查那间阁楼。

===Original===

There was a lull in the rain; as we sat expectant, the pushing sound was shortly followed by a light, regular patter, as of soft footsteps, along the floor of the garret. I had heard rats make precisely similar sounds traversing a ceiling; and though my heart beat a little faster, I was still quite certain it was these troublesome vermin.
The next thing which fixed our attention was a glimmer of light. I think the most spectral visitant could hardly have affected me as did that sudden ray of light, shooting through the keyhole and under the bottom of the door. Silently it crept along over the carpet, moving as if the object which threw it was carried in the hand of a person walking. I do not know exactly what I did expect when it paused in front of the door, except that the door would open, and I should see﻿—the mystery. An instant of suspense﻿—then the flickering light wavered and moved around to the opposite angle from that at which it had first appeared﻿—it was going through the corridor and down the stairs.
“All right,” breathed my companion, in a scarcely audible whisper. “Wait!”
The hand which he laid on my own was cold with excitement. As the last yellow gleam trembled and disappeared, the elements conspired in a grand attack upon our citadel; we could hear nothing but the roar of their artillery﻿—the tramp of their battalions. We waited perhaps five minutes.
“Now,” and I arose, following Mr. Burton through the darkness, as he silently opened the door, crossed the corridor, and, leaning over the railing, looked down into the lower hall. We could see nothing, until, as we descended the stairs, a faint effulgence from some distant room penetrated the obscurity. With cautious steps we followed it up through the hall and library, to the family-room, from which, it will be recollected, Mrs. Scott assured me she had heard mysterious noises. The door was open a little distance, but not sufficiently to give us a view of the interior. As we paused on the threshold, we heard a sigh﻿—a deep, long-drawn, tremulous sigh. With a deft hand my companion pushed the door ajar, so that we could step in, and we both silently entered. This room, in summer, was the favorite sitting-room of Mrs. Moreland; and here, upon the walls, she had the portraits, life-size, in oil, of her little family. In front of us, as we stepped in, hung the likeness of Henry Moreland.

===Chinese===

雨势歇了一歇；我们屏息坐候，那推挪之声过后不久，又起了轻轻的、规律的啪嗒声，像一双软底鞋的脚，沿着阁楼的地板走过。我从前听见过老鼠在天花板上跑出分毫不差这样的声响；虽然我的心跳快了些，我还是认定，是这些作祟的小东西。
接下来搜住我们注意的，是一线灯光。我想，哪怕最凶的鬼魅显形，也不及那突然射来的一线光叫我震动：它从锁孔里、从门底下钻进来，悄悄地在地毯上爬，随着移动，仿佛投下它的是一只提灯行走的手。它在门前停住的时候，我不知道自己到底指望着什么，只知道门要开了，我将看见——那个谜。一霎悬念——随即那摇曳的光晃了晃，转到了它初现时对面的墙角——它正穿过走廊，下楼去了。
「好，」我的同伴用几乎听不见的气声说。「等一等！」
他按在我手上的那只手，兴奋得冰凉。当最后一线黄光颤了颤、灭了，风雨合力朝我们这座堡垒发起了总攻；我们什么也听不见，只听见它们炮队的轰鸣、队伍的踏步。我们等了约莫五分钟。
「走，」他起身，我跟着伯顿先生摸黑前行；他轻轻开门，穿过走廊，俯在栏杆上，朝下面的门厅张望。什么也看不见；直到我们下到楼梯中段，某个远处房间里透出的一点微光，才刺破黑暗。我们蹑着脚步，循光穿过门厅和书房，来到那间家人起居的屋子——读者当还记得，斯科特太太就是在那里听见神秘声响的。门开着一道缝，却不足以让我们望见屋内。我们在门槛上站定时，听见一声叹息——一声悠长的、抽丝般的、颤巍巍的叹息。我的同伴手底利落，把门推开一些，容我们进去；我们两个悄然入内。这间屋子，夏天是莫尔兰太太最爱坐的起坐间；这里，四面墙上，挂着她的亲人——等身大的油画像。我们一进门，迎面便是亨利·莫尔兰的画像。

===Original===

Before it stood a woman, one hand holding aloft a lighted candle, in a small chamber-candlestick, the other pressed upon her heart, as if to keep down those painful signs. Motionless, rapt, absorbed she stood; we made no sound, and if we had, I do not think she would have heard us; her back was toward us; the light was thrown full on the picture upon which her gaze was bent.
The woman was Leesy Sullivan. I knew her at once, though her face was turned from us. Here, at last, we had found the fugitive we sought, haunting the home of the man of whose murder my thoughts accused her, standing before his portrait, in the dead of night, unwitting who were the witnesses of her secret, as she betrayed it now. How she had obtained access to the villa, or how long she had been its inmate, I left to future inquiry to develop﻿—the present scene was all-engrossing.
A long﻿—long﻿—long time she stood there; we did not interrupt her; it was probably the expectation that she would utter some soliloquy which would be of importance to us, as revealing what was on her mind, which kept my companion quiet. She said nothing, however; only drawing those deep sighs; until, at the last, she set the light on the little table beneath the picture, and, lifting up both hands with a passionate gesture toward it, sobbed one word﻿—“Henry!”
Then, slowly, as if her eyes refused to leave the object of their attraction, she began to turn away. We had one instant’s glance at her face before she discovered us; there was a burning spot upon either thin cheek, and two great tears, frozen, as it were, upon her eyelids; and a tremulous curve to the full, red lips of the tender and beautiful mouth, as if they quivered with grief and love. There was nothing wild or severe about her at that moment. Turning, slowly, she perceived us, standing there in the shadow﻿—two cruel men, hunting her even in this sacred solitude. That was the feeling she gave us by the look which passed over her countenance; I felt ashamed and unjustified until I forced myself to recollect all.
She did not scream; she had passed through too many vicissitudes to betray any fright; she only turned white, and put her hand on the table to steady herself.
“You two men have come here at last, have you? Why do you interfere with me? It’s only a little while I have to stay, and I want peace.”

===Chinese===

画像前站着一个女人，一手高举一支点着的蜡烛，插在小烛台上；另一只手按在胸口，仿佛要按住那里翻腾的痛楚。她站着，一动不动，全神贯注；我们没有出声；即便出了声，我想她也听不见；她背朝着我们；烛光正正照在那幅画像上，照着她的目光钉住的地方。
那女人是丽西·沙利文。虽然她背对着我们，我却一眼认出了她。我们苦苦搜寻的逃亡者，到底在这里找着了：她盘踞在这座宅子里——这座我心目中认定被她所害的人的宅子——深更半夜，立在他的画像前，浑然不知此刻是谁在见证她的秘密，而她正在泄露它。她是怎么进得别墅的，又做了多久的宅中人，我留给往后的查问——眼前这一幕，占去了我整个的人。
很久——很久——很久，她站在那里；我们不去搅扰；大约是盼着她吐出一段独白，叫我们知晓她心里的事——我的同伴才按捺不动。可她什么也没有说；只抽着那一声声长叹；直到末了，她把烛台放在画像底下的小桌上，朝画像扬起双手，做个热切的姿势，呜咽出一个字——「亨利！」
然后她缓缓地、仿佛两眼不肯离开它们眷恋的目标似的，开始转过身来。在她发现我们之前，我们有一霎看清了她的脸：瘦削的两颊各有一块烧红的斑，两大颗泪珠，仿佛冻结在眼睫上；那温柔秀美的嘴，饱满红润的唇上，一个颤抖的弧度，仿佛正为哀与爱而抖。那一刻，她身上没有一丝狂乱，也没有一丝严酷。她缓缓转身，看见了我们，站在阴影里的两个人——两个残忍的男人，追她追到这方圣洁的清静地。她脸上掠过的神色，给我们的就是这个感觉；我又羞愧、又觉得理亏，直到我强逼自己记起这一切。
她没有尖叫；她经历过的风浪太多了，不会流露惊惶；她只是变了脸色，一只手扶住桌子，站稳身子。
「你们两个男人终究寻到这儿来了，是不是？你们为什么不容我安生？我只剩一小会儿好待了，我要个清静。」

===Original===

“Peace only comes with a pure conscience,” said Mr. Burton, sternly. “What are you doing in this house?”
“I know I have no right here; but where else will you let me stay? Not even by his grave﻿—no, not even by his grave! You want to drag me forth before the world, to expose my foolish secret, which I have hidden from everybody﻿—to put me in prison﻿—to murder me! This is the business of you two men; and you have the power, I suppose. I am so poor and friendless it makes me a fit object for your persecution. Well, if you can justify yourselves, do as you will with me!”
She folded her hands, looking us full in the face with eyes which absolutely blazed.
“If you had no guilty secret, why did you fly from friends and enemies? Why did you not seek an interview and explanation which would have been satisfactory to us?” asked Mr. Burton.
“You would not believe me if I told you the reason,” scornfully. “It is not in the minds of men﻿—the gross, suspicious minds of men﻿—to conceive or credit my excuse. I will not make it to such people.”
Really, there was a majesty about the girl which quite awed me. As she confronted us, the undaunted spirit sparkling through her slight, wasted face and form, compelled a sort of acquiescence in me. I was not the one to subdue or handle this powerful nature. Mr. Burton was.
“This is not the proper hour, nor the proper place, to enter into explanations, Miss Sullivan. You must go with me to Mrs. Scott’s cottage; she will care for you until morning, and then we will have a talk together. You will not find me harsh; nor shall I take any step without good cause. All I want is the truth﻿—and that I am bound to have.”
“Let me stay here tonight; I promise you I will not attempt to leave the place. I will wait here until you see fit to come in the morning.”
“I can not; there is too much at stake,” he said, with determination.
“Then let me go and get the child,” she said.
She took up the lamp and we followed her; up and along the garret staircase, mounting the narrow steps which led into the attic. There, upon the pile of mattresses which I have mentioned as lying in the corner, reposed the baby girl before spoken of, sleeping sweetly, as only infancy can rest.
“We were under this when you paid us a visit the other day,” said Leesy, with a sort of bitter smile. “I had hard work to keep baby from crying out.

===Chinese===

「清静，只随洁净的良心而来，」伯顿先生严厉地说。「你在这宅子里做什么？」
「我知道我没有权利待在这儿；可你们还肯让我待在哪儿？连他的坟边也不肯——不，连他的坟边也不肯！你们要把我拖到世人面前，抖出我那桩傻气的秘密——我瞒过了所有人的——要把我下狱——要害死我！这就是你们两个男人的营生；谅必你们也有这个权柄。我又穷又无亲无故，正好做你们欺凌的份。好，你们问心无愧，那就随你们处置我吧！」
她交叠着双手，直直望定我们两个，眼睛里简直冒着火。
「你若没有亏心的秘密，为什么见了朋友也逃、见了仇人也逃？为什么不来见我们一面，把话说个明白？」伯顿先生问。
「我说了缘由，你们也不会信，」她轻蔑地说。「男人们的心——男人们粗鄙多疑的心——想不出、也不会信我的托辞。我不对这样的人说。」
说真的，这姑娘身上有一种威严，竟叫我生出敬畏。她同我们对峙着，那股无所畏惧的气性，透过她单薄憔悴的脸和身形闪出来，逼得我几分默许。降伏这股烈性、驾驭这股烈性的，不该是我。该是伯顿先生。
「现在不是时候，也不是地方，来解说这些，沙利文小姐。你得随我去斯科特太太的小屋；她会照看你到天亮，然后我们再细谈。你不会发现我苛刻；我没有充分的缘由，一步也不会乱走。我要的只是真相——真相，我势在必得。」
「让我今夜就留在这儿；我答应你们，我绝不试着离开这地方。我就在这儿等着，等你们明早过来。」
「不行；干系太大，」他斩钉截铁地说。
「那让我去把孩子抱来，」她说。
她端起灯，我们跟着她；上到阁楼的楼梯，蹬上通进阁楼的那几级窄梯。就在我先前提过的、堆在角落的那摞床垫上，卧着前文说过的那个女婴，睡得正甜，只有婴儿才睡得这样香。
「那天你们来查看的时候，我们就躲在这底下，」丽西带着一丝苦笑说。「我好容易才拦住宝宝不叫出声来。」

===Original===

She did make a fuss at last; you said it was a cat.”
“How sound the little creature sleeps,” said the detective. He had a gentle heart, which shrunk from disturbing the slumbering infant.
“It’s too bad to startle her up so,” murmured her nurse.
“Yes, it is. I’ll tell you what we will do. We will lock you up here, and keep guard in the chamber until morning, if that pleases you.”
“I don’t care to take Nora out in the storm.”
“Tell me one thing,” said Mr. Burton, his bright eye fixing itself on her own; “are you the mother of that babe?”
For a moment she answered his look with one of astonishment; then the rosy blood rushed up to neck, cheek and brow﻿—a virgin blush, which showed all the soft and girlish side of her character.
“Am I Nora’s mother?” she repeated. “I thought you knew I was not a married woman.”
The detective stood, a little embarrassed by the perfect simplicity of her reply.
“It is understood to be your deceased cousin’s child﻿—an orphan, I believe,” he said. “Well, Miss Sullivan, we will leave you here, undisturbed, for the remainder of the night.”
We descended to the second floor, turning the key of the little storeroom which enclosed the garret staircase, well satisfied to keep guard until morning, since we had secured the mysterious inmate of the haunted house.

===Chinese===

「她末了还是闹了一阵；你说是猫。」
「这小东西睡得多沉啊，」侦探说。他有一颗柔软的心，最不忍搅醒熟睡的婴儿。
「这样把她惊起来，实在太作孽，」她的保姆喃喃道。
「是作孽。这么办吧。我们把你锁在这儿，就在这间屋里守到天亮，这样你可愿意？」
「我不愿顶着这场风雨把诺拉带出去。」
「告诉我一件事，」伯顿先生说，他那双明亮的眼睛盯住了她的眼睛；「这婴孩，你是她的母亲吗？」
有一霎，她用惊讶的目光回望他；随后，玫瑰般的血涌上颈、颊和额头——一派处女的红晕，把她性情里柔婉的、少女的一面全露了出来。
「我是诺拉的母亲？」她重复道。「我以为你们知道，我不是个嫁过人的女人。」
侦探站在那里，被她这一答的坦然弄得有点窘。
「都说这是你亡故表妹的孩子——一个孤儿，我想，」他说。「好吧，沙利文小姐，今夜余下的时光，我们不再搅扰你。」
我们下到二层，把锁着阁楼楼梯的那间小储藏室的钥匙拧上；既然闹鬼宅子的神秘宅中人已经到手，守到天亮，我们心满意足。

## XIII The Shadow Assumes Shape / 十三 阴影成形
===Original===

We now lighted our lamp, and, finding a light cane sofa in the hall, nearly opposite the locked door, we took seats, and kept ourselves awake by talking. The storm had subsided into the monotonous patter of a steady rain.
“I am surprised,” said Mr. Burton, “that you did not at once comprehend the secret of this house. The moment you spoke the word ‘haunted,’ I knew how our investigations would end. It solved a mystery which has bothered me for some time. I knew that Leesy Sullivan was here, in this vicinity; the exact hiding-place was all I wanted to know; and when you mentioned Moreland villa, I said to myself, ‘that’s it!’ All I was then afraid of was, that she would again elude us, before we could lay hands on her. And in fact,” he added laughingly, “I hardly feel sure of her now. She may sublime through the ceiling before morning.”
“I did not think of her, Mr. Burton; I was quite sure some person was playing some game, either of mischief or worse, about the villa; but how could I be certain, when two thorough daylight examinations failed to reveal anything? There did not seem to be a place at which a person could enter the house; and as for a woman and child being actual inmates, living and subsisting here for weeks﻿—I think nothing but actual proof could have convinced me of the marvel. I am curious to know how she managed it.”
“I ought to have come right here at first,” continued my friend, pursuing his train of thought. “Women are like mother-birds, when boys approach the nest. They betray themselves and their cherished secret by fluttering about the spot. If this Miss Sullivan had been a man, she would have been in Kansas or California by this time; being a woman, I ought to have looked for her in exactly the place it would seem natural for her to avoid. One thing is certain﻿—she loved young Moreland with an intensity beyond the strength of most women. I have had to do with natures like hers before﻿—where a powerful brain is subservient to a still more powerful emotional force. She was proud, ambitious, discontented, with tastes and perceptions reaching up into a much higher sphere of life. Miss Sullivan would have made a magnificent heiress and pet daughter; yet in love she would be humble, self-abnegating﻿—give all and count it nothing. It’s a sad pity such a capacity for happiness should have brought only ruin.”

===Chinese===

我们点亮了灯，见门厅里有一张轻巧的藤沙发，正对着那扇上了锁的门，便坐下来，靠说话提神。风暴已经平息，化作一场绵密的雨，单调地敲打着。
“叫我奇怪的是，”伯顿先生说，“你竟没有立刻看破这宅子的秘密。你一说出‘闹鬼’两个字，我就知道咱们这趟查访会是什么结局了。它解开了烦扰我多时的一桩谜。我早知道丽西·沙利文就在这一带，缺的只是确切的藏身之处；你一提莫兰别墅，我心里就说：‘就是它了！’我当时唯一怕的，是还没等咱们拿住她，她又从指缝里溜掉。说实在的，”他笑着添了一句，“就是此刻我也不敢打包票。没准天亮以前，她还能穿过天花板升华走呢。”
“我没想到她身上，伯顿先生。我倒是一口咬定有人在别墅左近玩什么把戏——不是恶作剧，就是更坏的事；可两回彻头彻尾的白昼搜查都没查出一丝踪影，我怎能拿得准？这宅子看上去没有一处进得来人；至于说一个女人带着个孩子实打实住在这儿、在这儿过活了好几个星期——我想，若非亲眼见了实证，天大的奇迹我也信不了。她是怎么做到的，我真好奇。”
“我原该一开头就直奔这里。”我的朋友顺着他的思路接下去说。“女人同母鸟一样：孩子一挨近窝，她们便绕着那地方扑腾，把自己和心头的秘密一并泄露了。这位沙利文小姐若是个男人，这会儿早到了堪萨斯或加利福尼亚；正因为她是个女人，我就该到那个她照理最该躲开的地方去找她。有一件事是确定的——她爱小莫兰，爱得那样炽烈，寻常女人的心力受不住。她这路人我从前打过交道——一副强有力的头脑，臣服于一股更强大的情感之力。她骄傲，有野心，不知足，趣味和眼界都往上探进一层高得多的生活天地。沙利文小姐若是做了女继承人、父母的掌上明珠，必定出落得堂堂皇皇；可一入情场，她便卑微、忘我——把一切给出去，还当作算不得什么。这样的幸福容量，偏偏只招来毁灭，可惜呀可惜。”

===Original===

“If she had loved Henry, how could she, under any impulse of jealousy, have injured him? She is terrible to me in any view of the case.”
“I do not know that she did injure him, or cause him to be injured. Circumstances are against her. But I am far from believing her the guilty person. Yet I am exceedingly anxious to have a quiet interview with her. I must see her and talk with her alone. She is frightened now, and defiant. I shall soothe her﻿—magnetize her will, as it were﻿—and draw from her the truth. Every atom of knowledge which she has, in any way connected with Henry Moreland, I shall draw from her, and consolidate into one mass, to be used for or against her. If you have the reliance upon my judgment which I flatter myself you have, Richard, you will not object to my seeing Miss Sullivan alone, and deciding, upon that interview, whether there are causes for her arrest, as a party to the murder.”
“I shall not object. It is your privilege to see her alone; and I have the utmost confidence in you. I suppose Mr. Argyll and Henry’s father would be the proper persons to decide upon the arrest and prosecution.”
“Of course. And if, after I have talked with her, I can elicit no facts to warrant her being put on trial for her life, I shall not give her her liberty until I have consulted both families, laying all my evidence before them. They will be loth to begin a prosecution which they can not sustain, even if they have an *impression* of guilt. By the way, Redfield, these *impressions* are curious things! Supposing I should tell you there are persons who, without one particle of proof of any kind, have an impression that *you* are the guilty man.”
I arose from the sofa, looking at him, not knowing whether or not to knock him down.
“Don’t ‘slay me with a look,’ ” he said, laughing quietly. “I don’t say that *I* have any such inner revelation. And I did not say this, either, to hurt your feelings. I did it to save them. For, if I mistake not, the same person who confided his impressions to me, has recently been gradually confiding them to others. The very thought, the very possibility, once entertained, or half-entertained and driven away again, as an unwelcome guest, still has its injurious influence. You are standing upon an earthquake, Richard﻿—you may be swallowed up any instant.”
“I?”
“Yes. I have detected the premonitory rumblings.

===Chinese===

“她既爱着亨利，又怎么会出于妒火伤他呢？这件事无论从哪一面看，她在我眼里都可怕。”
“我并没有说她伤了他，或是教人伤了他。情形于她不利，可我远远谈不上信她是凶手。只是我极想同她安安静静谈一回。我必须单独见她，单独同她谈。她此刻又惊又倔。我要安抚她——不妨说，把她的意志磁化——从她心里引出实话来。凡她知道的、与亨利·莫兰有丝毫关连的每一星知识，我都要引出来，熔成一块，或为她所用，或对她不利。理查德，你若真如我自诗的那样信得过我的判断，便不会反对我单独去见沙利文小姐，也不会反对我凭那一席话断定：该不该以凶案同谋的罪名逮捕她。”
“我不反对。单独见她是你的职分；我对你有全副的信任。我想，该不该逮捕、该不该起诉，合该由阿盖尔先生同亨利的父亲来定夺。”
“当然。等我同她谈过，若问不出任何足以为凭的事实、教她拿性命去受审，我也不会就此放她自由，总要先同两家商议，把我全部的凭据摊给他们看。他们纵然心里有个*印象*，说人有罪，也不会愿意提起一场站不住的官司。顺便说一句，雷德菲尔德，这些*印象*可是顶古怪的东西！假如我告诉你：有人没有半点凭据，心里却有个印象，说*你*才是那罪人呢？”
我从沙发上站起来，直瞪着他，不知道该不该一拳把他打倒。
“别拿眼光‘杀’我。”他静静地笑着说。“我并没有说*我*心里有这等天启。我说这话，也不是要刺伤你；我是要救你的心。因为，若我没看错，那位把印象透露给我的人，近来正一点点把它透露给别人。哪怕只是那么一个念头、一种可能——一度存于心间，或半存半疑、又像撵不速之客一样撵了出去——那念头也照样留下毒害。你正站在地震上，理查德——随时都可能被吞进去。”
“我？”
“是的。地底下前震的轰响，我已经听出来了。

===Original===

I have said this only to warn you, that you may be ready for self-defense.”
“I scorn to defend myself! Defend myself, forsooth! against what? Who has dared to insinuate that thought against me which you have allowed yourself to echo? But I need not ask﻿—it is my natural foe, James Argyll. He hates me as the rattlesnake hates the black-ash tree!”
“Well, the dislike is mutual. Will you deny that you, too, have had a thought﻿—mind, I say a mere, floating thought﻿—that *he* may have instigated the deed?”
My conscious eye sunk before the steel-blue glance which pierced me. God knows, such a fear, such a belief, at times vague and shadowy, again vivid but brief as lightning, had again and again troubled me. I have hinted at it once, when I said that I was glad that if James ever took money, unpermitted, from his uncle, he took it to waste at the gaming-table. Soon I raised my eyes.
“If I have had such a suspicion, I have struggled against it; I have never breathed it into mortal ear. He has sought to injure me in various ways; I have wished to win and conciliate him; to be friendly with him, for the sake of my regard for his relatives. As to taking a step to fix a blasting stigma upon him, without giving him a chance openly to efface it, I am incapable of it. You are at liberty to judge between us, Mr. Burton.”
“You know that I do not like him,” answered my companion. “But no aversion which I may feel for him shall prevent my weighing all facts which come under my observation, with the utmost impartiality. I am on the right track, in this pursuit, and I shall follow it up to the dark end, though you, yourself, abandon it. Justice shall be meted out! If the bolt strikes the loftiest head in all this aristocratic vicinity, it *shall* fall where it belongs.”
He left the sofa, walking up and down the corridor with a stern, thoughtful face. As for me, I sunk back on my seat, overwhelmed by the confirmation of a thousand times more than my worst fears. *Suspicion of me* was creeping like a shadow over the Argyll household. I had felt its approach long ago; now my whole being grew cold, freezing, except one burning spasm of indignation which throbbed in my breast.
As the gray dawn approached, the rain ceased. Morning was long in coming. As soon as it grew light enough to see, I heard the gardener cutting wood for the fire, and shortly after I walked over, at Mr.

===Chinese===

我说这些，只为提醒你，好教你有所防备。”
“我耻于自辩！自辩，亏你说得出！辩什么？是谁胆敢含沙射影，你居然跟着附和？——不必问了，是我天生的对头，詹姆斯·阿盖尔。他恨我，恨得像响尾蛇恨黑梣树！”
“嗯，这憎恶原是相互的。可你敢不敢承认，你自己也有过一个念头——注意，我说的是一缕飘忽的念头——疑心*他*是这件事的主使？”
我这问心有愧的眼睛，在他那钢蓝色目光的逼视下垂了下去。上帝知道，这样一层疑惧、这样一个念头——有时朦胧如影，有时又亮如电光、一闪即逝——曾多少次搅扰过我。从前我也暗提过一回：我说过，詹姆斯若真从伯父那里私自拿过钱，亏得他是拿去赌台上挥霍。片刻，我抬起了眼。
“我纵然有过这样的疑心，也同它挣扎过；从不曾把它吐进任何活人的耳朵。他千方百计想伤害我；我却一直想笼络他、同他修好——为着我敬重他的亲眷。至于说迈出一步、把毁灭性的污名钉死在他身上、不给他当众洗刷的机会——这样的事我做不出来。你我之间孰是孰非，尽凭你裁断，伯顿先生。”
“你知道我不喜欢他，”我的同伴答道。“但无论我对他怀有怎样的嫌恶，都不能妨碍我以十二分的公允，去权衡我眼下看到的每一件事实。这条追索的路子我走对了，我要一路跟到底，跟到那黑暗的尽头——哪怕你本人半途撟手。正义必要量给！哪怕雷霆劈的是这一带贵族圈里最高的头颅，它也*必定*落在它该落的地方。”
他离了沙发，板着一副严峻沉思的脸，在走廊里踱来踱去。至于我，我跌回座位里，被那证实压垮了——那比我最坏的噩梦还凶一千倍。*对我的疑心*正像一片阴影，爬满阿盖尔家。它的逼近，我早觉出来了；此刻我浑身上下冷透了、冻僵了，只剩胸口一腔烧灼的愤懑，突突地跳。
灰蒙蒙的曙光将近，雨停了。早晨姗姗来迟。天刚亮得能看见东西，我就听见园丁在砍柴生火；不多时，我便走过去——

===Original===

Burton’s request, to ask for some breakfast for the woman and child. I will not describe the garrulous astonishment of the husband and wife upon my announcement that the ghost was cornered, and proved to be Leesy Sullivan. Of course the evil omen of hearing children crying was now explained, as well as the disappearance of a considerable quantity of flour, condiments and apples, which Mrs. Scott had charged to the rats.
It went sorely against the inclination of formal, correct Mrs. Scott, to furnish a comfortable breakfast to “such a jade as that seemed likely to prove; behavin’ in this style, which nobody on ’arth could account for;” but the gratification of her feminine curiosity was some reward for the outrage to her sensibilities, and she went with great expedition to carry the desired refreshments to the prisoners.
When we entered the attic, in the light of the rising sun, Miss Sullivan was sitting quietly on the edge of the mattresses, curling little Nora’s flaxen hair around her fingers. An obstinate reticence marked her looks and actions; she scarcely replied to any of Mrs. Scott’s inquiries﻿—only, when the comfort of the child was concerned. For *her* she took some of the warm food and tea, quietly feeding the eager little girl, while we made a survey of her surroundings.
I now ascertained that a small skylight, hidden from outside view by the chimneys and ornamental work of the battlements, had given egress to the mysterious brightness which had hovered so frequently over the roof. The tenant of this great house had evidently arranged herself for the winter. She had chosen the attic as a place of greatest safety, in the case of parties entering the deserted dwelling for any purpose; here she had brought a tiny charcoal-furnace, used in the basement in summertime for the purpose of heating smoothing-irons, which she supplied with fuel from the stock left over in the cellar. The provisions left in the house had served her wants equally well. It was evident that by the exercise of extreme care and vigilance, leaving the house only in the darkness of the night, she might have remained here for a considerable longer time undisturbed in her novel seclusion, had not the light, which she had never ventured to burn until all was dark and silent in the little cottage, by chance first attracted the curiosity which led finally to discovery.
Mr.

===Chinese===

——奉伯顿先生之嘱，替那母女俩讨些早饭。我宣布鬼已被逼进死角、而且查明就是丽西·沙利文时，那夫妻俩的滔滔惊叹，我就不去描摹了。听得孩子哭声的凶兆，自然就此有了下落；还有好些面粉、佐料和苹果的不翼而飞——斯科特太太原先都记在耗子账上——也一并有了交代。
要给“那样一个准是又贱又泼的丫头”——“做出这等行径，普天下没人说得出个道理”——备一顿舒舒服服的早饭，这与一板一眼、规矩堂堂的斯科特太太的心性实在大相悖逆；只是她那份娘儿们的好奇得了满足，总算补回些她体面上受的冒犯；她便迈开大步，赶着把吃食给那两个“囚徒”送去。
我们走进阁楼时，朝阳正照进来。沙利文小姐安静地坐在床垫边上，手指绕着小诺拉的亚麻色头发打卷。一股顽固的缄默，写在她的神色举止上；斯科特太太的问话，她几乎一概不答——只有涉及孩子舒坦不舒坦的，才应一声。为了*孩子*，她取了些热食和茶，安静地喂着那眼巴巴的小姑娘；我们则把她的住处打量了一遍。
我这才查明：屋顶上那个小小的天窗，外面被烟囱和雉堞的饰件挡住，望不见——那屡屡在屋顶上盘桓的神秘亮光，便是打这里透出去的。这座大宅的“房客”，显然是安顿下来过冬的了。她挑中阁楼做最稳妥的地方——万一日后有人为着什么缘故进这空宅来；她把那只小炭炉搬了上来——那是夏天在地下室里烙熨斗用的——燃料便从地窖存下的煤里取。宅里存下的粮食，也正好供她的需用。显而易见，只要她照这样十二万分地小心警醒，只在黑夜掩蔽下出宅，这场新奇的隐居原可以再维持好些时日、无人搅扰；坏就坏在那盏灯上——她从不肯点灯，除非小屋里已是一团漆黑、万籁无声——偏偏它碰巧先勾起了人的好奇，一步一步引来了这场发现。
伯

===Original===

Burton took a cup of tea and a roll, brought to him there; and then, at his request, he was left alone with the silent woman, who sat there with resolute brows and lips firmly closed, as if locked over her thoughts.
“It will require all his diplomacy to wile her into a communicative mood,” was my decision, as I took a parting glance at her face. I was chilled with my night’s watching, and chilled more utterly by the words the detective had spoken to me as I watched; I returned to the cottage-fire, sitting there three hours, in a painful reverie, answering almost at random the remarks of the housekeeper.
At the close of the three hours, Mr. Burton came into the little dwelling, carrying Nora in his arms, who was stroking his cheek with her chubby hand, and followed by the sewing-girl, whose cheeks bore traces of tears, and whose hunted, defiant look had given place to a dejected, gentle expression.
“Mrs. Scott, I want you to do me a kindness,” he said, in his authoritative, persuasive manner, to which people seldom thought it worth while to object. “I want you to take care of Miss Sullivan and this little cousin of hers, until I send them word they are wanted. It may be today, or not for a week. In the meantime, if you have any sewing to be done for yourself or little Johnny, she will be glad to help you.”
“She’s welcome to stay, I’m sure,” said the woman, in a tone not quite so sure.
“Thank you. I knew I could ask a favor of you. Johnny, come here, and make Miss Nora’s acquaintance. I’m ready, Richard, if you are, to return to the village. Lenore will wonder what has become of us. Good morning, all.”
We walked away.
“Are you not afraid to leave that girl unguarded, after all the trouble she has given us?”
“She will stay there; she has promised me. If she chooses to run away, now, it is a matter of no consequence. I am perfectly, entirely convinced that she is innocent of any participation in the murder of Henry Moreland; or any knowledge of the murder﻿—except, upon one point, I could use her testimony. I shall give my opinion to Mr. Argyll, with my grounds for it; if he chooses to arrest her, she will be there at the cottage. Richard, this affair has gone as far as it can! I shall tell Mr. Argyll, today, that I have withdrawn from it﻿—that I give it up.

===Chinese===

顿先生就着一杯茶、一个面包卷吃了——茶点是给他送到阁楼上来的；随后，应他的要求，众人退了出去，留下他独自面对那沉默的女人：她端坐那里，眉峰决绝，双唇紧闭，像上了锁，锁住满腹心事。
“要哄得她开口，他那套本领得全用上不可。”我临走再望她一眼，心里这样断定。守了一夜，我浑身发冷；守夜时侦探说的那番话，更把我冷透了。我回到小屋的炉火边，一坐三个钟头，沉浸在痛苦的冥想里，管家的太太搭话，我差不多随口应酬。
三个钟头期满，伯顿先生进了小屋，怀里抱着诺拉——小姑娘正用胖乎乎的手拍抚他的脸颊；身后跟着那做缝纫的姑娘。她颊上带着泪痕；那副被人追猎般的、倔强的神色，换成了一种颓然而温柔的表情。
“斯科特太太，我要请你帮个忙。”他说，用的是他那种不容分说又劝人俯首的口吻——难得有人觉得值得违拗。“我要你照看沙利文小姐和她这个小表妹，等我捎信来叫她们再动身。兴许就是今天，兴许要等一个礼拜。这期间，你自个儿或是小约翰尼有什么针线活，她一定乐意帮着做。”
“她尽管住下，我是欢迎的。”那位太太说，语气里却没多少欢迎的底气。
“谢谢。我就知道能求动你。约翰尼，过来，同诺拉小姐认识认识。理查德，你要是预备好了，我们就回村里去。莉诺还不知急成什么样了呢。各位，早安。”
我们走了。
“那姑娘给我们添了这许多乱子，你把她丢在这儿不看着，就不怕她跑了？”
“她会待在那儿的；她应承了我。她如今若真要走，也无关紧要了。我完完全全、彻彻底底地信了：亨利·莫兰被害一案，她既没有分毫参与，也毫无知情——只有一点，她的证词我用得上。我会把我的判断连同依据一并呈给阿盖尔先生；他若定要逮捕她，她就在那小屋里。理查德，这件案子，如今能查的都查到头了！我今天就要告诉阿盖尔先生，我从此退出——这案子，我撂手了。

===Original===

But I am willing *you* should understand that I have not dropped it entirely﻿—that I shall still retain my interest in it﻿—still secretly pursue my investigations, which I believe I can carry on to the best advantage if all parties believe that I have given the matter up. Are you satisfied?”
“If I am not, what difference does it make? It is not for me to dictate your course. I believe that you think it is the best one.”
“I do. So will you some day, if we live to see the termination of this thing. In the meantime, I am your friend, Richard, whether I give any outward signs of friendship very soon or not. You are at liberty to devote yourself to the cause as ardently as ever﻿—and if ever you wish to consult me, you will find me what you now know me.”
I felt strangely as we walked along together. He talked as if he thought some change were coming﻿—as if things were to assume new shapes﻿—as if I were to need friendship, and yet as if he should be compelled to conceal his for me behind a mask of coldness. I did not understand it. I felt half offended with him, and wholly disheartened.
I dined with him at Mr. Argyll’s. It was the last time I sat at that table.
In the afternoon he had a private interview with the family, *from which I was excluded*; and in the evening he returned to the city, taking with him Lenore, the last wave of whose hand was for James, her last kiss for Miss Argyll.
The next morning Mr. Argyll informed me that he had resolved to make his nephew his partner in the practice of the law, and that I was at liberty to take advantage of any other opportunity I might have for going into business for myself. His manner was cold; he expressed no regrets for my probable disappointment, caused by his own suggestions; I could feel myself dismissed from his friendship as well as his office. I would not ask why. My tongue grew dry as ashes when I thought of attempting it. Mr. Burton had given me the clue to the feelings which prompted this rupture of a lifelong friendship﻿—it was such as to forbid any questions. No explanations could be made﻿—nothing could obliterate the memory of so deadly a wrong as they were committing upon me. The golden bowl of friendship was broken at the fountain﻿—the waters spilled upon the ground.
I told him that I had contemplated a visit to my mother, which I would take this opportunity to make.

===Chinese===

不过我愿意*你*明白：我并没有全撂下——我对它的兴味还留着——还要暗地里追查下去；我自信，只要各方都当我撟手不管了，我这查访倒最能得力。你可放心？”
“我放不放心，又碍着什么呢？你的路数轮不到我来指点。我相信你自己认准了那是上策。”
“我认准了。有朝一日——只要咱们都活着看到这案子收场——你也会认的。这期间，我是你的朋友，理查德，不论我近不近就把交情挂在脸上。你要为这桩案子尽心，尽管一如既往地尽心——几时想找我商量，我这个人，还是你现在认识的这个人。”
两人一路走着，我心里说不出的异样。他说话的口气，仿佛觉得有什么变故要来——仿佛万事都要换一副新面目——仿佛我将有求于友情，而他那份友情，却又不得不藏到一副冷面具后头去。我不懂。我对他有几分愠意，更有十分的灰心。
那天我随他在阿盖尔先生家用了餐。那是我最后一次坐那张餐桌。
下午他同那家人密谈了一场，*唯独把我排除在外*；傍晚他回城里去，带走了莉诺——小姑娘最后一挥手，是挥给詹姆斯的；最后一个吻，是吻给阿盖尔小姐的。
第二天早晨，阿盖尔先生告诉我：他已决定立他侄子做事务所的合伙人；而我，尽可自便，另觅哪处机会，独自去开业。他的态度冷冰冰的；我这番落空多半是他亲口撺掇出来的，他却毫无半句歉意；我觉出自己被逐出了他的友谊，一如被逐出他的事务所。我不愿问为什么。一想到要开口去问，舌头便干得像灰。伯顿先生已把线头交到我手里，叫我看清了这场断交背后是什么心肠——那样的心肠，问了也是白问。什么解释都不可能有了——他们加给我的这样一场死冤孽，什么也抹不掉了。友谊的金碗在泉边碎了——水泼了一地。
我对他说，我本就打算去看望母亲，正好趁这个机会走一趟。

===Original===

I might find what I wished for, in the way of business, in the vicinity of my father’s former home; when, with formal thanks for his past kindness (which I was mentally vowing I would find some means to repay), and begging him to trouble himself not at all about my fortunes, I bowed myself from the office where I had spent so much of the last three years of my life.
Blind, dizzy, cold, I went to my boardinghouse to pack my trunks.
Before I went to bed, my few arrangements were completed. My clothes, books, the few little articles of taste, or gifts of friends, allowable in one small rented room, were easily put away in their traveling receptacle. But, as for the rest!﻿—for the wealth which my heart had silently garnered during the golden harvest of youth﻿—where was it? Swept away as by a mighty wind.
I slept some, for I was thoroughly worn out by my emotions, no less than by my recent vigils; but the earliest morning found me awake. I was to leave at noon; I had many pleasant acquaintances in the village, from whom I ought not to have parted without a farewell call; but all these small pleasures and courtesies of life were swept aside, as sand upon my path. I had nothing to do, all the tedious morning, save to pretend to eat my breakfast, until the hour which I had set in my thoughts for saying goodbye to the girls.
I would not go away without seeing them; if there was any accusation in their eyes I would confront it. And then, I did not believe that Eleanor would do me an injustice. Blue-eyed, just, gentle as was her character, *she*, at least, was grieved for me﻿—believed in me. I did not admit to myself how much comfort I drew from this faith, until I was startled from it. My baggage was dispatched; my watch told eleven; I passed the house on the way to the cars, giving myself a few minutes for this farewell. As I knocked at the door, one of the servants opened it. I sent her to ask Miss Argyll if she would come down to say goodbye, before I left on my visit to my mother; and Mary﻿—I would like to see her also.
While I waited for them, I stepped into the dear familiar parlors and library, mutely taking my leave of them, with all their mingled associations. Presently the messenger returned:
“Miss Argyll sent her farewell; she could not see Mr. Redfield that morning.”
“Where is she?”
“In the breakfast-room, looking at her flowers.”

===Chinese===

兴许在我父亲旧居一带，能寻着我要寻的营生。我客客气气道了谢，谢他往日的关照（心里暗暗立誓：这恩情我必要想个法子偿还），又请他千万不必为我的前程费心，随后躬身一礼，退出那间事务所——我一生最后三年，大半耗在那里。
我又瞎又晕又冷，一路回到寄宿处收拾行李。
睡前，我那点行装已收拾停当。衣裳、书籍，还有几件雅致的小物事、朋友们的赠予——一间小小的租屋里搁得下的，就这么几样——都顺顺当当归了行箧。可是其余的呢！——我年轻那黄金收成里，心头默默积下的那份财富——在哪儿？像被一阵狂风扫尽了。
我睡了几个钟头——接二连三的守夜，加上这番情感的折腾，早把我熬空了；可天刚透亮，我又醒了。我定于正午动身。村里我有许多交好的熟人，按理临别该一一登门辞行；可生活中这些小小的愉快与礼数，都被扫到一边去了，像扫我路上的沙。那漫长的早晨，我无事可做，只除了装样子吃顿早饭，直捱到我在心里定下的、向姑娘们道别的时辰。
我不见到她们不走；她们眼里若有指控，我就迎着它。再说，我不信埃莉诺会冤枉我。她生就一双蓝眼睛、一副公正温柔的心肠，*她*，至少，是在为我难过——是信我的。我从这份信任里得了多少安慰，我自己都不肯承认——直到后来一惊，才发觉。行李先打发走了；表上十一点；我循着去车站的路，绕经那座宅子，给自己留了几分钟道别。我叩门，一个女仆开了门。我请她上去问阿盖尔小姐，我可否在她家楼下见一面道个别——我就要动身去看母亲了；还有玛丽——我也想见见她。
等她们的工夫，我踱进那几间亲熟的书房客厅，默默向它们辞行——那里的一草一木都缠着我的旧忆。不多时，传话的女仆回来了：
“阿盖尔小姐托她道别；今天早晨她不能见雷德菲尔德先生。”
“她在哪儿？”
“在早餐间，看她的花呢。”

===Original===

I started for the room in a wild tumult of anger and passion, resolved to make her confess the reason of this treatment. Surely, three years of an intimacy like ours, gave me the right. In three minutes I confronted her where she stood, in the door between the breakfast-room and conservatory, like a statue draped in crape.
“Eleanor!”
She shrunk back; she held up her hands with an expression of horror. My God! that look in Eleanor’s eyes was enough to kill me. I turned away as hastily as I had come. As I stumbled along the passage, half blind with the terrible surging and throbbing of the blood through me, a soft pair of arms fell about my neck, a cheek wet with tears was pressed to mine﻿—it was Mary.
“Never mind what they say about you, Richard,” she sobbed. “I don’t believe one word of it﻿—not one word! I never shall. I am your friend. I love you; indeed I do. *I* do not want you to go away,” and she kissed me twice or thrice.
I took the sweet face in my cold hands, looked into the brimming eyes, hastily kissed the blushing cheek﻿—“God bless you, Mary,” said I, and was gone.

===Chinese===

我一阵狂怒、一阵激愤，大步朝那间屋子走去，非要她说出这般待我的缘由不可。像我们这样三年亲密的情分，总该给我这个权利。三分钟后，我已经立在她面前——她站在早餐间与花房之间的门口，像一尊披着黑纱的雕像。
“埃莉诺！”
她朝后一缩，双手举起来，满脸恐怖。我的上帝！埃莉诺眼里的那一眼，足够杀了我。我掉头就走，来得多快，去得多快。
我跌跌撞撞穿过走廊，血在周身汹涌奔突，两眼几乎发黑；这时一双柔软的胳膊搂住我的脖子，一张泪湿的脸贴上我的脸——是玛丽。
“别管他们怎么说你，理查德。”她抽抽噎噎地说。“他们的话我一个字也不信——一个字也不信！永远不信。我是你的朋友。我爱你；真的，我真的爱你。*我*不愿意你走。”说着她亲了我两三下。
我用冰冷的双手捧起那张甜甜的脸，望进那双噙满泪的眼睛，匆匆吻了吻那绯红的面颊——“上帝保佑你，玛丽。”我说完，人就走了。

## Part / 第二部
===Original===

II

===Chinese===

二

## I The Letter / 一 信件
===Original===

The reader can now understand why it was that I turned cold with excitement as I sat there in the dead-letter office, holding the time-stained epistle in my hand. Every word burned itself into my brain. Obscure as it was﻿—noncommittal﻿—directed to an unknown person of a neighboring village﻿—I yet felt *assured* that those vague hints had reference to the sinful tragedy which had occurred October 17th, 1857. Here was placed in my hands﻿—at last!﻿—a clue to that mystery which I had once sworn to unravel. Yet, how slender was the clue, which might, after all, lead me into still profounder labyrinths of doubt and perplexity! As I pondered, it seemed to break and vanish in my fingers. Yet, I felt, in spite of this, an inward sense *that I held the key which was surely to unlock the awful secret*. I can never rightly express the feelings which, for the first few moments, overpowered me. My body was icy cold, but my soul stung and stirred me as with fire, and seemed to rise on “budding wings” of flame with conviction of a speedy triumph which was to come after long suffering. I arose, clutched my hat, and went forth from the Department, to return to it no more, for the present. Half the night I sat in my room at my boarding-place, looking at that letter on the table before me.
Before I proceed further with its history, I will give, in a few words, the brief, monotonous record of my life, since I was driven﻿—driven is the word you must use, Richard, haughty and sensitive though you may be﻿—from the friendship and presence of the Argylls, and from my prospects of a long-cherished settlement in life. I made the visit to my mother. She was shocked at the change in me, and grieved that I withheld my confidence from her. But, I did not feel in a confiding mood. The gentleness of my nature had been hardened; I was bitter, sneering, skeptical; not from my own mother would I accept the sympathy which my chilled heart seemed no longer to crave. Only one thing saved me from utter loathing of humanity, and that was the memory of Mary’s face, as she had sought me at parting. In those sweet eyes were trust and love; the tears which streamed down and fell upon her bosom, the quiver of her lip, the sobs and fond words, attested to the sorrow with which she had beheld my banishment.

===Chinese===

读者如今可以明白，我为什么坐在死信办公室里，手里搜着那封蒙了时日污渍的信，激动得浑身发冷。每一个字都烙进了我的脑子。它那样晦涩——含糊其辞——寄给邻村一个查无此人——可我仍然*确信*，那些隐隐约约的暗示，指向的是1857年10月17日发生的那桩罪孽的惨剧。此刻落到我手里了——终于！——一条线索，指向我曾发誓要解开的那个谜。然而，这线索多么细弱啊，说不定，它只会把我引进更幽深的迷宫，叫我又疑又乱！我一路想，它仿佛就在我指间碎掉、散掉了。可尽管如此，我心里仍有一种感觉：*我握着那把钥匙，它必将打开那可怕的秘密*。最初那几刻吞没我的种种心情，我永远无法恰当地表达。我的身子冰凉，灵魂却像被火螫着、催动着，仿佛踩着初生的火焰之翼升起来，笃信长久受苦之后，一场速捷的胜利就要来了。我站起身，抓起帽子，走出邮政部，一时再没有回去。半夜的时光，我有一半是坐在寓所的屋里，望着桌上那封信。
在往下讲它的来历之前，我要用几句话，交代一下我那段简短而单调的履历：自从我被逐出——你必须用「逐出」这个词，理查德，任你怎样高傲、怎样敏感——阿盖尔一家的友谊和门庭，也失去我盼望已久的安身立业之机以后，我是怎样过的。我回去了母亲那里。她见我变了个人，又惊又痛：我不肯对她吐露心事。可我实在没有倾吐的心绪。我天性里的温软变硬了；我刻薄、讥诮、多疑；连生身母亲的怜恤，我那颗冷透的心仿佛已不再渴求，我也不肯领受。只有一样东西，救我不致对整个人类深恶痛绝：那是玛丽送我分手时那张脸的记忆。那双可爱的眼睛里盛着信托和爱；滚滚落下、落在她胸前的泪，颤抖的唇，呜咽和亲爱的话语，都作证她目睹我被放逐时是怎样的悲哀。

===Original===

Of course my mother was surprised to hear that I had left Blankville, with no intention of returning to it; that the long-understood partnership was not to be entered into. But, she did not press me for explanations. She waited for me to tell her all, patiently; ministering to my health and comfort, meanwhile, as a widowed mother will minister to an only son﻿—with a tenderness only less than that of heaven, because it is yet, perforce, of earth.
Before I had been at home a fortnight, the unnatural tension of my mind and nerves produced a sure result﻿—a reaction took place, and I fell sick. It was in the softer mood which came over me, as I was convalescing from this illness, that I finally told my mother all the dreadful story of the influences which had broken up my connection with the Argylls. Her grief for me, her indignation against my enemy or enemies, was what might have been expected. I could hardly restrain her from starting at once for Blankville, to stand before her old friend, the friend of my father, and accuse him, face to face, of the wrong he had done her boy. But, out of this I persuaded her. I asked her if she did not see that the wrong was irreparable? I could not forgive it. It did not admit of being talked about; let the cloud drop between them and us; our paths were henceforth apart. To this she finally yielded; and, if there could have been any balm to my wounded pride and still more deeply wounded affections, I should have found it in the enhanced, touching, almost too-perfect tenderness with which my parent sought to make up to me that which I had lost.
For a few weeks I abandoned myself to her healing attentions. Then I set myself resolutely to find work both for hands and mind. My mother was not without influential friends. As I have said, my fortunes were somewhat nipped by my father’s untimely death, but our family and associations were among the best. We had a relative in power at Washington. To him I applied for a clerkship, and received, in answer, the situation I was filling, at the time when that dead-letter came so strangely into my hands.
It may be thought improbable that I should abandon the profession for which I had studied with so much zeal. But, the very memory of that zeal, and of the hopes which had stimulated it, now gave me a dislike to the law. I required both change of scene and of pursuits.

===Chinese===

母亲听说我离开了布兰克维尔、也不打算回去；那桩久已说定的合伙，终于没有入成——她自然吃惊。可她并不逼我问缘由。她耐心等我向她和盘托出；一面只管照拂我的身体和起居，像一位孀居的母亲照拂独子那样——那份温存，只因到底还在人间，才次于天恩。
我到家不满半月，心神那番反常的绷紧便结出了必然的果子——一场反作用发作了，我病倒了。正是在这场病将愈未愈、我心绪转软的时候，我到底把那桩可怕的原委——那些毁掉我同阿盖尔一家情分的影响——统统讲给了母亲。她为我悲伤，对我的仇人或仇人们义愤，都是意料中事。我几乎拦不住她：她要即刻动身去布兰克维尔，站到她的老友、我父亲的故交面前，当面指控他亏待了她的儿子。这一层，我说服了她。我问她：难道看不出这亏待无可挽回？我宽恕不了。它也经不起谈论；就让那片云落在他们与我们之间吧；从今往后，两下里各走各路。她终于依了。倘若我那受伤的骄傲、伤得更深的感情，还有什么膏油可敷，那便在我父母亲身上：她那份加倍的、动人的、几乎过于周全的温存，一心要补上我所失去的。
有几个礼拜，我把自己交给她那医人的照拂。随后我打起精神，决意给双手和头脑都寻一份差事。母亲并非没有得力的朋友。我说过，父亲早逝，家道颇受挫折，可我们的家世和交游，都属上乘。我们在华盛顿有一位当权的亲戚。我便向他谋一个书记的职位；他给我的答复，就是那封信古古怪怪落到我手里时、我正在充任的这个差事。
有人也许会觉得难以置信：我竟抛开了自己曾经那样热忱修习的那门职业。可正因为记得那份热忱、记得激励它的种种指望，我如今对法律反倒生厌。我需要换一换地方，也换一换营生。

===Original===

The blow dealt at my heart had stunned my ambition, also. To one of my temperament, aspirations, acquisitiveness, all the minor passions and pursuits of life are but steps leading up the hillside to the rose-crowned summit, where love sits smiling under the eye of heaven. And I, being for the time at least, blasted prematurely, was no more myself, but was to myself like a stranger within my own sanctuary. I went into the dead-letter office, and commenced my routine of breaking seals and registering contents, as if I had been born for that business. I was a rapid worker, quiet, and well-thought-of by my associates, who deemed me a little cold and skeptical, a trifle reserved, very steady for so young a fellow, and an efficient clerk who thoroughly earned his salary. That was all they knew of Richard Redfield. And in those days I did not know much more about myself. The months had worn away, one after the other, with a dreary coldness. In the summer I struggled through the suffocating dust; in the winter I picked my way through the disgusting mud, to and fro, from my lodgings to the office buildings; that was about all the change which the seasons brought to me, whom once the smell of spring violets filled with pungent delight, and the odor of June roses made happy as a god on Olympus.
Half the night I sat brooding over that brief revelation, so precious to me, yet so loathsome. The longer I pondered its words the less vivid grew my hope of making any triumphant use of it for the detection of the two guilty persons﻿—the one who wrote it, and the one to whom it was addressed. I might lay it before Mr. Argyll, but he might not feel, as I did, that it had any connection with the murder, neither was there anything to prove but that the missive might have been directed to *me*. Indeed, Mr. Argyll might well inquire how I could pretend that it should have reached me through the routine of the dead-letter department, after all this stretch of time﻿—very nearly two years!
This was a matter which puzzled me exceedingly. In the ordinary course of affairs, it would, if not claimed, have been forwarded to Washington three months after its reception at Peekskill, and have long ago been consigned to the wastebasket and the flames. The hand of an overruling Providence seemed to be moving the men in this terrible game.

===Chinese===

打在我心上的那一击，也震昏了我的抱负。以我这样的性情，抱负也好，进取也好，连同一切次要的欲望和营求，不过是山坡上一级级台阶，通向玫瑰加冕的峰顶——那里，爱情在天目之下含笑而坐。而我，至少是暂时地，遭了雷殛，不再是原先的我；在自己心殿里，我竟像一个陌生人。我进了死信办公室，开始拆封、登记内件的日常功课，倒像是生来就该做这一行的。我手脚麻利，人又安静，同事们对我多有好评：他们觉得我有点冷淡多疑，有点缄默，年纪轻轻却非常沉稳，是个称职的书记，薪水拿得问心无愧。他们对理查德·雷德菲尔德的了解，尽在于此。那些日子里，我对自己也所知无几。月复一月，冷清清地磨损过去。夏天，我挣扎着穿过令人窒息的尘土；冬天，我一路挑着落脚处，穿过叫人作咽的泥泞，往返于寓所与办公的楼宇之间；季节带给我的变化，大约就这么多——虽然从前，紫罗兰的春气一香起来，我会满心锐利的欢喜，六月玫瑰的芬芳，能叫我快乐得像奥林波斯山上的神。
半夜的时光，我有一半用来对那短短的披露冥想——于我这样珍贵，又这样可憎。它的话，我越掂量，那借此侦破两个罪人——写信的一个，收信的一个——并赢得一场胜利的希望，就越黯淡。我可以把它摆到阿盖尔先生面前；可他未必像我一样，觉得它同凶案有什么牵连；再说，也毫无凭据，能证明这封信不是寄给*我*的。真的，阿盖尔先生满可以问：我凭什么说，隔了这么久——差不多两年！——这封信还会循着死信处的例行公事，送到我手里？
这一层叫我为难极了。按事务的常轨，这封信若无人认领，本该在到皮克斯基尔三个月后转寄华盛顿，早该进了字纸篓，进了火。那只主宰一切的天意之手，仿佛正在这局可怕的棋里拨动着人。

===Original===

At that hour I recognized it, and felt a solemn conviction that, sooner or later, the murderer would be checkmated. It was this assurance, more than any evidence contained in the letter, which gave me hope that it would eventually be the instrument of punishment to the guilty. I remembered the vow I had once made to my soul, never to rest in the peace of my own pursuits, until I had dragged the slayer of the innocent into the awful presence of Justice. That vow I had neglected to fulfill to the uttermost, partly because of the injury which had been done to my self-love, and also because the circumstance which had attached suspicion to me, in the eyes of those interested, had made it dangerous for me to move in a matter where all my motives were misconstrued. But now that Fate had interposed in this singular manner, in my behalf and in that of Truth, I took fresh courage. I was fully startled from my apathy. That night I wrote my resignation to the Department, gathered up my few effects again, and the next morning found me on the way to New York.
My first purpose was to consult Mr. Burton. I had not seen him since the day when we parted in Blankville; I only knew, by accident, that he was still a resident of New York, having casually heard his name mentioned in connection with a case which had brought some detectives on to Washington only a few weeks previous.
I had never forgiven or understood the part he had played in that last interview with the Argylls. I remembered the assurance he had given me of friendship, but I did not believe that he had shown any friendship for me, in that consultation with the relatives, or the results would not have been so disastrous to me. Nevertheless, I felt a confidence in him; he was the man for the emergency, and to him I would take the letter. I thought it quite probable, that in the multiplicity of new interests, the circumstances which had once brought us so much together had faded from his mind, and that I should have to reawaken his recollection of the details.
On the morning after my arrival in New York, I consulted the directory, and finding that Mr. Burton still resided in Twenty-third street, I called at the house at the earliest admissible hour.
While I was handing my card to the servant, his master came out of the library at the end of the hall, and hastening forward, shook me heartily by the hand.

===Chinese===

在那个时辰我看清了它，并怀着庄严的确信：那凶手，迟早要被将死。正是这一份确信——比信里含着的任何凭据都多——给我希望：它终将成为惩罚罪人的利器。我想起自己从前对灵魂立下的誓愿：不把杀害无辜的凶手拖到正义那可畏的面前，我便永不安享自己营生的清福。这誓愿我没有贯彻到底：一半因为我自爱所受的戕害，一半因为落在有关人等眼中、系在我身上的那些疑点，害得我一举一动都遭误解，再插手此案便是凶险。可如今，命运既这样奇特地出头，一维护我，二维护真相，我便重新鼓起了勇气。我从麻木里彻底惊醒了。当夜我写了辞呈呈给部里，重新收拾起我那点行装；第二天早晨，人已在去纽约的路上。
我的头一件事，是去请教伯顿先生。自布兰克维尔分手那天起，我再没有见过他；我只是偶然听说，他仍住在纽约——几个礼拜前，有一桩案子把几位侦探召到华盛顿来，我无意中听见有人提起他的名字。
他在同阿盖尔一家最后那次会面里扮演的角色，我从来不曾原谅，也从来不曾懂得。我记得他向我保证过友谊；可我不信他在同那家亲属的商议里对我有过什么友谊——不然，那结局断不至于对我那样惨。尽管如此，我对他存着一份信心；他是能应付这场危局的人；那封信，我就要带给他。我想，多半是：新的头绪太多，从前把我们引到一处的那些情由，在他心里已经淡了，我还得把他记忆里的细节重新唤醒一遍。
到纽约的第二天早晨，我翻查姓名住址簿，见伯顿先生仍住在二十三街，便在一早容许造访的钟点，登门去了。
我正把名片递给仆人，他的主人恰从门厅尽头的书房里出来，快步迎上，热热络络同我握手。

===Original===

His joyous tones were better evidence of his pleasure at seeing me, than even his words, which were cordial enough.
“I heard your voice, Richard,” he said, “and did not wait for you to be ushered in with the formalities. Welcome, my friend;” his expression was as if he had said﻿—“Welcome, my son.”
He led me into the library, and placing me in an armchair, sat down opposite me, looking at me with the well-remembered piercing shafts of those steel-blue eyes. After inquiring about my health, etc., he said, suddenly,
“You have news.”
“You are right, Mr. Burton﻿—else I should not have been here. I suppose you are aware that I have been a clerk in the dead-letter office for the last eighteen months?”
“I was aware of it. I never intended to let you slip out of the numbered rosary of my friends, and lose you so entirely as not even to know your whereabouts.”
“Day before yesterday this letter arrived at the office, and I chanced to be the clerk who opened it.”
I handed him the missive. He examined the envelope attentively, before unfolding the sheet within; and as he continued to hold it in his hand, and gaze at it, one of those wonderful changes passed over his countenance that I had remarked on some previous important occasions. His practical intelligence seized upon the date, the post-office marks, the hasty direction, and made the contents of the letter his own, almost, before he read it. For some moments he pondered the outside, then drew forth the letter, perused it with one swift glance, and sat holding it, gazing at it, lost in thought, and evidently forgetful of my presence. A stern pallor settled gradually over his usually placid face; at last he looked up, and seeing me, recalled his surroundings to his recollection.
“It is sad to be made to feel that such creatures live and flourish,” he said, almost despondingly; “but,” as his face brightened, “I can not say how glad I am to get hold of this. It partially explains some things which I have already found out. The chance which threw this document into your hands was a marvelous one, Richard.”
“However simple the explanation may prove to be, I shall always regard it as Providential.”
“All things are Providential,” said my companion, “none less, and none more so. Causes will have their effects.

===Chinese===

他那欢快的嗓音，比他的言语更作证他见我的欢喜——尽管那些话也够亲热的了。
「我听出你的嗓音了，理查德，」他说，「没有等仆人通报的俗套。欢迎你，我的朋友；」他的神气像是在说——「欢迎你，我的孩子。」
他领我进书房，让我坐在扶手椅里，自己在对面坐下，用那双钢蓝色的眼睛——我记得清清楚楚的那种锐利的光箭——望着我。问了问我的健康等等，他忽然说：
「你有消息。」
「您说对了，伯顿先生——不然我也不会来这儿。想必您知道，这一年半来，我在死信办公室当书记？」
「我知道。我从不打算让你从我那串数着念珠的朋友里滑出去，滑到连你的下落都不知道的地步。」
「前天，这封信到了办公室，碰巧拆它的书记就是我。」
我把信递给他。他先细看信封，才抽出里面的信纸；他把它拿在手里端详时，脸上起了那种奇妙的变化——从前几桩要紧的场合，我见过这变化。他那实际的智力，一把攫住了日期、邮戳、仓促的地址，信还没读，内容已先成了他的。他把外面琢磨了几分钟，然后抽出信纸，一眼扫过，便拿着它坐在那里出神，显然把我忘在了旁边。一层严峻的苍白，渐渐罩上他平日平和的脸；末了他抬起头，看见我，才想起身在何处。
「想到这样的东西还活着、还兴旺，实在叫人难过，」他几乎是沮丧地说；随即他的脸亮了，「可拿到这个，我说不出多高兴。它把几件我已查得的事，解说了一部分。把这份文件抛到你手里的那个机缘，理查德，真是个奇迹。」
「不管解释到头来多么简单，我永远要把它看作天意。」
「万事都是天意，」我的同伴说，「不多一分，不少一分。有因必有果。

===Original===

But now, as to the writer of this﻿—I am glad I have a specimen of the villain’s handwriting; it will enable me to know the writer when I see him.”
“How so, Mr. Burton?”
“Because I have a very good picture of him, now, in my mind’s eye. He is about thirty years of age, rather short and broad-shouldered, muscular; has dark complexion and black eyes; the third finger of his right hand has been injured, so as to contract the muscles and leave it useless. He has some education, which he has acquired by hard study since he grew up to be his own master. His childhood was passed in ignorance, in the midst of the worst associations; and his own nature is almost utterly depraved. He is bad, from instinct, inheritance and bringing-up; and now, our blessed Redeemer, himself, would hardly find good enough in him to promise a hope of ultimate salvation. It is curious that he should ever have seen fit to study, so as to acquire even the smattering of knowledge which he has. He must have been led into it by some powerful passion. If I could decide what that passion was, I might have a key to unlock the gate into some other matters.”
I stared at the speaker in astonishment as he rapidly pronounced the above analysis of the personal appearance and character of the writer.
“Do you know him?” I asked.
“I do not know his *name*, and I have never met him. All the acquaintance I have with him, I have made through the medium of his chirography. It is sufficient for me; I can not mistake,”﻿—then, observing my puzzled and incredulous look, he smiled, as he added, “By the way, Richard, you are not aware of my accomplishment in the art of reading men and women from a specimen of their handwriting. It is one of my greatest aids in the profession to which I have devoted myself. The results I obtain sometimes astonish my friends. But, I assure you, there is nothing marvelous in them. Patient study and unwearied observation, with naturally quick perceptions, are the only witchcraft I use. With moderate natural abilities, I assert that any other person could equal me in this art (black art, some of my acquaintances regard it,) by giving the same time to it that a musician would to master an instrument.”
“I do not know about that, Mr. Burton. I guess it would take a mind of the singular composition of your own to make much out of an art with no rules and no foundations.”

===Chinese===

「至于这写信的人——我高兴，我得了这恶棍笔迹的一件标本；见着他，我便认得他。」
「怎么会呢，伯顿先生？」
「因为此刻，我心眼里已经把他的一幅好画像描出来了。他年纪三十上下，个子不高，肩膀宽，筋骨结实；肤色深，眼睛黑；右手第三根手指受过伤，伤得筋络蜷缩，指头废了。他受过些教育，是成年自主以后苦读来的。他的童年在愚昧里度过，混在最坏的伙伴中间；他自己的天性，败坏得几乎无可救药。他坏，坏在天性，坏在血脉，坏在教养；如今，连我们蒙福的救主亲来，也难在他身上找出多少善，许他一个终得救拔的指望。稀奇的是，他竟觉得值得读书，连他那一星半点的学识也是苦读来的。他一定是叫某种强烈的情欲引上这条路的。我若断得出那是什么情欲，或许就拿到一把钥匙，好开别的几道门。」
他一口气吐出这番对写信人相貌性情的剖析，我愕然望着说话的人。
「您认得他？」我问。
「我不认得他的*名字*，也从未见过他。我同他打的全部交道，都是透过他的笔迹。于我这就够了；我错不了，」——见我一脸狐疑，他微笑着补了一句，「对了，理查德，你还不知道我有一样本事：从一份笔迹，读出写它的男人女人。这是我一桩最大的臂助，助我干我献身的这行。我得到的结果，有时叫我的朋友们吃惊。可我向你保证，里头毫无神通。耐心的研习，不倦的观察，加上天生敏锐的知觉，是我用的全部法术。我说，天资中等的人，只要肯下音乐家摸熟一件乐器那样的工夫，谁都能在这门艺术上同我比肩——我的熟人们里，有几位管它叫黑艺术。」
「这我可不知道，伯顿先生。我猜，总得先有一副您这样奇特的脑筋，才能在一门没有规则、没有根基的艺术里做出多大的名堂来。」

===Original===

“It has its rules, for me. But as proof is better than argument, show me any letters or scraps of writing you may have about you. I would like to satisfy you, before we proceed further, for I do not wish you to feel that you are working with a crackbrained individual, who is riding a hobby at your expense.”
I emptied my inside coat-pocket of its contents, among which were several letters﻿—one from my mother, a note from my uncle in Washington, an invitation from an old college-chum to attend his wedding in Boston, and two or three business epistles from casual acquaintances﻿—one, I remember, an entreaty from a young man to get him something to do in that magnetic center of all unemployed particles﻿—Washington. Of these, I revealed only to him the superscription and signature, with, perhaps, some unimportant sentence, which would, in no way, of itself, betray the characters or pursuits of the writers. I need not describe my surprise when, in each instance, he gave a careful and accurate description of the age, appearance, habits, profession and mental qualities of the person whose handwriting he had examined.
I could hardly credit my own senses; there must be some hocus-pocus about it, as in the tricks which jugglers play with cards. But my respect for the earnestness of my companion’s pursuits, and the indubitable nature of his proof, did not allow me to doubt any length of time. I became a believer in *his facts*, and I give these facts to my readers, at the risk of seeing the plain, sensible nose of the majority turned up with an expression of skepticism, mortifying to me. Mr. Burton’s character is a real one, and the truth of his wonderful achievements will become history.
The terrible interest of the subject which had brought us together did not permit us to spend much time in these interesting but irrelevant experiments. We discussed the past and present. Mr. Burton assured me that he had never, for a day, lost sight of the case﻿—that his interest in it had deepened, rather than lessened; that he had not been idle during all this long period; but that he had already gathered up a fact or two of some importance, and had been on the point of sending for me, once or twice. He had refrained, waiting for some lights to culminate, and “now, he was glad enough to get hold of that letter.”

===Chinese===

「于我，它自有规则。不过空谈不如实证：把你身上带的书信字条，随便拿几样给我瞧瞧。趁往下走之前，我想先叫你信服；我不愿你觉得，你是在陪一个疯头疯脑的家伙，让他花你的钱兜他的风。」
我把大衣内袋里的东西尽数掣出来，里头有几封信——母亲的一封，华盛顿的叔叔的一张便条，一位大学老同学从波士顿发来的婚宴请帖，还有两三封泛泛之交的事务信——我记得其中一封，是个年轻人恳求我给他谋个事由，好进华盛顿——那块吸聚一切无业分子的磁石。这些信，我只给他看了信头的称呼和落款的签名，至多再添一句不关紧要的话，单凭它决露不出写信人的性情行止。用不着描摹我的惊讶：每一封信，他都能把写信人的年纪、相貌、习惯、职业和心智，仔仔细细、分毫不差地说出来。
我几乎不能信自己的耳目；这里面必定有戏法，像变戏法的人耍牌那样。可我敬重我这位同伴治学的诚挚，他的证据又铁凿凿的，容不得我疑多久。我成了*他的事实*的信徒；我还要把这些事实交给我的读者，哪怕多数人那又平实又明白的鼻子会翘起来，露出叫我不痛快的怀疑相。伯顿先生这个人物实有其人，他那桩桩奇迹般的成就之真实，终将成为信史。
把我们聚到一处的那个题目的可怕兴味，不容我们在这些有趣而不相干的试验上多费工夫。我们把过去和现在谈论了一番。伯顿先生向我担保：这桩案子，他没有一天丢开过眼——他的兴味是有增无减；这漫长的时日里他也没有闲着；他已经拾得一两件要紧的事实，有一两回，已经到了要打发人唤我的地步。他忍住了，专等几处线索聚到头上；「如今，可教他把那封信拿到手了。」

===Original===

He informed me that Leesy Sullivan was living quietly in the city, subsisting mostly upon donations from himself, she being too far gone with consumption to exert herself much with the needle. The child was with her, healthy and pretty.
I made no inquiries after James Argyll, but he told me that the young man came frequently to the city; that, for a while, he had seemed dispirited, and gambled desperately, but that lately he was looking and behaving better.
“It is my impression,” added he, “that he is about to marry one of his cousins﻿—probably the youngest. And as to his bad habits, I caused him to understand, indirectly, that if they were not reformed, he should be convicted of them, before his uncle. This I did (after I became convinced that he would marry one of the young ladies) out of compassion to the family.”
My head drooped on my hand. It was long since I had any tidings of the Argylls﻿—death could hardly have created a more barren space between us. Yet, now that I heard the names of the girls mentioned, a flood of old emotions broke over me, beneath which I struggled, half-suffocated. Keen pain shot through my heart at the idea of Mary, that innocent, most sweet and lovable girl, becoming the wife of James. I felt as if it ought to be prevented, yet how could I interfere? Why should I wish to? I recalled the hour when she had flown to me﻿—had said, “*I* believe in you, Richard; *I* love you!” and I knew that I had put a construction upon the tearful, passionate words of her last avowal, which was, after all, not warranted. I had feared that she did really love me, and that, in the last moment of sorrow and trouble, her feelings had betrayed themselves to her own comprehension﻿—and I had felt a hope that it was not so. My own unanswered passion﻿—my lonely, unmated life﻿—had taught me sympathy; and I was not so utterly selfish as to have my personal vanity tickled with the idea that this young creature loved me, who did not love her, except truly as a sister.
Yet now, when hearing that James had turned from Eleanor to her, I felt a pang of pity﻿—a wish that she might rather have loved me than him whose cold, deceitful bosom could never be a safe shelter for a woman as affectionate as Mary. With this regret I felt a triumph that Eleanor had remained unassailable on the sublime and solitary height of her sorrow. It was what I expected of her.

===Chinese===

他告诉我，丽西·沙利文如今安安静静住在城里，生计大半靠他的接济；痨病已深，做不动针线了。孩子同她在一起，又健康，又好看。
关于詹姆斯·阿盖尔，我一言未问；他却告诉我：那年轻人常进城来；有一阵子神色颓唐，赌得凶；近来气色和行止都好些了。
「我的印象是，」他补了一句，「他要娶他的一位堂妹了——多半是小的那位。至于他的恶习，我间接叫人递话给他：再不收束，我就要在他伯父面前，拿这些恶习当面定他的罪。这事，是在我确信他要娶那两位小姐之一以后，出于对那一家的怜悯做的。」
我的头垂在手上。阿盖尔一家的音信，我久已不闻——便是死亡，也未必在我们中间隔出更荒的一片空白。可此刻听见两个姑娘的名字被提起，一阵旧情如潮水兜头打来，我在底下挣扎，几乎窒住。一想到玛丽——那个天真无邪、最甜美最可爱的姑娘——要成为詹姆斯的妻子，一阵剧痛射过我的心。我觉得这事该有人拦下；可我怎么插手？我又为什么想插手？我想起那一刻：她扑向我——说：「*我*信你，理查德；*我*爱你！」——而我知道，对她最后那含泪的热烈表白，我做的那一番解释，到底是没有凭据的。我原是怕她当真爱我，怕她在那悲伤困顿的最后一刻，情感泄露给了她自己知道——我也存过一线指望，盼不是这样。我自己那得不到回答的热情——我那孤身的、无偶的生活——教会了我体恤；我还没有自私到那个地步，会因为这年轻的生命爱着我而虚荣心痒——我对她，除了当真如妹妹，并无他情。
可如今，听说詹姆斯从埃莉诺转到了她身上，我觉得一阵怜惜的刺痛——巴不得她爱的宁可是我，而不是他：他那副冷酷奸诈的心肠，做不成玛丽这样深情的女子安稳的归宿。带着这份遗憾，我又觉得一阵得意：埃莉诺始终不可侵犯，守着她那崇高而孤绝的悲恸高地。这正合我对她的料想。

===Original===

I gloried in her constancy to the dead. I had loved her for this noble beauty of her nature, and should have been disappointed had the test found her wanting in any of the attributes with which my worship had invested her. She had done me a wrong too cruel for me to complain about; but I would rather, still, that she would wrong me than herself.
Lastly, Mr. Burton assured me that he had tidings of the five-hundred-dollar bill which had been stolen from Mr. Argyll’s desk. This was, indeed, important, and I showed by my looks how deeply I was absorbed in the particulars. That bill had come into the hands of Wells, Fargo & Co., about six months after the robbery, having been sold for specie to their agent in California, and forwarded to them along with the other sums which they were constantly receiving. At least, he had taken it for granted that it was the same bill, it being one of the two which left the city of New York the week of the robbery; the other he had traced to St. Louis, and ascertained that no possible suspicious circumstances attached to it.
Wells, Fargo & Co. had given him every assistance in their power to discover who had sold that bill to the California branch of their house; but an answer had been returned from there that the person who disposed of it was a stranger, on his way to the mining regions, whom they had never seen before or since, and whose name they had not taken. The clerk who transacted the brief business with him, had no distinct recollection of him, except that he was rather a thickset man, with an unpleasant expression﻿—doubtless one of the “hard cases” so frequent in the precincts of San Francisco.
Of course, it was clear to us two, who sat in company with the dead-letter, that the five-hundred-dollar bill was a part of the sum referred to by the writer; that it had come out of Mr. Argyll’s desk, and that it was blood-money paid for a murder; and the receiver was this person who, in the letter, so explicitly declared his intention of fleeing to California. We were much excited in the presence of these bold facts. In our enthusiasm, then, it seemed easy to stretch a hand across the continent and lay it upon the guilty. We scarcely realized the long and wearisome pursuit to which we were doomed﻿—the slight clue which we had to the individual whose deeds were yet so patent to us.

===Chinese===
