我为她对亡人的坚贞而荣耀。我爱的，正是她天性里这份高贵的美；倘若这一场试炼叫我看出她缺了我崇拜所加诸的任何一样品性，我倒要失望了。她给我的伤害，狠得叫我无从抱怨；可我宁肯——至今宁肯——她伤害的是我，不是她自己。
最后，伯顿先生向我担保：那张从阿盖尔先生书桌里偷走的五百美元钞票，他有了下落。这实在是件大事；听那细节的时候，我的神色露出了我怎样的全神贯注。那张票子，在失窃后约莫六个月，落进了威尔斯-法戈公司（Wells, Fargo & Co.）手里：有人把它兑成硬币，卖给了他们在加利福尼亚的代理，随同他们源源收到的其他款项，一并发了回来。至少，他是把它当作同一张票子认下的——那票子正是失窃那一礼拜离开纽约的两张之一；另一张，他已追到圣路易斯，查明不牵任何可疑的情节。
威尔斯-法戈公司已倾其所能，帮他查明是谁把那张票子卖给他们加州分号的；可那边的回信说：出手票子的是个过路生人，正往矿区去，前后再没人见过，名字也没有留下。同他办过那桩简短交易的公司职员，对他并无清楚的印象，只记得他是个矮壮汉子，面相不善——想来是旧金山那一带常见的「难缠角色」之一。
当然，我们两个陪着这封死信对坐的人，一眼看得分明：那张五百美元钞票，正是写信人话里点到的款项的一部分；它是从阿盖尔先生的书桌里出来的，是买一条人命的血钱；收钱的就是这个人——信里明明白白宣告了逃往加利福尼亚的打算的这个人。在这些露骨的事实面前，我们都激动起来。在那份热忱里，伸一只手横过大陆、按住罪人，仿佛易如反掌。我们几乎意识不到，等在我们前头的是怎样一场漫长而疲惫的追捕——我们掌握的关于那个人的线索，又何其微末，虽然他的行径在我们眼里已经这样昭彰。

===Original===

At this revelation of conspiracy, my mind eagerly searched about for the accessory, and again settled itself upon Miss Sullivan. It did seem to me that she had thrown a glamour over the usually clear sight of Mr. Burton; so that I resolved to keep a separate watch which should not be influenced by his decisions. While I was thinking of this, Mr. Burton was walking about the floor. Suddenly he stopped before me and looked into mine with those vivid eyes, so full of power, and said, as confidently as if a vision had revealed it to him,
“I have now made out all the meaning of the letter. In the first place, it is written ‘*by contraries*’﻿—that is, it means just the contrary of what it says. The contract *was* fulfilled. The price was expected, the emigration decided upon. The bright day was a rainy night; the picture taken was a human life. And, don’t you see it, Richard?﻿—the old friend was the hiding-place of the instrument of death, after which the accomplice is directed to look. That instrument is the broken toothpick. It was secreted in the pocket of the old friend. Now, who or what is this old friend? Richard, didn’t Leesy affirm she saw a man descending from the old oak tree at the right of the Argyll mansion, on the evening of the murder?”
“She did.”
“Then *that* is it. I want to know no more. The arms are the arms of that old oak. Unless it has been removed, which is not probable, since this letter was never received, the broken knife or dagger (of which I have the point which was taken from the wound), will be found in some hollow on the left side of that oak.”
I gazed at him in astonishment; but he, unconscious of my wonder, sat down, with a relieved, almost happy, expression.

===Chinese===

这一桩阴谋的水落石出，叫我的心急急四下里搜寻那从犯，又一次落定在沙利文小姐身上。我总觉得，是她布下了一道迷障，罩住了伯顿先生平日清明的眼目；于是我决意另立一份自己的侦查，不受他裁断的左右。我正想着，伯顿先生在屋里踱来踱去。忽然，他在我面前站住，用那双亮得逼人的、盛满力量的眼睛望进我的眼睛，那口气笃定得像有异象启示过他：
「如今我把信的意思全数看破了。第一，这封信是『*倒着写*』的——就是说，字面说的，全是反面。契约*履行*了。价款是盼着的，迁居是定了的。晴天是雨夜；拍下的照片，是一条人命。还有，你看出来没有，理查德？——那位老朋友，正是那件凶器的藏身处所，从犯受命去找的，就是它。那件凶器，就是那根断牙签。它就藏在那位老朋友的口袋里。那么，这位老朋友，是谁？是什么？理查德，丽西不是一口咬定，凶案那天傍晚，她看见一个男人从阿盖尔宅邸右边那棵老橡树上下来吗？」
「她说过。」
「那*就是*它了。我不用再知道别的了。那两条胳膊，就是那棵老橡树的胳膊。除非它已经被移走了——这不大可能，因为这封信从未送到——那把折断的刀子或匕首（刺进伤口的那一截刀尖，如今在我手里）就藏在那棵橡树左边枝干的某个洞里。」
我愕然望着他；他却浑然不觉我的惊奇，坐了下去，一脸如释重负的、几乎是幸福的光彩。

## II Our Visits / 二 我们的查访
===Original===

So engrossed were we by our plans, which we were laboring to get into shape, that we forgot the passing hours and the demands of appetite. It was long past the lunch hour when a servant appeared to ask if he should not bring in the tray, having waited in vain for the usual summons. With its appearance Lenore came in, the same lovely, sylphlike little creature, but looking rather less fragile than when I saw her last. At the sight of me, her color went and came﻿—one instant she hesitated, then approached and gave me her hand, with a smile and kiss. Her father had already told of her having made two or three visits to the Argyll mansion within the time of my absence; and I attributed her blushes, upon meeting me, to her frank heart accusing her of the disparaging thoughts she had entertained of me. The subtle influence of James had doubtless, without any necessity for putting the idea into words, warned her against me as a bad man; but now as she looked at me, she was sorry for what she had felt, and disposed to renew her old friendship.
Before lunch was concluded, Mr. Burton fell into a reverie, which he ended by saying,
“We must have the assistance of Lenore, if she can give us any.”
I felt reluctant to see the child placed again in that unnatural trance; but other considerations were even weightier than our fears for the shock to her nervous system; and after she had chatted a while with us, and had sung for me, Mr. Burton subjected her to the experiment. It had been so long since he had exercised his power over her, that it required a greater effort than on the former occasion which I witnessed, to place her in the desired condition. He, however, finally succeeded perfectly. The dead-letter was placed in her hands, when we observed her shrink as if a serpent had glided over her lap; but she did not throw it down, as she seemed moved to do.
“What do you see, Lenore?”
“It is too dark to see. A lamp shines across the walk, and I see a man dropping the letter in the box. He is muffled up so that I can not tell about his face; he steals up and goes off again very quickly.”
“Follow him, Lenore.”
“It is too dark, father. I am lost in the streets. Oh! now I have overtaken him again; he walks so fast﻿—he is short and thick﻿—he looks as if he were afraid of something. He will not pass the police-officer, but crosses the street, and keeps in the shadow.

===Chinese===

我们的计划正在努力成形，一门心思全陷在里面，竟忘了时辰流转，忘了肚子会饿。午饭时辰早过了好一阵，才有一个仆人进来请示，要不要把食盘端进来——往常的传唤他一直没等来。食盘端进来，莉诺也进来了——还是那个可爱的小精灵般的人儿，只是瞧着比我上回见时结实了些。一见我，她脸色一白又一红；迟疑了一瞬，便走上前来，把手伸给我，笑着吻了我。她父亲已经说过，我不在的这几个月里，她到阿盖尔府上去过两三回；我把她见我时的红脸，归给她那颗坦荡的心在自责——责她从前心里对我的那些贬损念头。詹姆斯那股潜移默化的影响，想必不必落成一个字，已教她提防我、当我是个坏人；可此刻她望着我，为先前那份心思难过，一心想同我重续旧日的友谊。午饭没吃完，伯顿先生陷入了沉思；末了他说，
「咱们必须借重莉诺，只要她出得上力。」
眼看这孩子再入那种非自然的入定状态，我很是不忍；可另有几层考虑，分量比我们对她神经受震的忧虑更重。她同我们闲谈了一阵，又给我唱了歌，伯顿先生便对她施行了那套试验。他许久不曾对她行使这股力量了，要她进入理想的状态，比我亲见的那回更费气力。不过末了他还是大功告成。那封死信放到她手里时，只见她猛一缩，像一条蛇滑过了她的膝头；可她虽像要甩脱，到底没有甩。
「你看见什么了，莉诺？」
「太黑了，看不清。一盏灯照着人行道，我看见一个人把信投进信箱。他裹得严严的，认不出面目；他蹑手蹑脚地来，又飞快地走掉了。」
「跟着他，莉诺。」
「太黑了，爸爸。我在街上迷了路。啊！这会儿又追上他了；他走得那样快——又矮又壮——像是怕着什么。他不敢打警察跟前过，偏要穿过街去，贴着暗影走。

===Original===

Now we are at the ferry﻿—it is the Fulton Ferry﻿—I know it well. Oh, dear! the water rises and the wind blows﻿—it is getting morning, but it rains so﻿—and the water is so wild I can not make my way on to the boat.”
“Don’t be discouraged, my child. I would give much to have you follow him across the river, and tell me at what house he stops.”
“The wind blows so,” continued Lenore, pitifully; “all is dark and uncertain. I have missed him﻿—I do not know him from others.”
“Try again, my darling. Look well at the letter.”
“All is dark and uncertain,” she repeated, in a vague tone.
“It is useless,” exclaimed Mr. Burton, in a burst of disappointment; “it has been too long since the letter was penned. The personality of the writer has departed from it. If she had only been able to pursue him to his haunts, our investigations in that vicinity might have richly repaid us.”
Finding it impossible to get any more information from the child, she was relieved from her trance, stimulated with a glass of cordial, and sent up to take a siesta before the hour for dinner. Scarcely had she left the library before I sprung to my feet, exclaiming,
“Good heavens, how easy!﻿—and here I have never thought of it.”
“What is easy?”
“To ascertain who is the John Owen who calls for these letters at Peekskill. Of course﻿—why, what a fool I am!”
“I am afraid you will not find it so easy. People carrying on a correspondence for such a purpose, do not come forward openly for their letters﻿—and this was a good while ago﻿—and it is quite possible this may be the only missive ever sent, through the mail, to that address﻿—and this, evidently, was never called for.”
“At least, it is worth inquiring into,” I added, less triumphantly.
“Of course it is. We wish, also, to ascertain how the letter came dragging along to Washington two years, nearly, behind its time. I propose that we start for Peekskill by the early morning train.”
To wait, even until morning, seemed too tardy for my mood. But as it was now four o’clock, and I had no right to ask the detective to resign his dinner and evening comfort, I made no objection to the time. And, in truth, the time sped more swiftly than I expected; we had still so much to discuss. Dinner came﻿—and the hour of retiring followed﻿—before we had matured our course of action. We were to go to Peekskill and learn all possible about John Owen.

===Chinese===

现在我们到渡口了——是富尔顿渡口——我认得它。哎呀！浪起来了，风刮起来——天快亮了，可雨下得紧——浪太大，我上不去船。」
「别灰心，孩子。只要你能跟他过河，告诉我他在哪家门前落脚，我什么代价都肯出。」
「风刮得这样大，」莉诺可怜巴巴地接着说；「一片黑，一片乱。我把他丢了——我认不出他和别人了。」
「再试试，我的宝贝。好好看着那封信。」
「一片黑，一片乱。」她含含糊糊地重复。
「没有用了，」伯顿先生一阵失望，脱口喊道；「写信人的气息，离开这封信太久了。写信人的那点灵性，早从纸上散尽了。她若能一路追到他出没的巢穴，咱们在那一带的查访，本可以满载而归。」
见再不能从孩子口里问出什么，他们便把她从入定中唤醒过来，灌了她一小杯提神的甜酒，打发她上楼歇午觉，好等晚饭时分。她前脚刚出书房，我便跳了起来，喊道：
「天哪，多容易的事！——我却从来没想到。」
「什么容易？」
「查一查到皮克斯基尔取信的这位约翰·欧文是谁呀。这不明摆着——唉，我真是个蠢货！」
「只怕没那么容易。为了这种用途通信的人，取信是不会露面的——何况事情过去这么久了——而且，寄往那个地址的信，说不定统共就这一封——再说，这封分明就没人来取过。」
「至少，值得查一查。」我添了一句，方才那股得意淡了下去。
「当然值得。咱们还要弄明白，这封信怎么会拖拖拉拉，几乎误了两年才到华盛顿的死信处理处。我提议，明早头一班火车，咱们就动身去皮克斯基尔。」
就算只等到明早，以我的心境也嫌太迟。可这时已四点，我没有权利要侦探舍掉他的晚饭和一晚的安适，便没有异议。其实时间过得比我想的快；要商量的还多着呢。晚饭上了——就寝的时辰也到了——行动计划才商量妥帖。我们要去皮克斯基尔，把约翰·欧文的一切查个明白。

===Original===

If we gained no important information there, we were to go on, in the evening, to Blankville, to enter, under cover of the darkness, the lawn of the Argyll house, and secure the broken knife or dagger, which, we believed, we should find secreted in a certain oak upon the premises. This we wished to do without the knowledge of the family, for two reasons: the smaller one of which was, that I did not wish my visit to be made known, and the larger, that we both were certain we could prosecute our plans more successfully if the friends knew nothing of our efforts. Then, if we still failed to discover the accomplice, we were to sail for California.
The reader may see that we were set upon the accomplishment of our purposes by the willingness with which we gave time, money and mind to our object. I had first proposed the visit to California, avowing my intention to make it, when Mr. Burton had surprised me by offering to be my companion. This was a sacrifice which I could not have asked or expected of him; but he would not allow me to view it in that light, saying, with pleasant peremptoriness, that Lenore needed a sea-voyage; and he had been thinking of taking one on her account. He would make it a pleasure-tour, as well as one of business, “and then,” with a laugh which would have been satirical if it had not been so frank﻿—“he was afraid my mission would not be so successful, if undertaken alone.” And I had answered him that I realized my own inefficiency, as compared with his talent and experience﻿—all I had to encourage me was the devotion with which I undertook my work﻿—to *that*, alone, I trusted to insure me some reward. But if he really were willing to go with me, I should feel almost elated.
We were at Peekskill the next day in good season. We found the same postmaster in service who had been in the office at the time the dead-letter arrived there. When Mr. Burton﻿—I lounging carelessly in the background﻿—showed the envelope and inquired how it had occurred that it had been forwarded to the Department at this late hour, the official showed a little embarrassment, as inferring that he was about to be taken to task for a neglect of duty by some indignant individual.
“I will tell you how it happened, Mr. Owen,” said he, “if you’re the person addressed on that envelope.

===Chinese===

若在那里问不出要紧的东西，我们便当晚接着去布兰克维尔，趁夜色掩蔽摸进阿盖尔家的草坪，取回那柄折断的刀或匕首——我们相信，它就藏在园中那株橡树的某处洞里。这事要瞒着那家人做，缘由有二：小的一条，是我不愿让人知道我回来过；大的一条，是我们俩都拿得准：亲友们对我们的努力一无所知，计划反倒进行得最顺。再不然，若我们仍旧寻不出那个同谋，便扬帆去加利福尼亚。读者可以看出，为了这个目标，我们时间、金钱、心力样样舍得——我们实现目的的心意，是何等坚决。去加利福尼亚是我先提的，我声言志在必行；不料伯顿先生竟主动请缨与我同行。这样的牺牲，我不敢求、也想不到他肯；他却不许我往这上头想，用一种愉快的、不由分说的口气说：莉诺正需要一次海上旅行，为着她，他本来就琢磨着要走一趟。他要把这趟差事顺带变成游历；「再说，」他笑了一声——那笑若不是那样坦荡，简直要算讥诮了——「只怕我这件使命，独自一人去办，未必成得了事。」我答他说，比起他的才具和历练，我深知自己的不济；给我鼓劲的，只有我担当这桩工作时的一腔赤诚——我指望的报酬，全系在*这一样*上。可他若真心肯同去，我几乎要欢欣鼓舞了。第二天一早，我们到了皮克斯基尔。主持邮局的，还是死信寄到那会儿的那位局长。伯顿先生拿出信封，问这信怎么会拖到这么晚才转去华盛顿部里——我懒懒散散倚在后面——那局长面露几分窘色，像是估摸着有哪位气冲冲的人物，要拿渎职的罪名数落他了。
「我把原委说给您听，欧文先生，」他说，「您就是信封上写的那位收信人的话。

===Original===

You never came for the letter, and before the expiration of the time required by law for forwarding it to Washington, it got slipped into a crack, and was never discovered till about a fortnight ago. You see, our place here wasn’t just the thing for an office; it never did suit, and this month, I finally had new boxes and shelves put in, and the room fixed up. In tearing down the old fixings, several letters were discovered which had slipped into a crack between the shelf and wall. This was one of them. I thought, ‘better late than never,’ though at first I had a mind to throw them into the stove. I hope, sir, the loss of the letter hasn’t put you to any very great inconvenience?”
“It was of some importance,” answered my companion, in a commonplace tone, “and I’m not sorry, even yet, to have recovered it, as it settles a matter I had been in doubt about. My man must have been very negligent; I certainly sent him for the letter. Don’t you remember a young man, a coachman, coming for my letters?”
“He never came but twice, to my knowledge,” answered the postmaster, giving a glance of curiosity at the speaker. “I wondered who it was they were for﻿—not being anyone that I knew﻿—and I know mostly everybody in the district. Traveling through our town, perhaps?”
“Yes, I was a stranger, who merely passed two or three times through your village, stopping on business. My usual address is New York. That coachman was hired at the next village to drive me about the country a few days. I have nearly forgotten him. I would like to call him to an account for some of his conduct which was not satisfactory. Can you describe his personal appearance?﻿—though, I suppose, you could not have taken any particular notice of him.”
“It was evening on both occasions of his calling. He was muffled up about the lower part of the face, and his cap pulled down. I couldn’t tell you a thing about him, indeed, except that he had black eyes. If I’m not mistaken, he had black or dark eyes. I think I remember of their looking at me very sharp through the window here. But it was evening, and I shouldn’t mind the circumstance at all if I had not wondered, at the time, who John Owen was. It’s likely the fellow was a rogue﻿—he looked kind of slippery.”
I, listening apart to the conversation, longed to ask if this muffled driver was small and slender, for I was thinking of a woman.

===Chinese===

您一直没来取；还没到法定的转寄华盛顿的期限，这封信就滑进了一道缝里，直到约莫两礼拜前才被发现。您知道，我们这地方做邮局本就不合适；从来没合适过。这个月我终于换了新的信箱和架子，把屋子翻修了一遍。拆旧家什的时候，翻出好几封信来，都是滑进架子同墙的夹缝里的。这就是其中一封。我心想，『迟了总比没有强』——虽说起初还想把它们统统塞进炉子。先生，但愿丢了这封信，没给您添多大的不便吧？」
「它有些要紧，」我的同伴用平平常常的口吻答道，「如今失而复得，我也不觉得亏——它了结了一桩我一直悬心的事。我那个仆人必是怠忽得很；我明明打发他来取过的。您记不记得，有个年轻人，一个马车夫，来取过我的信？」
「据我所知，他统共来过两回。」局长答道，好奇地打量说话的人。「我当时就纳闷信是给谁的——本区的人我几乎没有不认识的，偏偏这名字我不认得。是打我们镇上路过，住店的？」
「是啊，我是个外乡人，路过贵村两三回，因事小住。我平常通信处是纽约。那马车夫是在邻村雇的，替我赶了几天乡下路。我几乎把他忘了。他有些差事办得不叫人满意，我倒想同他算算账。您能说说他的模样么？——不过我猜，您未必对他留过什么心。」
「他两回来，都是傍晚。下半张脸裹着，帽檐压得低低的。说实话，他什么模样我说不上，只知道他有一双黑眼睛。若我没记错，是黑的或深色的眼睛。我记得那双眼睛隔着这扇窗子，很利地盯过我一眼。可那是傍晚，若不是我当时正纳闷约翰·欧文是谁，这点事我才不会放在心上。那家伙多半不是好东西——瞧着滑头滑脑的。」
我在一旁听着这场问答，一心想问一句：那裹得严严的车夫，是不是又矮又瘦？——因为我心里想着的是个女人。

===Original===

While I was studying how to propose the question to Mr. Burton, he continued,
“A smallish fellow, if I remember rightly? I really wish I had his name.”
“Can’t say anything more about it,” was the reply of the postmaster. “I couldn’t answer if he were large or small, white or black, except as to his eyes, which were about all I saw of him. If you want to find out about him, why don’t you go to the livery-keeper who furnished your team to you? Of course, his employer could tell you all you want to know.”
“That *would* be the most sensible course,” answered the detective, with a laugh. “But, my good friend, it is considerably out of my way to go to S﻿—﻿—; and I must leave on the train up, in half an hour. After all, the matter is not of so much importance. I had a curiosity to learn what had kept the letter so long on its travels. Good day, sir.”
It never entered the official’s thoughts to inquire how we came in possession of a document which had not been returned from the Dead-Letter Department﻿—at least, not while we remained with him﻿—though he may afterward have puzzled his brains over the affair.
As we did not wish to arrive in Blankville until after dark, we had to leave the cars once again, and to get off at a little intermediate station, with half a dozen houses clustered about it; and here we whiled away, as we best could, several tedious hours, whose dreariness was only partially soothed by the influences of such a supper as could be obtained in the small public-house attached to the depot.
As the sun drew toward setting and the night approached, a fierce restlessness thrilled along my nerves. That peace﻿—if the dullness and sluggishness of my chilled feelings could be called peace﻿—into which I had forced myself for many months, was broken up. The mere fact of my nearness to the spot which had once been so dear to me, overpowered me with strong attractions; the force of habit and of memory was at work; and when, at twilight, the train stopped and took us up, my mind ran on before the iron-horse, and was at the end of the little journey before the commencement. Upon arriving at Blankville, we descended the rear car and walked up toward the village, without approaching the depot, as I was afraid the lamps might betray me to some former acquaintance.

===Chinese===

我正盘算怎样向伯顿先生提这个问题，他先接了下去：
「是个小个子吧，如果我没记错？我真想知道他叫什么。」
「这个我就说不上来了。」局长答道。「他是高是矮、是白是黑，我都答不上，只有那双眼睛——我看见的差不多也就那双眼睛。您若想打听他，何不去找租马车给您的车行老板？他的东家，自然什么都告诉您。」
「那*确实*是最聪明的路数。」侦探笑着答道。「可是我的好朋友，再折回 S—— 镇去，实在绕得太远；半个钟头后我就得搭北上的火车。说到底，这也不算顶要紧的事。我不过是好奇，什么东西叫这封信在路上走了这么久。日安，先生。」
这封信还没经死信处理处发还，怎么到了我们手里——这一层，那位局长竟没想到问；至少我们没走之前没想到——过后他也许会为这事挠破脑袋。
我们不愿在天黑前到布兰克维尔，只好又中途下车，落在一个小小的中间站上——站旁拢共簇着六七户人家。就在这里，我们想法子捱过了好几个乏味的钟头；车站附设的小酒馆里能弄到什么样的晚饭，就弄来什么样的晚饭，也只勉强冲淡了几分凄清。日头西沉，夜色渐近，一股凶猛的躁动顺着我的神经窜动。那几个月里我强按着自己的心挣来的一份平静——若麻木迟钝、冷透了的知觉也配称平静的话——就此破碎了。仅仅是靠近这个曾经那样亲的地方，就已经用强大的磁力把我攫住；习惯和记忆的力量都在暗中做工。黄昏时火车停下来捎上我们，我的心早已跑在铁马前头——短短一程还没起头，已经到了终点。到了布兰克维尔，我们从尾车厢下来，绕开车站朝村里走——我怕车站的灯，会把我的行藏泄露给哪个旧相识。

===Original===

It was a mild evening, early in September, and I had no excuse for muffling up; so I pulled my hat down over my eyes, quite sure that I should escape recognition, in the dim moonlight, which, overblown by light, thin clouds, transfused the western sky. We walked about, in quiet parts of the village, until ten o’clock; and then, the moon having set, we approached the Argyll mansion, along the well-remembered street. I know not if my companion guessed my disturbance, as I passed the office and came up in front of the lawn, black beneath the starlight, with the shadows of its fine old trees. The past was not half so dead as I had got in the habit of believing it﻿—life is sweet and strong in the heart of youth, which will endure many blows before it will cease to beat with the tremulous thrill of hope and passion.
A bright light was shining from the windows of the parlor and several of the other rooms, but the hall-door was closed, and everything was so quiet about the premises that I did not believe I ran any risk in entering the gate and seeking out the monarch oak﻿—a mighty tree, the pride of the lawn, which stood quite to one side from the central avenue which led up to the front portico, and only some thirty feet from the left corner of the mansion, which was, at times, almost touched by the reach of its outermost branches. We advanced together through the darkness, it being the understanding that, should any accident betray our visit, before its purpose was accomplished, I was to retreat, while Mr. Burton would boldly approach and make the excuse of a call upon Mr. Argyll. My familiarity with the premises and my superiority in the art of climbing, made the duty of ascending the tree devolve upon me. While my companion stood on guard beneath, I drew myself up, carefully making my way through the night, out along to the “second branch to the left,” feeling for the hollow which I knew existed﻿—for, in my more boyish days, I had left no possible point of the grand old tree unvisited. Not five minutes had elapsed since I began my search, before my fingers, pressing into the ragged cavity of the slowly-decaying limb, touched a cold object which I knew to be steel. My hand recoiled with an instinctive shudder, but returned immediately to its duty, cautiously drawing forth a slender instrument of which I could not make out the precise character.

===Chinese===

那是九月初一个温和的夜晚，我没有理由把自己裹起来，便把帽檐拉低遮住眼睛——薄薄的轻云漫过月亮，月色昏昏，浸染着西天，我满有把握不会被人认出来。我们在村里僻静的地方转到十点；月亮落了，我们便沿着那条记得烂熟的街，走近阿盖尔府。打事务所门前经过、走到草坪跟前时——星光下草坪一片黑，浸着老树的浓影——我心里的翻腾，我同伴猜没猜到，我不知道。过去远不像我惯常自以为的那样死透了——青春心里的生命，又甜又韧，任多少番捶打，也还要怀着希望与热恋的震颤跳动，不肯停息。客厅和另外几间屋的窗子透出明亮的灯光，正门却关着，宅子四下里静悄悄的，我料想进大门去找那株橡树之王，不至于冒什么险。那是一株大树，整片草坪的骄傲，立在通往前廊的中央甬道侧旁，离宅子左角不过三十来英尺，最远处的枝条，有时几乎要拂着屋角。我们一同摸黑上前。事先讲定：目的未成而万一败露，由我退走，伯顿先生则大大方方迎上去，托名拜访阿盖尔先生。我熟悉这园子，爬树的本事又比他强，上树的差事便落在我头上。同伴在下面望风，我攀了上去，在夜色里小心挪动，一路探到「左边第二根枝桠」上，摸索我知道必有的那个树洞——少年时代，这株老树身上可去的地方，我没有一处不曾去过。搜寻不过五分钟，我的手指探进那根渐渐朽烂的枝桠上参差的洞口，触到了一样冰凉的东西——我知道，那是钢。我的手本能地一哆嗦缩了回来，随即又回到它的差事上，小心翼翼抽出一柄细长的家伙——它究竟是什么，我一时辨认不出。

===Original===

Upon raising my head, after securing the object of our anxiety, my eyes fell upon a scene which held them fascinated for so long a time that the patience of my friend at the foot of the tree must have been sorely tried.
The windows on the side of the parlor looking on the left were both open, the chandeliers lighted, and from my airy eyrie in the tree, I commanded a full view of the interior. For a time I saw but one person. Sitting by a center-table, directly under the flood of light from the chandelier, was one of the sisters, reading a book. At first﻿—yes, for a full minute﻿—I thought it was Eleanor!﻿—Eleanor as she was, when the homage of my soul first went out toward her, like the exhalation of a flower to the sun﻿—as young, as blooming and radiant as she was before the destroyer came﻿—the dew upon the lip, the light on the brow, the glory of health, youth and joy upon every feature and in every flow of her garments, from the luster of her hair to the glimmer of her silken slipper.
“Can it be?” I murmured. “Is there such power of resuscitation in human vitality as this?”
While I asked myself the question, I was undecided. I saw (and wondered how I could have been mistaken for an instant), that this beautiful woman was Mary, grown so like her older sister, during the months of my absence, as to be almost the counterpart of what Eleanor had been. When I left her she was a girl, half-child, half-woman, bright with the promise of rare sweetness; and now, in this brief summertime of fifteen months﻿—so rapid had the magic culmination been﻿—she had expanded into the perfection of all that is loveliest in her sex. A thoughtfulness, caused, probably, by the misfortune which had befallen the house﻿—a shadow from the cloud which wrapped her sister﻿—toned down the frolicsome gayety which had once characterized her, and added the grace of sentiment to her demeanor. I could not gaze upon the fair, meditative brow without perceiving that Mary had gained in depth of feeling as well as in womanly beauty.

===Chinese===

把这样牵肠挂肚的东西弄到手，我抬起头来，目光落在一幅景象上——那景象迷住我的眼睛，一看便看了许久，树底下我那位朋友的耐性，必定受尽了煎熬。
客厅朝左的那两面窗都敞着，枝形吊灯亮着；从我那高高的树梢瞭望台望下去，屋里的光景一览无余。起初我只看见一个人。姐妹俩中的一个，坐在正中吊灯光流正下方的圆桌旁，读着一本书。起初——是的，足足有一分钟——我以为那是埃莉诺！——是我灵魂的敬慕初次奔向她时的那个埃莉诺，像花朵向着太阳吐纳芳息——是灾星降临之前那样年轻、那样明媚焕发的她：唇上凝着露，额上亮着光，健康、青春和欢乐的荣辉洒满她眉眼之间、裙裾每一道流转，从秀发的光泽，直到绸缎便鞋的一闪。
「可能吗？」我喃喃道。「人的生机里，竟有这样起死回生的力量？」
问着自己这话，我一时犹疑不决。随即我看清了（又奇怪自己怎会有那一瞬的错认）：这位美丽的女子是玛丽。我不在的这几个月里，她出落得同姐姐那样相像，几乎就是当年的埃莉诺的翻版。我离开她时，她还是个姑娘，半是孩子，半是女人，透着一种异日必将极甜的指望；如今，在这短短十五个月的夏天里——那点化的完成竟这样快——她已绽放成她那性别里一切可爱的极致。一层沉静——多半是那场降临门庭的不幸带来的，是她姐姐那片乌云投下的影子——把她从前那副嬉闹欢畅的性子调柔了，又给她的举止添上情致的韵致。望着那光洁沉思的眉宇，我不能不看出：玛丽长进的不只是女子的美，还有感情的深。

===Original===

She wore a dress of some lustrous fabric, which gleamed slumberously in the yellow light, like water shining about a lily; as she bent above her book, her hair clustered about her throat, softening its exquisite outlines; so near, so vivid, was the unconscious *tableau-vivant*, seen through the open frame of the window, that I imagined I heard her breathe, and inhaled the fragrance lingering in her curls and handkerchief.
While I gazed, another figure glided within range of my vision. Eleanor, as I beheld her in my dreams, colorless, robed in black, young still, beautiful still, but crowned, like a queen, with the majesty of her desolation, which kept her apart from sympathy, though not from adoration. Gliding behind her sister’s chair, she bent a moment to see what volume had such attractions, kissed the fair face turned instantly with a smile to hers, and passed away, going out into the hall. I had heard her low “good night.”
Then, almost before she had vanished, came the third figure into the picture. James, approaching as if from some sofa where he had been lounging, took the book from Mary’s hand, which he held a little, saying something which brought blushes to her cheeks. Presently she withdrew her hand; but he caught it again, and kissed it, and I heard him say,
“Oh! Mary, you are cruel with me﻿—you know it.”
Not until I heard him speak, did it rush upon me that I had no business to be there, spying and eavesdropping. I had looked at first, unconscious of the circumstances, like a wandering spirit lingering by the walls of Eden, gazing upon the beauty which is not within its sphere. No sooner did I realize my position than I began to descend from my eyrie; but James had drawn his cousin from her chair, and the pair approached the window, and stood there, their eyes fixed, apparently, upon that very point in the giant oak where I crouched, suddenly fear-blasted, with the square of light from the window illuminating the limb where I lay concealed. I had crawled from my first resting-place, and was about jumping to the ground, when their presence transfixed me, in the most dangerous possible predicament. I dared not move for fear of being discovered. I was paralyzed by a lightning consciousness that should I then and there be betrayed, I would be the victim of a singular combination of *circumstantial evidence*.

===Chinese===

她穿一身光泽莹莹的衣料，在昏黄灯光下沉沉泛亮，像百合四围漾着的水光；她俯身书上，发鬟簇拥着颈项，把那优美的轮廓衬得柔了。隔着敞开的窗框望去，这幅浑然不觉的*活的画*那样近、那样鲜明，我仿佛听得见她的呼吸，闻得到她鬈发和手帕间留驻的芬芳。
正望着，又一个人影滑进了我的视野。埃莉诺——正是我梦中所见的模样：毫无血色，一身黑衣，依旧年轻，依旧美丽，却像王后一般，冠着她的孤寂的庄严；那庄严教人无从亲昵，却教人无从不敬爱。她溜到妹妹椅后，俯身看了一眼那本教人入迷的书，吻了吻那张霎时含笑转向她的脸，便飘然离去，出了厅堂。她那一声低低的「晚安」，我听见了。
接着，她身影未消，第三个人影又进了画。詹姆斯像是刚从哪张沙发上懒懒起身，走过来，把书从玛丽手里拿去，捏着她的手握了一小会儿，说了句什么，说得她双颊绯红。她把手抽了回来；他却又捉住，吻了吻，我听见他说：
「唉！玛丽，你对我太狠心了——你自己知道。」
直到听见他开口，我才猛然醒悟：我守在这里窥探偷听，实在名不正言不顺。起初我看得出神，忘了自己的处境，像个流落的魂灵，徘徊在伊甸园墙外，望着不属于它那个世界的光华。一认清自己的处境，我便开始从瞭望台上往下退；可詹姆斯已把表妹从椅子上拉起来，这一对儿走向窗口，站住了——他们的眼睛，分明正望着大橡树上我蜷伏的那一点。我霎时被恐惧击懵了：窗口那一方灯光，正照亮我藏身的那根枝桠。我已从先前的歇脚处爬开，正要往地下跳，他们俩的到来却把我钉在了原地——钉在最险不过的境地。我怕被发现，一动也不敢动。一道闪电般的意识把我击瘫了：我若此刻此地败露，一整套环环相扣的*旁证*，足够把我钉死。

===Original===

Found lingering at night, like a thief, upon the premises of those I had injured; stealthily seeking to remove the evidence of my guilt﻿—the weapon with which the murder was committed, hidden by me, at the time, in this tree, and now sought for in order to remove it from possible discovery﻿—why, I tell you, reader, had James Argyll sprung upon me there, seized the knife, accused me, nothing would have saved me from condemnation. The probabilities are, that the case would have been so very conclusive, and the guilt so horribly aggravated, that the populace would have taken the matter in their own hands, and torn me to pieces, to show their love of justice. Even the testimony of Mr. Burton would not have availed to turn the tide in my favor; he would have been accused of seeking to hide my sin, and his reputation would not have saved him from the ban of public opinion. A cold sweat broke over me as I thought of it. Not the fear of death, nor of the horror of the world﻿—but dread of the judgment of the two sisters took possession of me. If this statement of my critical position, when the trembling of a bough might convict an innocent man, should make my reader more thoughtful in the matter of circumstantial evidence, I shall be repaid for the pangs which I then endured.
The young couple stepped out upon the sward. I did not trouble myself about what had become of Mr. Burton, for I knew that he was in the shadow, and could retreat with safety; he, doubtless, felt more anxiety about me.
“Draw your scarf up over your head, Mary,” said James, in that soft, pleasant voice of his, which made me burn with dislike as I heard it﻿—“the night is so warm, it will not harm you to be out a few moments. Do not deny me a little interval of happiness tonight.”
As if drawn forward more by his subtle will than by her own wish, she took his arm, and they walked back and forth, twice or thrice, in the light of the window, and paused directly under the limb of the tree, which seemed to shake with the throbbing of my heart. A beam of light fell athwart the face of James, so that I could see its expression, as he talked to the young creature on his arm﻿—a handsome face, dark, glowing with passion and determination, but sinister. I prayed, in my heart, for Mary to have eyes to read it as I read it.
“Mary, you promised me an answer this week. Give it to me tonight.

深更半夜，像贼一样，赖在我伤害过的人家的园子里；鬼鬼祟祟，要销毁自己犯罪的罪证——行凶的那件家伙，当年就藏在这树里，如今又来取走、免得东窗事发——你说说，读者，詹姆斯·阿盖尔若当时当刻扑上来，一把攥住那把刀，振臂一呼，还有什么能救我不遭定罪？多半还用不着法庭：案情那样铁案如山，罪状那样穷凶极恶，老百姓怕是要自己动手，把我撕成几片，好显一显他们敬爱公道。连伯顿先生的证词，也未必扳得回这场风浪；他反倒要被咬作包庇我的罪行，他半生的声名，也救他不被舆论所放逐。想到这里，我出了一身冷汗。攫住我的，不是怕死，也不是怕世人的惊骇——我怕的是那两姐妹的裁决。我那命悬一线的处境——一根枝桠的颤动，便足以给一个无辜者定罪——这番陈述若能让读者对旁证这回事多一分审慎，我当时受的那场苦楚，也就算有了报偿。
这一对年轻人踱到草地上来了。伯顿先生怎样了，我倒不操心——我知道他在暗处，要退随时退得安全；为我悬心的，多半是他。
「把披巾拉上来罩住头，玛丽。」詹姆斯说，用的还是他那副柔婉好听的嗓子——一听见它我就满腔厌恶地烧起来——「夜里这样暖和，出来一小会儿不妨事的。别不给我今晚这一点点快乐的时光。」
她挽上他的胳膊，仿佛更多是被他那份潜移默化的意志牵着走，而不是出于本心；两人在窗光里来回走了两三趟，停在了那根枝桠的正底下——那枝桠仿佛正随着我的心跳在颤。一道光斜落在詹姆斯脸上，他同臂弯里那个年轻生灵说话时，脸上的神情我看得分明——一张英俊的脸，黝黑，燃烧着热恋与决断，却透着凶邪。我在心里祈祷：愿玛丽有一双眼睛，能像我一样读懂它。
「玛丽，你答应过这礼拜给我答复。今晚就给我吧。

===Original===

You have said that you would be my wife﻿—now, tell me how soon I may claim you. I do not believe in long engagements; I want to make you mine before any disaster comes between us.”
“Did I promise you, James? I really did not know that you considered what I said in the light of a promise. Indeed, I am so young, and we have always been such friends﻿—cousins, you know﻿—that I hardly understand my own feelings. I do wish you would not overpersuade me; we might both be sorry. I never believed in the marriage of cousins; so I do not think you ought to feel hurt, cousin James.”
He interrupted the tremulous voice with one a little sharper than his first persuasive tone:
“I am surprised that you do not feel that I regard you as already betrothed to me. I did not think you were a coquette, Mary. And, as for cousinship, I have already told you what I think of it. I know the secret of your reluctance﻿—shall I betray it to you?”
She was silent.
“Your heart is still set on that scoundrel. One might suppose that dread and loathing would be the only sentiment you could entertain toward a traitor and﻿—I will not speak the word, Mary. You took up swords in his defense, and persisted in accusing us of wronging him, against the judgment of your own father and friends. I suspected, then, by the warmth of your avowed friendship for him, that he had, among his other *honorable* deeds, gained my little cousin’s heart, for the pleasure of flattering his self-love. And I shall suspect, if you persist in putting me off, when you know that your father desires our union, and that my whole existence is wrapped up in you, that he still holds it, despite of what has passed.”
“He never ‘gained’ my heart by unfair means,” said the girl, speaking proudly. “I *gave* him what he had of it﻿—and he never knew how large a part that was. I wish he *had* known, poor Richard! for I still believe that you are all wronging him cruelly. I am *his friend*, James, and it hurts me to hear you speak so of him. But that would not prevent my being your friend, too, cousin﻿—”
“You must not say ‘cousin,’ again, Mary. I’m worn out, now, and half mad with my feelings﻿—and it makes me desperate. One thing is certain: I can not stay any longer where you are, if you continue so undecided. I want a final answer tonight.

===Chinese===

你说过愿做我的妻子——现在告诉我，还要多久我才能娶你。我不信什么漫长的订婚；我要赶在任何灾祸插进来之前，把你变成我的人。」
「我答应过你吗，詹姆斯？我真不知道，你竟把我随口的话当成了许诺。实在说，我还这样小，我们又一直是那样好的朋友——表姐弟，你知道的——连我自己的心思都摸不清。求你别再苦劝我了；弄得不好，你我两个都要懊悔的。我从来不信表亲该成婚；所以你也犯不着伤心，詹姆斯表哥。」
他打断了那发颤的声音，嗓音比先前劝诱的调子尖利了些：
「你竟不觉得我早把你当作我的未婚妻，我真意外。我原当你不是个卖弄风情的女子，玛丽。至于表亲这一层，我的想法早就告诉过你了。你推三阻四的缘故，我知道底细——要我替你说破么？」
她不作声。
「你的心还在那个恶棍身上。照理说，对一个叛徒、一个——那个词我不说出口，玛丽——你所能怀的，该只有恐惧和憎恶才对。你却拔剑为他辩护，顶着你自己父亲和亲友的裁断，一口咬定是我们冤枉了他。那时我就疑心，凭你那份公然友谊的热度：他在他那许多*光彩*的行事之外，又骗走了我小表妹的心，好喂一喂他的虚荣。你若还是这样把我悬着——你明知你父亲盼着我们的亲事，明知我的整个性命都系在你身上——我便要疑心：尽管出了这许多事，那颗心如今还在他手里。」
「他从来不曾用什么不正当的手段『骗取』我的心。」姑娘骄傲地说。「他得到的那一点，是我*给*他的——他始终不知道那是多大的一片。我倒真愿他*知道*，可怜的理查德！因为我至今相信，你们大家都在残忍地冤枉他。我是*他的朋友*，詹姆斯，听你这样说他，我心里疼。可这也并不妨碍我也做你的朋友呀，表哥——」
「不许再叫『表哥』了，玛丽。我已经熬坏了，情火烧得我半疯——再这样下去，我就什么都不顾了。有一件事是定了的：你再这样拿不定主意，我就一刻也待不下去了。今晚我要一个最后的答复。

===Original===

If it is unpropitious, I shall go away tomorrow, and seek for such poor fortune as may be mine, in some other part of the world.”
“But what will father do without you, James?”
There was distress and a half-yielding cadence in Mary’s voice.
“That is for you to think of.”
“His health is failing so rapidly of late; and he leans so much upon you﻿—trusts everything to you. I am afraid it would kill him to have all his hopes and plans again frustrated. He has never recovered from the shock of Henry’s death, and Richard’s﻿—going away.”
“If you think so, Mary, why do you any longer hesitate? You acknowledge that you love me as a cousin﻿—let me teach you to love me as a lover. My sweetest, it will make us all so happy.”
But why should I try to repeat here the arguments which I heard?﻿—the main burden of which was the welfare and wishes of her father and sister﻿—mingled with bursts of tender entreaty﻿—and, what was more powerful than all, the exercise of that soft yet terrible will which had worked its way, thus far, against all obstacles. Suffice it, that when the cousins at last﻿—after what seemed to me an age, though it could not have been twenty minutes﻿—returned through the window, I had heard the promise of Mary to become the wife of James before the beginning of another year.
Never was a man more glad to release himself from an unpleasant predicament than I was to descend from my perch when the two figures had passed within the house. My fear of discovery had become absorbed in my keen shame and regret at being compelled to play the eavesdropper to a conversation like that which I had overheard. Moving a few paces in the shadow of the trees, I whispered﻿—“Burton.”
“Got yourself into a pretty scrape,” was instantly answered, in a low tone, as my friend took my arm and we moved forward to the gate. “I didn’t know but we should have a tragico-comedy upon the spot, impromptu and highly interesting.”
“I almost wonder that you are not too greatly out of patience with waiting to jest about the matter.”
“I’ve told you my motto﻿—‘learn to *wait*,’ Richard. The gods will not be hurried; but have you the knife?”
“Ay!” was my grim answer; I felt grim, as I grasped the treacherous, murderous thing which had wrought such deadly mischief.

===Chinese===

若是答复不吉利，我明天就走，到世界别的角落里，去碰我那份可怜的运气。」
「可是你走了，爸爸怎么办呢，詹姆斯？」
玛丽的声音里，带着愁苦，带着半分将就的软调。
「这该由你来想。」
「他近来身子垮得多快啊；他那样倚重你——什么都信托你。他的指望和打算若再一次落空，我怕真要了他的命。亨利的死，还有理查德的——出走，他始终没有缓过来。」
「你既这样想，玛丽，还犹豫什么呢？你承认你像爱表哥一样爱我——就让我教你怎么像爱情人一样爱我吧。我最亲爱的人儿，那样我们一家就都齐全了。」
可我何必在这里重述那一晚听到的话呢？——话的大宗，无非是她父亲和姐姐的安康与心愿，其间夹着一阵阵温柔的恳求；而比这一切都更有力的，是那股柔软而可怕的意志的施为——一路披荆斩棘走到今天的，正是它。只消说一句：等这对表姐弟终于——在我看来隔了一世，其实未必有二十分钟——从窗口回进屋里时，我已亲耳听见玛丽许下诺言：不等新的一年开年头，就做詹姆斯的妻子。
两个人影进屋之后，我从栖身处爬下来——从难堪的处境里脱身，天底下没有第二个人比我更如释重负。怕被发觉的心思，已被更尖锐的羞惭和痛悔吞没了——我竟被迫充当了偷听者，偷听的还是那样一场私谈。我在树影里挪了几步，低声唤：「伯顿。」
「好一出险局。」暗处立刻低低应了一声，我的朋友挽住我的胳膊，我们朝大门走去。「我还当咱们要看一出悲喜闹剧呢——当场即兴，精彩纷呈。」
「我倒奇怪，你等了这半晌，居然还有心思打趣。」
「我的座右铭早告诉过你——『学会*等待*』，理查德。神明是不受催逼的。——刀拿到了吗？」
「拿到了！」我沉沉地答；攥着那件凶残嗜血的、酿成这滔天祸端的东西，我心里只有沉。

===Original===

The sound of shutters drawn together startled us into a quicker pace; we looked back and saw the lower part of the house dark﻿—hurried forward, and without any molestation, or our presence in Blankville being known to a single acquaintance, took the night-train back to New York, which we reached about two a.m. and were at Mr. Burton’s house, ringing up the surprised servants, shortly after.
It was not until we were in the library, with the doors closed, and the full blaze of a gas-burner turned on, that I took from my pocket the weapon, and handed it to my companion.
Both of us bent curiously forward to examine it.
“This,” said the detective, in a surprised and somewhat agitated tone, “is a surgical instrument. You see, it is quite unlike a common knife. It corroborates one of my conclusions. I told you the blow was dealt by a practiced hand﻿—it has been dealt by one skilled in anatomy. There’s another link in my chain. I hope I shall have patience until I shall have forged it together about the guilty.”
“There is no longer any doubt about the dead-letter referring to the murder. You see the instrument is broken,” I remarked.
“No doubt, indeed,” and Mr. Burton went to a drawer of a secretary standing in the room, and took out the little piece of steel which had been found in Henry Moreland’s body.
“You see it is the very fragment. I obtained this important bit of evidence, and laid it away, after others had given up all efforts to make it available. How fortunate that I preserved it! So, the wedding is to take place within three months, is it? Richard, we must not rest now. A great deal can be done in three months, and I would give all the gold I have in bank to clear this matter up before that marriage takes place. Should *that* once be consummated before we are satisfied with our investigations, I shall drop them forever. A doctor﻿—a doctor”﻿—he continued, musingly﻿—“I knew the fellow had half-studied some profession﻿—he was a surgeon﻿—yes! By George!” he exclaimed, presently, leaping from his chair as if he had been shot, and walking rapidly across the room and back.
I knew he was very much excited, for it was the first time I had heard him use any expression like the above. I waited for him to tell me what had flashed into his mind so suddenly.
“The fellow who married Leesy’s cousin, and ran away from her, was a doctor﻿—Miss Sullivan has told me that.

===Chinese===

百叶窗合拢的声音惊得我们加快脚步；回头望去，宅子底层已黑了——我们急急前行，无人阻拦，也未曾有一个熟人知道我们来过布兰克维尔——搭上夜车回纽约，约莫凌晨两点到站，不多时便已立在伯顿先生家门前，把睡眼惺忪的仆人们唤起来开门。
直到进了书房、关上门、拧亮煤气灯满堂的光，我才从口袋里取出那件凶器，递给我的同伴。我们俩都好奇地俯身细看。
「这一件，」侦探说，声音里带着惊讶，也有几分激动，「是外科器械。你看，它同寻常的刀全然不同。这证实了我的一个推断。我说过，那一击出自惯于出手之人——如今更明确了：出自精通解剖之手。我的链子上又添了一环。但愿我有耐心，等把它在罪人身上锻拢。」
「那封死信与凶案的干系，再无可疑了。你看，器械是折断的。」我说。
「确实再无可疑。」伯顿先生走到房中立着的写字台前，拉开抽屉，取出那一小片钢——从亨利·莫兰身体里取出来的那一片。
「你看，正是这一片。旁人都死心、都当它派不上用场了，我却把这要紧的物证弄到手，收了起来。亏得我收着！这么说，婚礼三个月内就要办了，是不是？理查德，我们如今片刻也歇不得。三个月里能办成多少事！我情愿把银行里的金子都倒出来，只求赶在那桩婚事之前查个水落石出。*那桩婚事*若在我们查得称心之前一成了局，我便永远撂手，再不问了。一个医生——一个医生——」他若有所思地接下去——「我早觉出那家伙半途读过什么行当——他是位外科郎中——对了！嗐呀！」说到这里他猛地跳起来，像中了枪似的，在房里疾步走了一个来回。
我知道他激动极了——这样口没遮拦的惊叹，我还是头一回听见。我等着他说出那道忽然闪进心里的光。
「那个娶了丽西表姐、又弃她而逃的家伙，是个医生——沙利文小姐这样告诉过我。

===Original===

Richard, I begin to see light!﻿—day is breaking!”
I hardly knew whether his speech was figurative or literal, as day was really breaking upon us two men, plotting there in the night, as if we were the criminals instead of their relentless pursuers.
“Three months! There will be time, Richard!” and Mr. Burton actually flung his arms about me, in a burst of exultation.

===Chinese===

理查德，我望见亮光了！——天要亮了！」
他这话是玄言还是实指，我几乎分不清——天，真的正在破晓，照着我们这两个男人：我们彻夜密谋，倒仿佛我们才是罪犯，而不是罪犯们穷追不舍的猎手。
「三个月！来得及，理查德！」伯顿先生竟一把张开双臂，在狂喜中紧紧搂住了我。

## III The Confession / 三 供词
===Original===

In the afternoon we paid Miss Sullivan a visit. It was the first time I had met her since that strange night of watching at Moreland villa; and I confess that I could not meet her without an inward shudder of abhorrence. Unbounded as was my respect and confidence for Mr. Burton, I did think that he had erred in his conclusions as to the character of this woman; or else that he concealed from me his real opinions, for some purpose to be explained at the proper time. If he still had suspicions, it was evident that he had kept them from their object as skillfully as from me, for I saw, by her manner of receiving him, that she regarded him as a friend.
Notwithstanding I had been informed of her rapidly-failing health, I was shocked at the change in Miss Sullivan since I had seen her. It was with an effort that she rose from her easy-chair at our approach; the fullness had all wasted from her naturally queenly figure; her cheeks were hollow, and aflame with the fire of fever; while those black eyes, which had ever seemed to smolder above unfathomable depths of volcanic passion, now almost blazed with light. Something like a smile flitted across her face when she saw my companion, but smiles were too strange there to feel at home, and it vanished as soon as seen. I do not think she liked me any better than I did her; each recoiled from the other instinctively; she would not have spoken to me had I come alone; but out of concession to the presence of her friend, she bowed to me and asked me to be seated. A little child in the room ran to Mr. Burton, as if expecting the package of bonbons which he took from his pocket; but, as he became engaged in conversation with Leesy, I coaxed her over to me, where she was soon sitting on my knee. She was a pretty little girl, about three years old, in whose chubby little features I could no longer trace any resemblance to her “aunt.” She prattled after the fashion of children, and in listening to her, I lost a remark or two of Mr. Barton’s; but soon had my attention aroused by hearing Miss Sullivan exclaim,
“Going away! For how long?”
“Three months, at least.”
Her hands sunk in her lap, and she became pale and agitated.
“It is presumptuous in me to dare to be sorry; I am nothing to you; but you are much to me. I don’t know how we shall get along without you.”
“Don’t be uneasy about that, my child.

===Chinese===

当天下午，我们拜访了沙利文小姐。自莫兰别墅那个奇异的守夜之后，这是我头一回见到她；我承认，同她见面，我心里止不住一阵憎恶的寒栗。我对伯顿先生的敬重和信任固然无边无际，我却总以为，他对这女人性情的论断是错了；不然，就是他为了某种待时而宣的目的，把真意见瞒着我。他若仍有疑心，那他瞒这疑心的对象，显然同瞒我一样巧妙；因为从她接待他的态度看得出，她把他当作朋友。
虽然早有人告诉我她的身体正在急速垮下去，重见沙利文小姐，我还是为她形容的改变吃了一惊。我们走近时，她从安乐椅里起身，已是很费一把力；她天生一副雍容的身材，如今丰润消磨殆尽；两颊陷了下去，烧着热病一般的火；那双黑眼睛——从前总像在深不见底的火山情欲上头阴燃着——此刻几乎灼灼放光。见到我的同伴，她脸上掠过一点近乎微笑的东西；可微笑在那里太生疏，安不了家，一现便消失了。我想她不比我更喜欢我；彼此都本能地退避；倘若我独自来，她是不会同我说话的；但看在朋友在场的份上，她朝我点点头，请我坐。屋里一个小孩子朝伯顿先生跑去，像是盼着他从口袋里摸出来的那包糖果；趁他同丽西谈起来，我把孩子哄到我这边，不一会儿她就坐上了我的膝头。那是个漂亮的小姑娘，约莫三岁，圆嘟嘟的小脸蛋上，我再寻不出一丝同她「姨妈」相像的地方。她照孩子们的样子咿咿呀呀；听她说话，我漏了伯顿先生的一两句话；随即，沙利文小姐一声惊呼，唤起了我的注意：
「要走！走多久？」
「至少三个月。」
她的双手沉进膝间，脸色发白，心绪大乱。
「我竟敢难过，实在放肆；我于您算不得什么；可您于我，分量重得很。没有您，我不知道我们怎么撑下去。」
「这个不必担心，我的孩子。

===Original===

I shall make arrangements with this same person who boards you now to keep you until my return, and, if you should fall sick, to take good care of you.”
“You are far too good,” she responded, tremulously. “You will have the blessing of the friendless. I only wish it had the power to bring you good luck on your journey.”
“Perhaps it will,” he said, with a smile. “I have a great deal of faith in such blessings. But, Leesy, I think you can assist my journey in even a more tangible way than that.”
She looked at him inquiringly.
“I want you to tell me all and everything you know about the father of little Nora.”
“Why, sir?” she quickly asked. “I hope you have not heard from him,” looking over toward the child, as if afraid it might be snatched from her.
“Your health is very far gone, Leesy; I suppose you hardly hope ever to recover it. Would you not be glad to see Nora under her father’s protection before you were taken away?”
She stretched out her arms for the child, who slid off my knee, ran and climbed into her lap, where she held the curly head close to her bosom for a moment; her attitude was as if she sheltered the little one from threatened danger.
“I know, much more surely than anyone else, that my days are numbered. I believe I shall never see your face again, Mr. Burton; and that was what grieved me when you spoke of going away﻿—it was not that I thought of my comfort so much. The winter snow will hide me before you come back from your journey; and my darling will be left friendless. I know it﻿—it is my only care. But I would rather, far rather, leave her to the cold charity of an orphan asylum﻿—yes, I would rather turn her upon the street, with her innocent face only for a protector﻿—than that her father should have aught to do with Nora.”
“Why?”
“Because he is a bad man.”
“I understand that he is in California; and as I am going to San Francisco, and perhaps shall visit the mining regions before my return, I thought you might wish to send him a message, telling him the child’s condition. He may have laid up money by this time, and be able to send you a sum sufficient to provide for little Nora until she is old enough to take care of herself.”
She only shook her head, drawing the child closer, with a shudder.
“I have forgotten his name,” said Mr. Burton.

===Chinese===

「我会同如今照应你的这位房东讲好，让她照看你到我回来；万一你病了，也好好侍候你。」
「您实在好得过了头，」她颤声答道。「无亲无故的人会为您祝福。只愿这祝福有力气，为您这一路带来好运。」
「说不定真有力气呢，」他含笑说。「这样的祝福，我信得很。可是，丽西，我想你能帮我的行程，还有比这更实在的一样。」
她探问地望着他。
「我要你把你知道的、关于小诺拉父亲的一切，原原本本讲给我。」
「为什么呢，先生？」她急忙问。「但愿您没有听到他的消息，」说着朝孩子那边望过去，仿佛怕她会被人夺走。
「你的身子已经很亏了，丽西；我想你也不指望痊愈了吧。倘若在你被带走之前，能看见诺拉得到父亲的照管，你不愿意吗？」
她朝孩子伸出双臂；孩子从我膝上溜下，跑过去爬上她的膝头；她把那颗长着鬈发的头紧紧搜在胸前；那姿势，像是护着小的躲开什么威胁。
「比谁都清楚，我知道自己的日子有数。我想我再見不到您的脸了，伯顿先生；方才您说要走，我心里难过，为的就是这个——倒不是多想了自身的安逸。您这趟远门回来之前，冬雪已经把我盖上了；我的宝贝要孤零零丢在人世。我知道——这是我唯一挂心的事。可我宁肯——宁肯得远远的——把她留给孤儿院冷冰冰的慈善——是的，我宁肯把她掠到街上，单凭她那张天真脸蛋做保护人——也不叫她父亲同诺拉有半点相干。」
「为什么？」
「因为他是个坏人。」
「我听说他在加利福尼亚；我正要去旧金山，回来之前说不定还要去一趟矿区；我想你也许愿意捎个信给他，告诉他孩子眼下的情形。他到这时或许攒下了钱，能给你寄一笔来，供养小诺拉，直到她大到能自己照管自己。」
她只是摇头，把孩子搂得更紧，一阵寒噤。
「我忘记他叫什么了，」伯顿先生说。

===Original===

“I will not tell you,” answered Miss Sullivan, with a return of the old fierceness, like that of a hunted panther. “Why can I never, never, never be let alone?”
“Do you think I would do anything for your injury or disadvantage?” asked the detective in that gentle yet penetrating voice which had such power to move people to his will.
“I do not know,” she cried; “you have seemed to be my friend. But how do I know that it is not all simply to compass my destruction at last? You have brought into my house that person,” looking at me, “who has persecuted me. You promised me that I should be free from him. And now you want to set a bloodhound on my track﻿—as if I must be driven into my grave, and not allowed to go in peace.”
“I assure you, Leesy, I had no idea that you regarded Nora’s father with so much dislike. I have no object in the world in troubling you with him. I promise you that no word of mine shall give him the clue to your present circumstances, nor to the fact that he has a child living, if he is ignorant of it. You shall be protected﻿—you shall have peace and comfort. What I would like is, that you shall give me a history of his life, his habits, character, where he lived, what was his business, etc.; and I will give you my reasons for wishing the information. A circumstance has come to light which connects him with an affair which I am investigating﻿—that is, if he is the person I think he is﻿—a sort of a doctor, I believe?”
Miss Sullivan did not answer the question so skillfully put; she still watched us with shining, half-sullen eyes, as if ready to put forth a claw from the velvet, if we approached too near.
“Come, Leesy, you must tell me what I want to hear.” Mr. Burton’s air was now that of a master. “Time is precious. I can not wait upon a woman’s whim. I have promised you﻿—and repeat it, upon my honor﻿—that no annoyance or injury shall come to *you* through what you may tell me. If you prefer to answer me quietly to being compelled to answer before a court, all is right. I *must* know what I desire about this man.”
“*Man*, Mr. Burton! Call him creature.”
“Very well, creature, Leesy. You know him better than I do, and if you say he is a creature, I suppose I may take it for granted. His name is﻿—”
“Or was, George Thorley.”
When the name was spoken, I gave a start which attracted the attention of both my companions.

===Chinese===

「我不告诉您，」沙利文小姐答道，旧日那股凶悍又回来了，像一头被追猎的豹。「为什么就永远、永远、永远不能放我清净？」
「你以为我会做什么伤你、害你的事吗？」侦探用他那又温和又透彻的嗓音问——那嗓音，向来有本事把人拨到他的意志上去。
「我不知道，」她叫道；「您素来像是我的朋友。可我怎么知道，这一切到头来不是为了把我除掉？您把那个人带进了我的家门，」她朝我望了一眼，「那迫害过我的人。您答应过我，叫我摆脱他。如今您又要放一条猎犬咬我的踪迹——仿佛非把我逼进坟墓不可，连安安静静地去都不许。」
「我向你担保，丽西，我全不知道你这样厌恶诺拉的父亲。我在这世上没有半点目的，要拿他来搅扰你。我答应你：我的话，一个字也不会给他线索，教他摸到你眼下的境况，也不会教他知道他还有一个活着的孩子——倘若他不知道的话。你会受保护——你会得到安宁和照应。我想请你做的，是把他的生平给我讲一讲：他的习性、品行，住在哪里，做什么营生，等等；我再把要这消息的缘由说给你。如今出了一桩情形，把他同我手头查办的一件事牵上了——倘若他确是我疑心的那个人——一个半路的郎中，我记得是？」
沙利文小姐没有正面回答这个问得这样巧的问题；她仍旧用那双亮闪闪的、半愠怒的眼睛盯着我们，仿佛我们再走近一步，天鹅绒里就要探出一只爪子。
「说吧，丽西，我要知道的，你非告诉我不可。」伯顿先生此刻是一副主人的气派。「时间宝贵。我不能等一个女人使性子。我答应过你——再凭我的名誉说一遍——凡你告诉我的，断不会叫*你*烦恼或受害。你若宁可安安静静答我，也不愿被逼到法庭上当众作答，那就再好不过。这个人的事，我想知道的，我*必须*知道。」
「*人*，伯顿先生！叫他畜生还差不多。」
「很好，畜生就畜生，丽西。你比我了解他；你说他是畜生，我就只当是定论。他的名字是——」
「从前叫，乔治·索利（George Thorley）。」
这名字一出口，我一个激灵，惊动了两位同伴。

===Original===

“You probably know something about him, Mr. Redfield,” remarked the girl.
“George Thorley, of Blankville, who used to have an apothecary shop in the lower part of the village, and who left the place some three years ago, to escape the talk occasioned by a suspicious case of malpractice, in which he was reported to be concerned?”
“The same person, sir. Did you know him?”
“I can not say that I was acquainted with him. I do not remember that I ever spoke a word with him. But I knew him, by sight, very well. He had a face which made people look twice at him. I think I bought some trifles in his shop once. And the gossip there was about him at the time he ran away, fixed his name in my memory. I was almost a stranger then in Blankville﻿—had lived there only about a year.”
“How did he come to have any connection with your family, Leesy?”
Miss Sullivan had grown pale during the agitation of our talk, but she flushed again at the question, hesitated, and finally, looking the detective full in the eyes, answered:
“Since you have promised, upon your honor, not to disturb me any further about this matter, and since I am under obligations to you, sir, which I can not forget, I will tell you the rest of the story, a part of which I told you that morning at Moreland villa. I confessed to you, there, the secret of my own heart, as I never confessed it to any but God, and I told you something of my cousin’s history to satisfy you about the child. I will now tell you all I know of George Thorley, which is more than I wish I knew. The first time I ever saw him was over four years ago, a short time after he set up his little shop, which, you recollect, was not far from my aunt’s in Blankville. My aunt sent me, one evening, for something to relieve the toothache, and I went into the nearest place, which was the new one. There was no one in but the owner. I was surprised by the great politeness with which he treated me, and the interest he seemed to take in the case of my aunt. He was a long time putting up the medicine, pasting the label on, and making change, so that I thought my aunt would surely be out of temper before I could bring her the drops. He asked our name, and where we lived, which was all, I thought, but a bit of his blarney, to get the good will of his customers.”

===Chinese===

「您多半知道他的底细，雷德菲尔德先生，」姑娘说。
「布兰克维尔的乔治·索利？在村子下头开着一间药铺，约莫三年前为了躲避一桩可疑的医疗事故招来的闲话走掉的？——那桩案子，据说同他有牵连。」
「正是这个人，先生。您认得他？」
「说不上相识。我不记得同他讲过一句话。可他的模样，我认得很熟。他生了一张叫人要看上去第二眼的脸。我记得还在他铺子里买过一两样小东西。他逃走前后那一阵的风言风语，把他的名字钉进了我的记忆。那时我在布兰克维尔还算生客——总共才住了一年上下。」
「他怎么会同你家有了来往，丽西？」
方才一番激动的谈话里，沙利文小姐的脸白了；听到这一问，她重新红了脸，迟疑了一下，终于直直望住侦探的眼睛，答道：
「既然您凭名誉应承过，此事不再来搅扰我；又既然我受着您的恩，先生，忘也忘不掉，那我就把这故事的其余部分讲给您——那天早晨在莫兰别墅，我已讲过一部分。在那里，我把我心底的秘密向您招认了——除了上帝，我从不曾招认给任何人；为了叫您明白孩子的来历，我也讲了些我表妹的事。如今我把乔治·索利的事，凡我知道的，全讲给您——知道的这许多，但愿我从来没知道。我头一回见他，是四年多前，他那升小铺子开张后不久——您记得，铺子离我姨妈在布兰克维尔的家不远。有天傍晚，姨妈打发我去买点牙痛药，我就进了最近的一家，正是那家新铺子。店里只有掌柜的一个人。他待我那样客气，又那样惦记我姨妈的病，倒叫我吃了一惊。他配药、贴标签、找零钱，磨蹭了老半天；我心想，等我拿了药水回去，姨妈准要发脾气了。他问了我们的姓名、住处；我当时只当那是他讨顾客欢心的一点花言巧语。」

===Original===

(Miss Sullivan usually spoke with great propriety, but occasionally a touch of her mother’s country, in accent or expression, betrayed her Irish origin.) “That was the beginning of our acquaintance, but not the end of it. It was but a few days before he made an excuse to call at our house. I was a young girl, then, gay and healthy; and the plain truth of it is that George Thorley fell in love with me. My aunt was very much flattered, telling me I would be a fool not to encourage him﻿—that he was a doctor and a gentleman﻿—and would keep his wife like a lady﻿—that there would be no more going out to sew and slave for others, if I were once married to him; it was only what she expected of me, that I would at least be a doctor’s wife, after the schooling she had given me, and with the good looks I had. It is no vanity in me, now, to say of this clay, so soon to be mingled with the dust of the earth, that it was beautiful﻿—too much so, alas, for my own peace of mind﻿—for it made me despise the humble and honest suitors who might have secured me a lowly, happy life. Yet it was not that, either, and I’ll not demean myself to say so﻿—it was not because I was handsome that I held myself aloof from those in my own station; it was because I felt that I had thoughts and tastes they could not understand﻿—that my life was above theirs in hope, in aspiration. I was ambitious, but only to develop the best that was in me. If I could only be a needlewoman all my days, then I would be so skillful and so fanciful with my work, as almost to paint pictures with my needle and thread. But this isn’t telling you about George Thorley. From the first I took a dislike to him. I’m not good at reading character, but I understood his pretty thoroughly, and I was afraid of him. I was very cold to him, for I saw that he was of a quick temper, and I did not mean he should say that I had ever encouraged him. I told my aunt I did not think he was a gentleman﻿—I had seen plenty of real gentlemen in the houses where I sewed, and they were not like him. I told her, too, that he had a violent temper, and a jealous disposition, and could not make any woman happy. But she would not think of him in that light; her heart was set on the apothecary’s shop, which, she said, would grow into a fine drugstore with the doctor’s name in gilt letters on the door of his office.

===Chinese===

（沙利文小姐说话素来很得体，只是偶尔一个乡音、一个土词，露出她母亲故土的印记，泄了她爱尔兰的血统。）「我们同他相识，那是起头，不是结尾。不出几天，他就寻了个由头，登门来了。那时我是个年轻姑娘，欢蹦乱跳；说白了，乔治·索利爱上了我。我姨妈受宠若惊，说我若不巴结他，就是个傻瓜——说他是个大夫，又是个上等人——娶过去会像太太一样养着我——只要嫁了他，再不用出门给人家缝衣受累；她说我受了她的教养，又生了这样一副好相貌，嫁个医生的太太，原是她对我的指望，起码也得这样。如今我说这副皮囊美——它转眼就要归入地上的尘土了——并不是自夸；它确实美——唉，美得过了头，坏了我自己的安宁——因为它叫我瞧不起那些本分老实的求婚人，他们本可以给我一份低微而快活的日子。可也不尽然是那样，我也不肯自贬到那样说——我同本阶级的人保持距离，并不是因为我生得俊；是因为我自觉有些心思、有些趣味，是他们懂不了的——自觉我的人生在指望上、在志向上，高出他们一等。我有野心，可无非是要把身上最好的东西发挥出来。倘若注定一辈子做针线，那我也要做得又巧又别致，几乎拿针当笔、拿线当色，绣出画来。可这讲的不是乔治·索利。打一开头，我就看不上他。我看人不大在行，他的为人，我却摸得七七八八，我怕他。我对他冷得很，因为我看出他脾气急，我不给他半点由头，说我曾经撩拨过他。我告诉姨妈，说他不像个上等人——我出门做工的人家，见过的是真正的上等人，不是他那副样子。我还告诉她，他脾气暴，性子妒，讨不到任何一个女人的好日子。可她偏不肯那样想他；她一门心思在那升药材铺上，说它会发达成一家漂亮的大药房，诊室的门上，用金字漆着大夫的名字。

===Original===

“George soon offered himself, and was terribly angry when I refused him. I believe he loved me, in his selfish way, better than he loved any other human creature. He would not give me up, nor allow me any peace from his persecutions. He dogged my steps whenever I went out, and if I spoke to any other man, it put him in a rage. I got to feeling that I was watched all the time; for sometimes he would laugh in his hateful way, and tell me of things he had seen when I thought him miles away.
“Twice, in particular, I remember of his being in a savage passion, and threatening me. It was after”﻿—here the speaker’s voice, despite of her efforts to keep it steady, trembled and sunk﻿—“he had seen me riding out in the carriage with Mrs. Moreland. He said those people were making a fool of me﻿—that I was so set up, by their attentions, as to despise him. I told him that if I despised him, it was not for any such reason. It was because he behaved so ungentlemanly toward me, spying around me, when he had no business whatever with my affairs. That made him madder than ever, and he muttered words which I did not like. I told him I was not afraid of any mortal thing, and I didn’t think he would frighten me into marrying him. He said he would scare me yet, so that I would never get over it. I think he liked the spirit I showed; it seemed the more I tried to make him hate me, the more determined he was to pursue me. I don’t know how it was that I understood him so well, for in those days there had been nothing whispered against his character. Indeed, people didn’t know much about him; and he got himself into the good graces of some of the leading citizens of Blankville. He had told me something of his history; that is, that his family were English; that he, like myself, was an orphan; that, by dint of good luck, he had got a place in a doctor’s office in one of the towns in this State﻿—one of those humble situations where he was expected to take care of the physician’s horse, drive the carriage, put up medicines, attend upon orders, and anything and everything. He was smart and quick; he had many hours of leisure when waiting behind the little counter, and these hours he spent in studying the doctor’s books, which he managed to get hold of one at a time.

===Chinese===

「乔治不久便来提亲，叫我回绝了，气得要命。我相信，他爱我爱得自私，胜过爱世上任何活物。他不肯放我，也不容我有半日安生，一味缠磨。我一出门，他就钉着我的脚步；我同别的男人说一句话，他就暴跳如雷。我渐渐觉得时时刻刻都有人盯着；有时他咧着他那副讨厌的嘴笑，说起一些我当他远在数里之外时他亲眼看见的事。
「有两回，我记得他怒得像头野兽，威胁我。那是在——」说到这里，说话的人尽管强自镇定，声音还是抖了、低了——「在他看见我坐莫兰太太的马车出游之后。他说那些人拿我当猴耍——说我叫那家的抬举抬得晕了头，竟看不起他。我对他说，我看不起他，不为这个；为他待我全无上等人的体统，鬼鬼祟祟盯着我，而我的事同他毫不相干。这话叫他越发的怒，他咕哝出几句我不爱听的话。我说我什么都不怕，谅他也吓不着我嫁他。他说他迟早要吓我一遭，叫我一辈子缓不过来。我想他倒喜欢我这股烈性；我越想叫他恨我，他越铁了心追我。我也不明白我怎么把他摸得这样透，因为那时候，他的名声还没有一句闲话。说真的，人们对他知道得不多；他还巴结上了布兰克维尔几位头面人物，得了他们的青眼。他对我说过些他的来历：说家里是英国人；说他同我一样，也是个孤儿；说靠着一桩好运，他在本州一个镇上的医生诊所得了个位置——就是那种低微的差事：看马、驾车、配药、跑腿，什么都干。他人精明麻利；守着那升小柜台，有的是闲工夫，他就拿这些工夫读大夫的书——想法子一本一本弄到手。

===Original===

By these means, and by observing keenly the physician’s methods, his advice to patients who called at the office, and by reading and putting up prescriptions constantly, he picked up a really surprising smattering of science. Making up his mind to be a doctor, and to keep a drugstore (a profitable business, he knew) he had the energy to carry out his plans. How he finally obtained the capital to set up the little business in Blankville, I never understood, but I knew that he attended lectures on surgery, one winter, in New York, and was in a hospital there a short time. All this was fair enough, and proved him ambitious and energetic; but I did not like or trust him. There was something dark and hidden in the workings of his mind, from which I shrunk. I knew him, too, to be cruel. I could see it in his manner of treating children and animals; there was nothing he liked so well as to practice his half-learned art of surgery upon some unfortunate sufferer. The more he insisted on my liking him, the more I grew to dread him.
“Affairs were at this crisis when my cousin came from New York to pay my aunt a visit. Coming to our rooms almost every evening, of course he made her acquaintance immediately. For the purpose of making me jealous, he began to pay the most devoted attention to her. Nora was a pretty girl, with blue eyes and fair hair; an innocent-minded thing, not very sharp, apprenticed to a milliner in the city; she believed all that *Doctor* Thorley told her, and fell in love with him, of course. When she went away, after her little holiday, George found that, instead of provoking me to jealousy, he had only roused my temper at the way he had fooled Nora. I scolded him well for it, and ended by telling him that I never would speak to him again.
“Well, it was just after that the scandal arose about his causing the death of a person by malpractice. He found it was prudent to run away; so he sold his stock for what he could get, and hid himself in New York. I did not know, at first, where he was; but felt so relieved to be rid of him. I had made up my own mind to go to New York, and get employment in a fancy-store. You know, Mr. Burton, for I once laid my heart before you, what wild, mad, but sinless infatuation it was which drew me there. I am not ashamed of it. God is love.

===Chinese===

「靠这些，也靠他冷眼细看大夫的手段、诊室里给病人的嘱咐，加上读方子、配方子，他居然拾掇起一门着实惊人的皮毛学问。他打定主意要做医生、开药房（他知道那是赚钱的行当），也有那股劲头把计划做成。他最后从哪里弄到本钱，在布兰克维尔开起那升小店，我始终没弄明白；我只知道有一年冬天，他去纽约听了外科学的课，还在那边一家医院待过些日子。这些都无可指摘，也证明他有志气、有干劲；可我就是不喜欢他、信不过他。他心思的运转里有一种幽暗的、藏着掖着的东西，叫我退避。我还知道他残忍。看他对待孩子和牲口的样子就看得出；顶叫他快活的，莫过于拿他那半瓶子刀功，往哪个倒霉的病痛身上练手。他越逼着我喜欢他，我越怕他。
「事情正闹到这个关口，我表妹从纽约来给姨妈问安。她几乎每晚都来我们屋里，自然同他立刻熟了。为了叫我吃醋，他开始对她大献殷勤。诺拉是个漂亮姑娘，蓝眼睛，金头发；心思天真，不大灵光，在城里一家女帽店学徒；*索利大夫*的话她句句当真，自然就爱上了他。她假日过完回去以后，乔治发觉他不但没惹出我的醋意，倒惹恼了我——恼他那样作弄诺拉。我把他好生训了一顿，末了告诉他，我再不与他讲话。
「就在那以后不久，那桩医疗事故——说他医死了人——的丑闻闹起来了。他觉得走为上策；于是把存货贱价出脱，躲到纽约去了。起初我不知道他在哪儿，只觉得甩脱了他，如释重负。我自己也打定了主意要去纽约，进一家时装饰物铺找事。您知道的，伯顿先生——我从前把心摊开给您看过——是什么样一股狂野的、发疯的、却无罪的痴情把我引到那里去。我并不为它羞愧。上帝就是爱。

===Original===

When I stand in his presence, I shall glory in that power of love, which in this bleak world has only fretted and wasted my life. In heaven our whole lives will be one adoration.” She clasped her thin hands together, and turned her dark eyes upward with an expression rapt to sublimity. I gazed upon her with renewed surprise and almost reverence. Never do I expect to meet another woman, the whole conformation of whose mind and heart so fitted her for blind, absolute devotion as Leesy Sullivan’s.
“When I went to the city to see about getting a place, I met my cousin, who told me that she was married to George Thorley, and had been for some weeks; that they were boarding in a nice, quiet place, and that George stayed at home a great deal﻿—indeed, he hardly went out at all.
“It was evident that she had not heard of his reasons for leaving Blankville, and that she did not guess why he kept himself so quiet. Of course I hadn’t the heart to tell her; but I made up my mind that I’d be better to stay where I was, for the present﻿—so I went back to my aunt, without trying to get a situation in New York.
“It was about six months after this I got word from Nora, begging me to come and see her. I loved my cousin, and I’d felt grieved that she was married to Dr. Thorley. I mistrusted something was wrong; so I went to the city, and found her out in the miserable tenement where she was now stopping, starving herself in a room with hardly a bit of furniture. She burst out a-crying when she saw me; and when I stopped her sobbing, she told me she had not seen George for more than three months; that either he had met with an accident, or he’d run away from her, leaving her without a cent of money, and she in such health that she could hardly earn enough to buy a bit of bread and pay the rent of this room.
“ ‘Do you really think he has left you?’ I asked her.
“ ‘Sure, how can I tell?’ she answered, looking at me so pitifully with her innocent blue eyes. ‘He was a fine gentleman, and it’s afraid I am that he’s grown tired of his poor Irish Nora.’
“ ‘I warned you, cousin,’ I said; ‘I knew George Thorley for a villain; but you were taken with his fine words, and wouldn’t heed. I’m sorry, sorry, sorry for you﻿—but that won’t undo what’s done. Are you sure you are his wife, Nora dear?’
“ ‘As sure as I am of heaven,’ she cried, angry with me.

===Chinese===

「将来我站到他面前，我还要夸耀那爱的伟力——在这荒寒的人世，它只把我的生命磨伤、耗尽。到了天上，我们整个的一生，将是一场朝拜。」她把枯瘦的双手扣在一起，乌黑的眼睛望向上天，一副出神到庄严的神情。我重新讶异地、几乎敬畏地望着她。我此生再不指望遇见第二个女人，心智与心肠的构造，像丽西·沙利文这样，天生就为着盲目的、绝对的献身。
「我进城去谋事的那趟，遇见了我表妹；她告诉我，她嫁给了乔治·索利，已经几个礼拜了；他们在一处清静的好地方包饭住；乔治大半时候待在家里——实在说，几乎门也不出。
「看得出，她没听说他离开布兰克维尔的缘由，也猜不到他为什么那样深居简出。我自然不忍告诉她；只是拿定了主意，眼下还是住在原处好——于是我没有在纽约找事，回姨妈那里去了。
「此后约莫半年，诺拉捎信给我，求我去看她。我爱我表妹，她嫁了索利大夫，我心里一直难受。我疑心出了什么事，便进了城，在如今落脚的那座破旧的分租房里找到她：她守着一间几乎没有一件家具的屋子，饿着自己。她一见我就放声大哭；等我止住她的抽噎，她告诉我，她已有三个多月没见到乔治了；他要么遭了意外，要么丢下她跑了，一个铜板也没留；而她身子那样，挣的钱几乎不够买一口面包、付这间屋的房钱。
「『你真当他丢下你了？』我问她。
「『真的，叫我怎么知道呢？』她答道，拿那双天真的蓝眼睛那样可怜巴巴地望着我。『他是个体面老爷，我真相心，他腻了他可怜的爱尔兰诺拉了。』
「『我早提醒过你，妹妹，』我说；『我早认出乔治·索利是个恶棍；可你叫他的漂亮话迷住了，不肯听。我替你难过，难过，难过——可难过也换不回既成的事。你真真是他的妻子吗，亲爱的诺拉？』
「『像信天堂一样地真，』她叫道，对我动了气。

===Original===

‘But it’s married we were by a Protestant clergyman, to please George﻿—and I’ve got my certificate safe﻿—ah, yes, indeed.’
“I could never ascertain whether the ceremony had been performed by a legalized minister; I always suspected my poor cousin had been deceived, and it was because my aunt thought so, too, and was sore on the subject, that she got so angry with you two gentlemen when you went to inquire. But, whether my suspicions were or were not correct, Nora was George’s wife as certainly, in the sight of the angels, as woman was ever the wife of man. Poor child! I no longer hesitated about coming to New York. She needed my protection, and my help, too. I paid her board till the day of her death, which was but a few days after her poor little baby was born; I saw her decently buried, and then I put out the infant to nurse, and I worked to keep that. It was a comfort to me, sir. My own heart was sad, and I took to the little creature almost as if it was my own. I had promised Nora that I would bring it up, and I have kept my word, thus far. I hated its father for the way he’d treated Nora, but I loved the child; I took pleasure in making its pretty garments and in seeing that it was well taken care of. I knew I should never marry; and I adopted Nora’s child as my own.
“Hardly was poor Nora cold in her grave when I was, one evening, surprised by a visit from George Thorley. Where he had been during his absence I did not know. He tried to excuse his conduct toward my cousin, by saying that he had married her in a fit of jealousy, to which I’d driven him by my coldness; that he’d been so tormented in mind he couldn’t stay with her, for he didn’t love her, and he’d gone out West, and been hard at work, to try and forget the past. But he couldn’t forget it; and when he saw his wife’s death in the papers, he had felt awfully; but now he hoped I’d forgive it all, and marry him. He said he had a good business started in Cincinnati, and I should want for nothing, and I *mustn’t* say no to him again. I stood up, I was so indignant, and faced him till he grew as white as a sheet. I called him a *murderer*﻿—yes, Nora’s murderer﻿—and ordered him never to speak to me nor come near me again. I knew he was terribly angry; his eyes burned like fire; but he did not say much that time; as he took up his hat to go, he asked about his baby﻿—if it was living?

===Chinese===

「『不过我们是请了一位新教牧师成的婚，为了依着乔治——我的婚书我稳稳收着呢——啊，是了，真的。』
「那场婚礼到底是不是合法牧师行的，我始终没能查明；我一直疑心我那可怜的表妹受了骗；姨妈也这样想，也在这事上心里作痛，所以两位先生上门探问的时候，她才对你们那样动气。可无论我的疑心对与不对，在众天使眼里，诺拉是乔治的妻子，同天下做妻子的女人一样地真。可怜的孩子！我不再迟疑，决意来纽约。她要人护，也要人帮。她的房饭钱，我一直付到她死——那不过是她那可怜的小婴孩落地后几天的事；我像样地葬了她，随后把孩子送出去雇乳喂养，自己做工供这份开销。这于我是一桩安慰，先生。我自己的心是悲的，我疼这小东西，几乎当亲生的一样。我应承过诺拉，要把孩子拉扯大；到如今，我没有食言。我恨孩子的父亲，恨他那样待诺拉；可我爱这孩子；做她漂亮的衣裳、看着她受好好的照应，是我的乐事。我知道我一辈子不会嫁人；我便把诺拉的孩子当自己的收养了。
「可怜的诺拉坟土未干，一天傍晚，乔治·索利忽然来访，吓了我一跳。他失踪那些日子在哪里，我不知道。他为他待我表妹的行径开脱，说娶她是赌气，是叫我的冷淡逼出来的；说他心里苦得受不住，同她过不下去，因为他并不爱她；说他去了西部卖力做工，想把前事忘掉。可他忘不掉；报上登出他妻子的死讯，他心里难受得很；如今他求我饶恕这一切，嫁给他。说他在辛辛那提起了一升好买卖，我什么也不会缺，这一回我*万不许*再回绝他。我站起身来，气得不行，直直同他对面站着，站得他脸白得像一张纸。我叫他*杀人凶手*——是的，害死诺拉的凶手——喝令他再不许同我说话、不许近我的身。我知道他怒极了；他的眼睛烧得像火；可那一次他没有多说什么；他拿起帽子要走，问了问他的孩子——孩子活着没有？

===Original===

I would not answer him. He had no right to the child, and I did not wish him to see it, or have anything to do with it.
“What became of him, after that, for a long time, I don’t know. He may have been in the city all the time, or he may have been in Cincinnati. At any rate, one day, as I was going from my boardinghouse to the store, I found him walking along by my side. Nora was nigh a year old then. He commenced talking to me on the street, asking me again to marry him; and then, to frighten me, he said what a pretty baby Nora had got to be; and that he should have to find a wife to take care of his child. She was his, and he was going to have her, right away; and if I had any interest in her, I could show it by becoming her stepmother. He said he had plenty of money, and pulled out a handful of gold and showed me. But this only made me think the worse of him. He followed me home, and into my room, against my will, and there I turned upon him and told him that if he ever dared to force himself into my presence again, I would summon the police, and he should be turned over to the Blankville authorities for the crime that had driven him out of the village.
“After he was gone, I sunk into a chair, trembling with weakness, though I had been so bold in his presence. He looked like an evil spirit, when he smiled at me as he shut the door. His smile was more threatening than any scowl would have been. I was frightened for Nora. Every day I expected to hear that the little creature had been taken from her nurse; I trembled night and day; but nothing happened to the child, and from that day to this I have not seen George Thorley. If he is in California, I am glad of it; for that is a good ways off, and perhaps he’ll never get track of his daughter. I’d far rather she’d die and be buried with her mother and myself, than to live to ever know that she had such a father.
“It seems a strange lot has been mine,” concluded the sewing-girl, her dark eyes musing with a faraway look, “to have been followed by such a man as that, to have set my heart so high above me, and then to have fallen, by means of that love, into such a dreadful pit of circumstances﻿—not only to be heartbroken, but so driven and hunted about the world, with my poor little lambkin here.”
The pathetic look and tone with which she said this touched me deeply.

===Chinese===

「我不答他。他没有权利要孩子；我不愿他见她，不愿他同她有任何瓜葛。
「那以后很长一段日子，他去了哪里，我不知道。他也许一直就在城里，也许去了辛辛那提。总之，有一天，我正从寓所往铺子去，忽觉得他走在我身边。那时小诺拉快一岁了。他就在街上同我搭起话来，又来求我嫁他；随后，存心吓唬我，他说小诺拉长成多俊的一个娃娃了；说他得寻个妻子照看他的孩子。孩子是他的，他马上就要领走；我若心疼她，可以拿出心疼来，做她的后娘。他说他有的是钱，掣出一把金子给我看。可这倒叫我愈发看轻他。他一路跟我到住处，不由分说跟进我的屋子；在那里我翻过脸来告诉他：他若再敢闯到我面前，我就叫警察，把他扭送布兰克维尔官府，办那桩把他逐出村子的罪。
「他走了以后，我跌坐进椅子里，虚弱得直抖——方才在他面前，我倒是那样泼辣。他带上门时朝我一笑，那样子像一个恶灵。他那副笑，比任何狰狞都更吓人。我是替诺拉害怕。每一天我都等着听说那小东西被人从乳母那里抱走；我日夜发抖；可孩子那边始终没有出事；从那天到今天，我再没见过乔治·索利。他若真在加利福尼亚，我倒高兴；那里隔着千山万水，或许他一辈子摸不着他女儿的踪迹。我宁可她死了，同她母亲、同我葬在一处，也不愿她活着知道，自己有这样一位父亲。
「我这份命，也真古怪，」缝衣姑娘收了尾，一双黑眼睛出着神，望向远处，「叫这样一个男人死缠着；把心悬得那样高，到头来，又因为那一番爱，跌进这样可怕的一个境遇的深坑——不但心碎，还被撵着、追着，满世界地逃，怀里还搜着我这只可怜的小羊羔。」
她说这话时那副凄恻的神情和声调，深深打动了我。

===Original===

For the first time, I felt fully the exceeding cruelty I had been guilty of toward her, if she were as innocent as her words averred of that nameless and awful crime which I had written down against her. At that moment, I did believe her innocent; I did pity her for her own melancholy sufferings, which had wasted the fountains of her life; and I did respect her for that humble and perfect devotion, giving all and asking nothing, with which she lavished her soul upon him whose memory called upon his friends for sleepless vigilance in behalf of justice. I did not wonder that she shrunk from me as from one ready to wound her. But this was only when in her presence; as soon as I was away I felt doubtful again.
“Have you any likeness of George Thorley?” asked Mr. Burton.
“No. Poor Nora had his ambrotype, but after her death I threw it into the fire.”
“Will you describe him to us?”
Miss Sullivan gave a description corresponding in all particulars with that given by Mr. Burton, after reading the dead-letter; he asked her about the third finger of the right hand, and she said﻿—“Yes, it had been injured by himself, in some of his surgical experiments.”
We now proposed to take leave, the detective again assuring Leesy that he should rather protect her against Thorley than allow him any chance to annoy her; he assured her she should be cared for in his absence, and, what was more, that if little Nora should be left friendless, he would keep an eye on the child and see that it was suitably brought up. This last assurance brightened the face of the consumptive with smiles and tears; but when he gave her his hand at parting, she burst into sobs.
“It is our last meeting, sir.”
“Try to keep as well as you are now until I come back,” he said, cheerfully. “I may want you very much then. And, by the way, Leesy﻿—one question more. You once told me that you did not recognize the person you saw upon the lawn, at Mr. Argyll’s, that night﻿—have you a suspicion who it might be?”
“None. I believe the man was a stranger to me. I only saw him by a flash of lightning at the instant he was descending from the tree; if he had been an acquaintance I do not know that I should have known him.”
“That is all. Goodbye, little Nora. Don’t forget Burton.”
We heard the girl’s sobs after the door was shut.
“I’m her only friend,” said my companion, as he walked away.

===Chinese===

头一回，我全然感到了自己待她的残忍，何其过甚——倘若她当真清白，不曾犯下我记在她名下的那桩无名的可怕罪行。那一刻，我真的信她清白；我真的怜她——为她自己那些憔悴了生命之泉的惨痛；我也真的敬她——敬她那卑微而完全的忠贞，倾其所有，一无所求，把整个灵魂泼在他身上——正是对他的记忆，才呼召他的朋友们为正义彻夜不眠。她见我便退避，像退避一个存心伤她的人，我毫不奇怪。可这只在我同她同处一室的时候；一离开，我的疑心又回来了。
「你有乔治·索利的画像吗？」伯顿先生问。
「没有。可怜的诺拉存过他的玛瑙版照片，她死后，我把它扔进了火里。」
「那把他给我们描述一遍吧。」
沙利文小姐描述的模样，同伯顿先生读完死信后讲的那番，桩桩件件都对得上；他问到他右手第三根手指，她说——「是的，那是他自己弄伤的，拿他自己的身子练他那套刀功来着。」
我们于是起身告辞；侦探又一次向丽西担保：他要护她对付索利，不容他有半点搅扰她的机会；他担保他不在时她会得到照应；而且，倘若小诺拉孤零零没了亲人，他会看顾这孩子，叫她得到合式的抚养。最后这一句，说得那痨病人的脸上又是笑、又是泪；可临别他把手伸给她时，她放声呜咽起来。
「这是我们最后一面了，先生。」
「保重，就这样撑到我回来，」他高高兴兴地说。「到那时，我说不定很用得着你。对了，丽西——还有一问。你从前说，那天夜里在阿盖尔先生家草坪上看见的那个人，你没认出来——你可疑心过他是谁？」
「没有。我想那人于我是生人。我只在一道闪电里见了他一眼，就在他从树上下来那一瞬；他若是个相熟的人，我也不知道认不认得出。」
「就这些了。再见，小诺拉。别忘了伯顿。」
门关上以后，我们还听见那姑娘的呜咽。
「我是她唯一的朋友，」我的同伴边走边说。

===Original===

“No wonder she is moved at letting me go. I think, with her, that it is doubtful if she lasts until we get back. Still, her disease is a lingering one﻿—I hope I shall see her live to witness the sad triumph of our industry.”
“You speak as if the triumph were already secured.”
“If he’s on the face of the earth, we’ll find Doctor George Thorley. It is no longer possible that we should be on the wrong track. You know, Richard, that I have not confided all my secrets to you. There will be no one more astonished than yourself when I summon my witnesses and sum up my conclusions. Oh, that the hour were come! But I forget my motto﻿—‘learn to labor and to wait.’ ”

===Chinese===

「难怪她放我走要这样伤心。同她一样，我也疑心她撑不到我们回来。不过，她那病是拖延的病——我盼着还能见她活着，亲眼看见我们这一番辛苦那悲哀的胜利。」
「您说话的口气，倒像胜利已经到手了。」
「只要他还在这地球的面上，我们就找得到乔治·索利大夫。我们再不可能走岔了路。你知道，理查德，我并没有把秘密全都托付给你。等我传齐证人、总结论断的那一天，最吃惊的莫过于你自己。啊，但愿那个时辰就到来！可我忘了我的座右铭——『学会劳作，学会等待。』」

## IV Embarked for California / 四 启程赴加州
===Original===

We were on our way to California by the next steamer. By the advice of Mr. Burton I purchased my ticket under an assumed name, for he did not wish to excite the curiosity of the Argylls, who might happen to see the passage-list, and who would be sure to suspect something from the contiguity of our names. To his friends, who chanced to know of his sudden intentions, Mr. Burton represented that the health of his daughter demanded a change of climate, and business matters had led him to prefer California.
It was fortunate, since the expenses of such a trip had become so unexpected a necessity, that I had lived in the plain, retiring manner which I had done in Washington. I had wasted no money on white kids, bouquets, nor champagne-suppers; I had paid my board and washing-bills, and a very moderate bill to my tailor; the rest of my salary had been placed in a New York bank to my account. My scorched soul and withered tastes had demanded no luxurious gratification﻿—not even the purchase of new books; so that now, when this sudden demand arose, I had a fund sufficient for the purpose. Mr. Burton bore his own expenses, which, indeed, I could not help, for I had not the means of urging a different course upon him.
We had a very definite object, but no definite plans; these were to be formed according to the circumstances we had to encounter after our arrival in El Dorado. Of course our man was living under an assumed name, and had traveled under an assumed one; we might have every difficulty in getting upon his track. At the time the detective had discovered the return of the five-hundred dollar bill from San Francisco, he had, with great perseverance, gained access to, and “made a note of” the passengers’ lists of all the steamers which sailed at or about the time of the murder, for California. These he had preserved. Out of the names, he had chosen those which his curious sagacity suggested were the most likely to prove fictitious, and, if no quicker method presented itself, he intended to trace out one and all of those passengers, until he came upon *the man*. In all this I was his assistant, willing to carry out his directions, but trusting the whole affair to his more experienced hand.
During the long, monotonous days of our voyage, I seemed to have

> “Suffered a sea-change”

===Chinese===

下一班轮船，我们便上了去加利福尼亚的路。依伯顿先生的劝告，我用了个化名买船票；因为他不愿惹起阿盖尔一家的好奇——他们万一看见旅客名单，我们两人名字挨在一处，准会疑心出了什么事。对那些碰巧得知他突然动身的朋友们，伯顿先生则说：女儿的身体需要换一换气候，而生意上的事，叫他选了加利福尼亚。
幸亏我如今不得不预备这样一笔意外的旅费——亏得我在华盛顿过日子简朴、收敛。白羔羊皮手套、花束、香槟夜宴，我一样不曾破费；膳宿、浆洗，我都付清了，裁缝那里也只有一张很有限的账单；薪水的其余部分，都存进了纽约一家银行我的名下。我那颗烧焦的灵魂、那些枯萎的趣味，不曾要求过任何奢侈的餍足——连新书都不曾买；所以眼下这突然的需求一起，我手里的钱尽够用。伯顿先生自付他的开销；这也由不得我，因为我没有财力劝他另行一套。
我们有一个极明确的目标，却没有明确的计划；这些要看我们到了黄金国（El Dorado）以后遇到的情形再定。当然，我们那个人用的是化名，一路行来用的也是化名；要摸上他的踪迹，说不定千难万难。早在侦探发现那张五百美元钞票从旧金山回流的时候，他就以非凡的坚忍，设法调阅并「一一记下」了凶案前后那几天驶往加利福尼亚的各班轮船的旅客名单。这些他都保存着。他从那些名字里，凭他那奇特的慧眼挑出最可能是捏造的；倘若没有更快的门路，他就打算把这些旅客一个不落地访查过去，直到撞上*那个人*。这一切里，我是他的帮手，甘愿照他的吩咐行事，把全局托给他那更老练的手。
航程漫长而单调，那些日子里，我仿佛
「历了一场海的幻变」

===Original===

into something quite different from the wooden sort of being into which I had gradually been hardening. With the dull routine of my office-life were broken up also many of the cynical ways of thinking into which I had fallen. I felt as if the springs of youth were not quite dried up. The real secret of this improvement was in the eager hope I entertained that the real criminals were soon to be brought to light, and the Argylls made to realize the cruel wrong they had done me. Already, in imagination, I had accepted their regret and forgiven them their injustice. It seemed as if every breath of the sea-breeze, and every bound of the sparkling waves, swept away a portion of the bitterness which had mingled with my nature. The old poetry of existence began to warm my chilled pulses and to flush the morning and evening sky. For hours most melancholy, yet most delicious, I would climb to some lonely post of observation﻿—for I was a perfect sailor among the ropes﻿—and there, where the blue of heaven bent down to meet the blue of the ocean, making an azure round in which floated only the ethereal clouds, all the sweetness of the past would come floating to me in fragments, like the odor of flowers blown from some beloved and distant shore.
The most vivid picture in my sea-dreams, was that of the parlor of the old Argyll mansion, as I had seen it last, on the night of my excursion to the oak-tree. Mary, in the rosy bloom of young womanhood, the ideal of beauty to the eye of a young and appreciative man, whose standard of female perfection was high, while his sensitiveness to its charm was intense﻿—Mary, reading her book beneath the rich light of the chandelier﻿—I loved to recall the vision, except always that it was marred by that shadow of James coming too soon between me and the light. But that flitting vision of Eleanor was as if a saint had looked down at me out of its shrine. I saw, then, that she was no longer of this world, as far as her hopes were concerned. My once strong passion had been slowly changing into reverence; I had grieved with her with a grief utterly self-abnegating, and when I saw that her despair had worked itself up to a patient and aspiring resignation, I now felt less of pity and more of affectionate reverence.
